# The Congress of Vienna and the Concert of Europe - Live Learning Session

> **What this session teaches:** It begins with a question that sounds procedural and is not - why did four victorious powers, having just destroyed the most successful conqueror in modern European history, decide that the hardest part of their work was only beginning? - and it ends with an examiner asking whether the order those powers built was a peace system, a police system, or a single thing that cannot be separated into the two. Between those points it teaches the chain of instruments that actually made the settlement, from Chaumont in March 1814 to the second Paris treaty of November 1815, because the settlement is a sequence and not a single document; who was in the room, what each of the five principal negotiators wanted, and how a congress that never held a plenary session reached decisions at all; the three principles the settlement announced - legitimacy, balance of power, compensation - and the fourth it operated without announcing, the containment of France; the territorial settlement region by region, with the reason for each transfer rather than a list of transfers; the Poland-Saxony crisis that took the congress to the edge of war among the victors and the secret treaty of 3 January 1815 that ended it; the treatment of France before and after the Hundred Days, and why the restraint of 1814 and the severity of 1815 are both parts of one calculation; the three alliances that are constantly confused with one another and with the Concert itself; the congress system in operation at Aix-la-Chapelle, Troppau, Laibach and Verona; the quarrel over intervention that split the system down its middle, with the Troppau Protocol on one side and the British State Paper of 5 May 1820 on the other; the interventions themselves in Naples, Piedmont and Spain; the two questions - Spanish America and Greece - on which the system acted against its own stated principle; why Britain was inside the Concert and never inside its ideology; the repressive order inside the German states, from the Carlsbad Decrees to the police machinery of the Age of Metternich; the three tests of 1820, 1830 and 1848 and what each proved; the peace claim, stated precisely enough to be defended and qualified honestly enough to survive a hostile examiner; the historians' quarrel between the reactionary reading and the equilibrium reading, with the counterfactual that decides how much weight the settlement can bear; the long legacy, including the exact and limited sense in which the Concert prefigures later collective security; and finally the conversion of all of it into marks. Napoleon's rise and fall belong to the topic before this one; Italian and German unification belong to the topic after it; the partition of Africa belongs to the imperialism topic. Here you learn what the victors built in 1815, how it worked, where it bent, and when it broke.

## How to read this session

Work through the lessons in order. The order is not chronological throughout, and that is deliberate: Lesson 3 comes before Lesson 4 because a map redrawn without its governing principles is a list to be memorised rather than an argument to be used, and Lesson 9 comes before Lesson 10 because the interventions in Naples and Spain are unintelligible until you know what the powers had just quarrelled about at Troppau. Inside a lesson, take the visual first, then the plain explanation, then the exact evidence, then the objection and the reply, and only then the examination use.

Two labels run through every lesson, and keeping them apart is the single habit that separates a confident answer from an over-confident one.

| Label | Meaning | Use in an answer |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Fact** | Directly supported by verified evidence - the standard account of this topic and its analytical companion, the subject's master chronology and revision chart, the folder's syllabus mapping, the verified question ledgers, or the single bounded factual pass run for this session to fix standard instruments, dates and names | Assert it. Treaty names, dates, offices and signatory lists can be stated flatly |
| ⚠️ **Analytical inference** | Interpretation, weighting, comparison, counterfactual or synthesis built on those facts | Argue it, signal it, and expect a counter-position |
| 📰 **Current linkage** | A dated contemporary item, carried exactly once in this session | Use as illustration only; it supplies no historical evidence and no teaching claim rests on it |

Three further disciplines are specific to this topic, and each exists because this is the part of the syllabus where a confident answer most easily becomes a wrong one.

**The names-and-dates rule.** This topic is made of instruments. A candidate who writes "the Congress of Vienna decided" where the correct subject is "the second Treaty of Paris provided", or who dates the Holy Alliance and the Quadruple Alliance to the same act because both fall in 1815, has not made a stylistic slip; they have merged two different things with two different memberships and two different purposes. This session therefore names the instrument, gives its date, gives its signatories, and says what it did - every time. Where the standard account did not itself carry an instrument name or date, it was fixed in one bounded factual pass before the teaching was written, and nothing is asserted that was not fixed that way. Where a dating convention is genuinely contested - the congress's own opening date is the clearest case - both conventions are given and the contest is stated rather than hidden.

**The non-equivalence rule.** The Concert of Europe is routinely described as an ancestor of the League of Nations and the United Nations. There is a real and defensible version of that claim and a lazy version, and the difference is worth marks. This session states the genuine continuities exactly, then states the disqualifying differences exactly, and refuses the equation. A Concert without a charter, a membership rule, a secretariat, a defined obligation, a voting procedure or a single non-European member is not an early United Nations; it is a habit of consultation among five monarchies whose declared purpose included the suppression of constitutional government. Both halves of that sentence are required.

**The boundary rule.** Three neighbouring topics own material that this one constantly touches. Napoleon's rise, his empire, his defeat and the internal history of revolutionary France belong to the previous topic; this session takes 1814 as its opening condition and does not re-narrate what produced it. The political narrative of Italian and German unification belongs to the topic that follows; this session shows what Vienna built in Italy and Germany and hands over at the point where the undoing of that arrangement begins. The drivers and partition of New Imperialism belong to the imperialism topic. This session names each boundary where it arises, supplies what this topic genuinely owns, and hands over rather than annexing.

*Read the diagram downwards as a chain and sideways where two things had to be true at once. The single most examinable feature of it is the split in the middle: the same system that used intervention in Italy and Spain refused it in Spanish America and reversed it in Greece. Most weak answers on this topic describe only the left branch and then cannot explain why the system dissolved.*

**Scope of this session.** Napoleon's career, the Napoleonic wars and the internal history of revolutionary France belong to the topic that precedes this one; this session opens with the victors already in possession of the problem and does not re-derive how they got there. Italian and German unification belong to the topic that follows; this session teaches what the settlement built in Italy and Germany, and what pressure that arrangement generated, and stops at the point where the unification narrative begins. The drivers and partition of New Imperialism belong to their own topic. The doctrines of liberalism, conservatism and nationalism as *doctrines* belong to the overview topic that owns the political-philosophies clause; this session uses them as forces acting on a settlement, names the boundary where it arises, and does not re-define them.

## Lesson 1 - Why a congress at all, and the chain of instruments that made the settlement

### Visual: the settlement as a chain of six instruments

*The diagram is the answer to a question many candidates never ask: which document actually did what? Chaumont made the alliance permanent. The first Paris treaty disposed of France and called the congress. The Final Act consolidated the map. The second Paris treaty punished the relapse. Writing "the Congress of Vienna imposed an indemnity on France" gets the history wrong and the instrument wrong in the same clause.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

In the spring of 1814 the coalition had done the hard thing. Napoleon had been driven out of Germany, out of Spain, and finally out of France; he abdicated and was sent to Elba. On any ordinary reckoning the war was over and the victors could go home.

They did not, and the reason is the whole of this topic. Twice within twenty-two years a French government had overturned the arrangements of Europe - once as a revolutionary republic exporting a doctrine, once as an empire annexing territory. Each time, a coalition had eventually assembled and eventually won, and each time the peace that followed had lasted only until France recovered. The victors of 1814 had therefore learned something about their own success: winning a war against a great power in the middle of Europe was possible, but it was ruinously expensive, it took years, and it had to be repeated.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the question they set themselves was consequently not "how do we punish France?" but "how do we build an arrangement that does not have to be defended by another twenty-year war?" That is a different and much harder question, and it explains almost every feature of the settlement that looks odd at first sight - the moderation towards France in 1814, the readmission of France in 1818, the obsession with buffers and barriers, the willingness to give territory to states that had done little fighting, and the decision to keep meeting after the war had ended.

### 2. Chaumont, 1 March 1814: turning a coalition into a system

✅ **Fact:** By the **Treaty of Chaumont**, dated **1 March 1814**, **Britain, Austria, Prussia and Russia** bound themselves into an alliance of **twenty years**, each undertaking to keep **150,000 men** in the field against France and to make no separate peace.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three features of that instrument decide the shape of everything that follows. First, the twenty-year term. A wartime coalition dissolves when the war ends; a twenty-year alliance is a statement that the victors intend to remain jointly responsible for the outcome long after the fighting stops. Second, the equality of the military obligation. Each of the four pledged the same force, which makes the alliance a partnership of four rather than a British-financed coalition with three continental clients. Third, the prohibition on a separate peace. That clause exists because every previous coalition against France had broken exactly there - one member accepted favourable terms and the rest were left exposed. Chaumont is the moment at which the coalition stops behaving like an alliance of convenience.

✅ **Fact:** Chaumont also laid down preliminary arrangements for the post-war order which were later formalised at Vienna.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** The Vienna settlement begins at Chaumont, not at Vienna. What Vienna drew on the map, Chaumont had already decided in principle: that the four victors would act together, indefinitely, as the managers of the result.

### 3. The first Peace of Paris, 30 May 1814: the generous treaty

✅ **Fact:** The **first Treaty of Paris**, signed **30 May 1814** between France and the coalition, ended hostilities after Napoleon's abdication. It returned France substantially to her **frontiers of 1792**, confirmed the restoration of the **Bourbon monarchy under Louis XVIII**, and imposed **no indemnity and no occupation**. Its **Article 32** provided that a general congress of all the belligerents should meet at **Vienna** to complete the settlement.

This is the treaty that surprises students, and it should. France had been at war with most of Europe for over two decades, had occupied or dominated the greater part of the continent, and had just been defeated at enormous cost. She was left with more territory than she had held in 1789, kept the art collections taken during the wars, paid nothing, and was not occupied.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the moderation is not sentimentality and it is not weakness. It is a calculation with two parts. The first part concerns the restored monarchy: a Bourbon king imposed on France by foreign armies and then saddled with a humiliating peace would have no domestic support at all, and the victors had a direct interest in the survival of the government they had installed. The second part concerns the shape of Europe: a France stripped, occupied and permanently resentful would become the standing grievance around which the next coalition war would form. Moderation towards the defeated power was, on this reasoning, a *balance-of-power measure* rather than an act of mercy - it is the clearest single illustration in the topic of the difference between punishing an enemy and settling a system.

### 4. The congress itself, and a dating problem worth stating

✅ **Fact:** Delegates began assembling at Vienna from **late September 1814**, and the congress ran until **June 1815**.

⚠️ **Dating control:** the "opening" of the congress is given differently in different standard accounts, and the disagreement is real rather than careless. Some date it from the arrival of the delegations in late September 1814; others give a formal opening in **November 1814**, after weeks of ceremonial and informal negotiation had already taken place. Both conventions are defensible because the congress never had a single opening plenary to date from - which is itself the point taught in Lesson 2. **Write "1814-15" for the congress and reserve exact days for the instruments**, which have unambiguous dates. An answer that asserts a single day for the opening has claimed more precision than the event permits.

### 5. The two June instruments, and why they are two

✅ **Fact:** The **German Federal Act** (*Bundesakte*) of **8 June 1815** created the **German Confederation of 39 states**, with a **Federal Diet at Frankfurt** under the **permanent presidency of Austria**.

✅ **Fact:** The **Final Act of the Congress of Vienna**, also called the **General Treaty**, was signed on **9 June 1815**. It consolidated the separate treaties, conventions and agreements negotiated during the congress into one instrument, supplying a new political map of Europe together with new norms for the conduct of relations between states.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the one-day gap is not an accident of scheduling; it reflects what the two documents are. The German arrangement had to be settled *before* the general instrument could consolidate it, because the reorganisation of Germany was the largest single piece of the map and the most contested. The Final Act is a consolidating instrument - it did not decide things so much as gather decisions - and that is why it can be signed nine days before Waterloo without anybody thinking the settlement premature. The congress had finished its work; it had not yet found out whether the work would hold.

### 6. The Hundred Days: the settlement tested before it was implemented

✅ **Fact:** Napoleon left Elba and landed in France on **1 March 1815**. The powers assembled at Vienna responded on **13 March 1815** with a declaration placing him outside the protection of the law - an outlaw. He was defeated at **Waterloo on 18 June 1815**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Hundred Days matter to this topic for a reason that has nothing to do with the campaign. They arrived in the middle of the congress, at the precise moment when the Poland-Saxony quarrel of Lesson 5 had divided the victors into two armed camps. The effect was immediate and, for the settlement, decisive: an external threat that all four powers recognised instantly re-cohered an alliance that had been within measurable distance of breaking up. ✅ The outlawry declaration of 13 March is the visible proof - it is a *joint* act taken by powers that had, weeks earlier, been secretly preparing against one another.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** there is a general proposition here worth carrying into the later lessons and into an answer. The Vienna order held together most reliably when there was a shared external danger and fractured most reliably when the only questions on the table were internal ones. That single observation explains the cohesion of 1815, the drift of 1818-22, and the collapse of consensus once the surviving questions were about revolutions inside other people's states.

### 7. The second Peace of Paris, 20 November 1815: the harder treaty

✅ **Fact:** The **second Treaty of Paris**, signed **20 November 1815** after Waterloo and Napoleon's second abdication, reduced France to her frontiers of **1790**, imposed an indemnity of **700 million francs**, and provided for an **allied army of occupation for up to five years** at French expense. The occupation in fact ended earlier, in **1818**, when France met her obligations ahead of schedule.

| | First Peace of Paris | Second Peace of Paris |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Date | 30 May 1814 | 20 November 1815 |
| ✅ Frontiers | Those of 1792 | Those of 1790 |
| ✅ Indemnity | None | 700 million francs |
| ✅ Occupation | None | Allied army, up to five years |
| ✅ Ended by | — | Aix-la-Chapelle, 1818, ahead of schedule |
| ⚠️ Logic | Secure the restored monarchy; avoid creating a grievance | Price the relapse; demonstrate that a second attempt has a cost |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the comparison is one of the highest-yield things in this topic and it should be used as an argument rather than a table. The victors did not become vindictive between 1814 and 1815; they received new information. In 1814 they had gambled that a restored Bourbon monarchy would hold France; in March 1815 an emperor landed with a handful of men and the country came over to him within three weeks. The second treaty is the revised premium on a risk that had just been repriced by events - and the fact that the occupation was lifted three years early, and France readmitted to the councils of Europe in the same year, shows that even the revised terms were designed to end rather than to last.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** Between 1814 and 1815 the victors moved from moderation to severity without abandoning their strategy, because the strategy was never generosity - it was the rapid reconstruction of France as a stable member of the states system rather than a permanent enemy of it.

### 8. Comparison: 1815 against 1919, stated carefully

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the comparison with the peace settlement after the First World War is the most frequently attempted and most frequently botched move on this topic, so it is worth fixing its limits now and using it deliberately in Lessons 15 and 18. What the comparison legitimately shows is a difference of *method*: Vienna negotiated with the defeated power present and represented, and readmitted it to the great-power directorate within three years of the final defeat; the later settlement was negotiated among the victors and presented to the defeated. What the comparison does **not** establish is that Vienna was simply the better settlement. Vienna bought its stability by declining to recognise national and constitutional claims that the later settlement at least attempted to address, and it is not obvious that a settlement which suppresses a problem for thirty years is superior to one that fails to solve it in twenty. ❌ Do not write "Vienna succeeded where Versailles failed" as a bare proposition; ✅ write that the two settlements applied different criteria of legitimacy - dynastic right in one case, national self-determination in the other - and that each achieved what its criterion made possible and failed at what its criterion excluded.

### 9. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** If the settlement was as carefully engineered as this lesson claims, why did the powers need six separate instruments across twenty months? Does the sequence not suggest improvisation rather than design?

**Reply.** The sequence is not evidence of improvisation; it is evidence that the tasks were genuinely distinct and had to be done in order. An alliance among the victors (Chaumont) had to precede the disposal of the defeated power (first Paris), which had to precede the reorganisation of the territory in dispute (the congress, the Federal Act, the Final Act), which had in turn to be revised once the defeated power relapsed (second Paris). No single document could have done all four, and the attempt to combine them would have required the powers to settle the Polish question before they had settled the French one - which, as Lesson 5 shows, would probably have destroyed the coalition.

⚠️ **Residual.** What cannot be settled from this evidence is how much of the design was foreseen at Chaumont and how much was reconstructed afterwards as a rationale. The instruments show what was done and in what order; they do not show the negotiators' expectations at each stage. Treat the coherence of the chain as a real property of the outcome and a claim about intention only where an instrument states the intention itself - as Article 32 of the first Paris treaty does when it summons the congress.

### UPSC application

The syllabus clause that owns this topic names the **redrawal of national boundaries** among the events from the eighteenth century onward that world history must cover, and this settlement is the first great modern instance of it. The examination value of this lesson is precision. Almost every weak answer on this topic begins "The Congress of Vienna was held in 1815 to redraw the map of Europe after Napoleon's defeat" - a sentence in which the date is imprecise, the purpose is thin and the agency is wrong. The strong opening states the problem the victors faced and the instrument chain they used to solve it, in three sentences, and then proceeds to the principles.

**Probable framing.** Any question on post-Napoleonic reconstruction, on the redrawal of boundaries, on the treatment of a defeated great power, or on the comparison of peace settlements will require this lesson's chain. It is also the supply line for any answer that contrasts 1815 with 1919.

**Common traps.** Attributing the indemnity or the occupation to the congress rather than to the second Paris treaty. Dating the Final Act to 1814. Assuming the congress ended when Napoleon returned - it did not; it completed its work during the Hundred Days. Writing that France was excluded from the settlement, when France was present throughout and readmitted to the directorate in 1818.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Chaumont, 1 March 1814** - Britain, Austria, Prussia, Russia; 20-year alliance; 150,000 men each; no separate peace.
2. ✅ **First Peace of Paris, 30 May 1814** - France to 1792 frontiers; Louis XVIII restored; **no** indemnity, **no** occupation; Article 32 summons the congress.
3. ✅ Delegates assemble at Vienna from **late September 1814**; some accounts date a formal opening to **November 1814**; the congress runs to **June 1815**.
4. ✅ **German Federal Act, 8 June 1815** - Confederation of **39** states; Diet at **Frankfurt**; **Austrian** presidency.
5. ✅ **Final Act (General Treaty), 9 June 1815** - consolidates all the separate agreements; signed nine days before Waterloo.
6. ✅ **Hundred Days** - Napoleon lands **1 March 1815**; the Congress outlaws him **13 March 1815**; **Waterloo, 18 June 1815**.
7. ✅ **Second Peace of Paris, 20 November 1815** - 1790 frontiers; **700 million francs**; occupation up to **five years**, ended **1818**.
8. ⚠️ The moderation of 1814 was a balance-of-power measure, not mercy; the severity of 1815 was a repricing after the relapse, not vindictiveness.
9. ⚠️ The order held when an external danger was shared and fractured when only internal questions remained - the proposition to carry into Lessons 8 to 14.
10. ⚠️ Against 1919, the defensible contrast is one of **method** - the defeated power present and readmitted - not a verdict that Vienna was simply better.
11. ❌ Never write that "the Congress of Vienna imposed an indemnity on France"; the instrument was the second Treaty of Paris.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "At the Congress of Vienna in 1815 the victorious powers punished France by taking her conquests, imposing a heavy indemnity and occupying her territory." Identify every error in that sentence and rewrite it correctly in not more than three sentences.

**Model answer:** There are four errors. The congress is dated to a single year when it ran from 1814 to 1815. The indemnity and the occupation are attributed to the congress, whereas both were imposed by the second Treaty of Paris of 20 November 1815, a separate instrument concluded after the congress had finished its work. The verb "punished" misdescribes the first settlement, since the first Treaty of Paris of 30 May 1814 imposed neither indemnity nor occupation and left France her 1792 frontiers. And the single act described conceals a sequence in which the terms changed because the circumstances did. A correct version reads: the first Treaty of Paris of 30 May 1814 restored the Bourbon monarchy and left France her 1792 frontiers without indemnity or occupation, and summoned a general congress at Vienna; that congress met from late September 1814 and consolidated the territorial settlement in the Final Act of 9 June 1815; after Napoleon's return and defeat at Waterloo, the second Treaty of Paris of 20 November 1815 reduced France to her 1790 frontiers, imposed an indemnity of 700 million francs and provided for an allied occupation of up to five years, which ended in 1818.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "the Congress of Vienna" as the name of the whole settlement. It is the name of one stage in a chain of six instruments, and the terms that candidates most often attribute to it - the indemnity, the occupation, the alliance obligations and the periodic meetings - were in fact imposed by Chaumont, by the two Paris treaties and by the Quadruple Alliance. Naming the correct instrument is not pedantry; it is what allows an answer to explain why the terms changed between 1814 and 1815.

**Bridge to Lesson 2.** The chain of instruments tells you what was decided. It does not tell you who decided it, or how - and the "how" is genuinely strange, because the congress that gave its name to the settlement never held a plenary session of the assembled powers to vote on anything. That is the next problem.

## Lesson 2 - Who decided, and how a congress without plenaries decided anything

Progress: 2/18 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the five principal negotiators and what each wanted; the committee method; Talleyrand's re-entry of France; the secondary achievements - diplomatic precedence, river navigation, the slave-trade declaration

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

CA search: not sought - the single current linkage for this topic is held in Lesson 17

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: five men, five problems, one table

```text
                        THE FIVE WHO MATTERED

  METTERNICH (Austria)              CASTLEREAGH (Britain)
  Foreign minister from 1809;       Foreign Secretary 1812-1822
  Chancellor from 1821              WANTS: a continental balance
  WANTS: Austrian primacy in        that removes the need for
  Germany and Italy; a barrier      Britain to fight in Europe;
  against Russia; monarchical       maritime and colonial gains
  authority everywhere              already banked
  FEARS: revolution, and a          FEARS: any single power
  Russia that stands on the         dominating the continent -
  Vistula                           French, then Russian
             \                              /
              \                            /
               \                          /
     ALEXANDER I (Russia)  ----  HARDENBERG (Prussia)
     Emperor, negotiating in       Chancellor
     person                        WANTS: compensation for
     WANTS: a Polish kingdom       the losses of 1806; ideally
     under his own crown;          the whole of Saxony
     moral leadership of a         DEPENDS ON: Russian support,
     Christian Europe              which makes Prussia the
     UNPREDICTABLE: mystical       junior partner in the
     and autocratic by turns       Polish-Saxon bargain
                        \        /
                         \      /
                    TALLEYRAND (France)
                    Foreign minister of the restored Bourbons
                    STARTS WITH: nothing - the defeated power,
                    admitted on sufferance
                    ENDS WITH: a seat at the centre, gained by
                    supplying the one principle the others
                    needed and could not supply themselves:
                    LEGITIMACY
```

*Read the diagram as a set of pressures, not personalities. Austria and Britain converge because both fear a Russia advanced into central Europe. Russia and Prussia converge because each can pay for what it wants with the other's gain. France has no weight at all until she offers a principle that splits the four - which is exactly what Lesson 5 shows her doing.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

A modern reader assumes that an international conference works like a legislature: everyone assembles, proposals are tabled, votes are taken, a majority decides. The Congress of Vienna worked in almost the opposite way, and understanding why is the difference between describing the congress and explaining it.

Representatives of almost every European state came to Vienna, together with principalities, free cities, ecclesiastical bodies and dispossessed rulers, because Article 32 of the first Paris treaty had summoned a general congress of the belligerents and because everybody with a claim wanted to press it in person. If that assembly had actually sat as a deliberative body, nothing could have been decided at all: the small German states alone would have deadlocked any question about Germany, and the dispossessed rulers of Napoleon's satellite kingdoms would have had an equal voice with the powers whose armies had just won the war.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the congress therefore solved the problem by never convening as a whole. Business ran through committees and through direct negotiation among the powers that had the armies, with the great mass of delegates present, lobbied, entertained and consulted, but not deciding. This is not an incidental administrative fact. It is the first appearance of the organising principle of the entire century that follows: **the great powers decide, and the rest of Europe is informed**. Everything in Lessons 7 to 12 about the Concert is an extension of this procedural habit into peacetime.

### 2. The five, one by one

✅ **Fact:** The principal negotiators were **Klemens von Metternich** for Austria, **Viscount Castlereagh** for Britain, **Tsar Alexander I** for Russia, negotiating in person, **Karl August von Hardenberg** for Prussia, and **Charles Maurice de Talleyrand** for France. **The Duke of Wellington** represented Britain during Castlereagh's absence.

✅ **Fact:** Metternich was Austria's foreign minister from **1809** and became chancellor in **1821**. Castlereagh was British Foreign Secretary from **1812 to 1822**.

| Negotiator | ✅ Position | ⚠️ Core objective | ⚠️ The constraint that shaped it |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Metternich** (Austria) | Foreign minister from 1809; chancellor from 1821 | Austrian primacy in Germany and Italy; monarchical authority as a general principle; a barrier against Russian advance | Austria was a multinational dynastic state; any concession to the national principle anywhere threatened its own existence, so conservatism was structural rather than merely temperamental |
| **Castlereagh** (Britain) | Foreign Secretary 1812-22 | A continental equilibrium stable enough that Britain need not keep intervening; naval and colonial security | Britain's parliamentary system made permanent commitments to police other countries' internal politics impossible to defend at home - the constraint that produces Lesson 9 |
| **Alexander I** (Russia) | Emperor, negotiating personally | A Polish kingdom under his own crown; moral and religious leadership of the settlement | Personal rule meant his objectives could shift with his convictions; the other powers had to negotiate with a man whose instructions were his own |
| **Hardenberg** (Prussia) | Chancellor | Territorial compensation for the catastrophic losses of 1806; the whole of Saxony if obtainable | Prussia was the weakest of the four and could get Saxony only with Russian backing, which tied her to the Polish demand she might not otherwise have supported |
| **Talleyrand** (France) | Foreign minister of the restored monarchy | Re-entry of France into the directorate of Europe | France had no bargaining power from strength; the only available lever was a *principle* that could divide the victors - and legitimacy was that principle |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the table is worth more than the sum of its rows because it shows that the settlement was not produced by a shared conservative ideology. Only Metternich and, in a different register, Alexander held anything like a doctrine. Castlereagh wanted an equilibrium and was indifferent to the internal politics of the states composing it; Hardenberg wanted acres; Talleyrand wanted a chair. The order they produced is therefore a bargain among incompatible objectives, which is precisely why it contained, from the first day, the fracture line that opened at Troppau in 1820.

### 3. Talleyrand and the uses of a principle

✅ **Fact:** Talleyrand championed the principle of **legitimacy** - the rights of lawful monarchs, including the King of Saxony, whose throne Prussia proposed to annex - and used it to break France's diplomatic isolation and become a party to the central bargaining.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most instructive single manoeuvre in the topic, and it deserves to be understood as a piece of reasoning rather than as cleverness. France in 1814 had no armies to offer, no territory to trade and no allies. What she had was a position that the victors could not occupy themselves without embarrassment. Prussia wanted to annex a kingdom whose king was still alive and had committed no crime other than choosing the wrong side; Russia wanted a Polish crown for a Russian emperor. Both demands were naked appropriations. By insisting that lawful sovereigns could not simply be extinguished by the victors, Talleyrand made an argument that Austria and Britain already wished to make on grounds of the balance of power, and gave it a form that sounded like principle rather than interest.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the lesson for an answer is that **legitimacy at Vienna was simultaneously a genuine principle and an instrument**. It was genuine in that the negotiators believed dynastic right was the only available basis for a stable order after the collapse of the revolutionary alternative. It was an instrument in that it was invoked selectively - Saxony's king was protected by it, the Venetian Republic and the ecclesiastical states of Germany were not restored by it, and Poland received no restoration at all. A candidate who writes that Vienna applied legitimacy consistently has not read the map; a candidate who writes that legitimacy was mere hypocrisy cannot explain why Saxony survived.

### 4. How business was actually done

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the working method had four features that repay attention.

1. **No plenary decision-making.** The assembled congress never sat as a deciding body. Questions were prepared and settled in smaller groupings of the powers concerned and then incorporated into the record.
2. **Concentric circles.** The innermost circle was the four victorious powers; the next was those four plus France, once Talleyrand had forced his way in; outside that sat the signatories of the Paris treaty and the many claimants.
3. **Specialist committees.** Distinct questions - the German arrangement, the Swiss question, the navigation of rivers, the ranking of diplomats, the slave trade - went to bodies that worked on them separately, which is why the Final Act of 9 June 1815 is a consolidating instrument stitched together from many separate agreements.
4. **Continuous informal negotiation.** The famous social life of the congress was not a distraction from the business; it was the medium of the business, because it produced the constant unstructured contact in which positions could be explored without being formally tabled.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** Vienna invented nothing as a formal institution and everything as a practice. There was no charter, no secretariat and no voting rule - only the habit of the strongest powers settling questions among themselves and recording the result in a general instrument. That habit is what later observers named the Concert of Europe.

### 5. The secondary achievements, which are not secondary at all

✅ **Fact:** The congress issued a **Declaration on the abolition of the slave trade on 8 February 1815**, condemning the trade as repugnant to the principles of humanity and universal morality. It carried no enforcement machinery and met resistance from states with continuing interests in the trade, so its immediate practical effect was limited.

✅ **Fact:** The **Vienna Regulation on diplomatic precedence of 19 March 1815** established classes of diplomatic agents - ambassadors and papal legates, envoys and ministers, and chargés d'affaires - and provided that precedence within a class should follow the date of official notification of arrival.

✅ **Fact:** The congress agreed on the **free navigation of international rivers**, notably the Rhine and the Danube, opening major waterways to the states along them.

✅ **Fact:** The **perpetual neutrality of Switzerland** was recognised and guaranteed by the powers, in a declaration of **20 November 1815**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** these four items are the answer to the question "what did Vienna contribute that outlived the political order it created?" The political settlement was substantially dismantled between 1830 and 1871. The technical settlement was not. A rule of precedence that ends duels over who enters a room first, a principle that great rivers are not the private property of the states whose banks they run between, a declaration that converts the slave trade from a commercial activity into an acknowledged moral wrong, and a guaranteed neutral state in the centre of the Alps - all four survived every subsequent upheaval, and the first two are recognisable ancestors of modern diplomatic and international-waterway law.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** there is a sharp examination point buried here. The slave-trade declaration shows the exact shape of the congress's moral achievement and its exact limit in one instrument: the powers were capable of announcing a universal principle and incapable of enforcing one, because enforcement would have required an authority above the sovereign states, which is the one thing the settlement was designed never to create. The same structural fact explains why the Concert could coordinate and could not legislate - the point that decides the non-equivalence argument of Lesson 17.

### 6. Comparison: the Vienna method against a modern conference

| Feature | ✅ Vienna, 1814-15 | ⚠️ A modern multilateral conference |
|---|---|---|
| Membership | Everyone present; a handful deciding | Defined membership with formal standing |
| Decision rule | None written; effective agreement among the great powers | Written voting rules, quorum, majorities |
| Permanent organs | None | Secretariat, continuing bodies, records |
| Record | One consolidating general treaty | Charter or convention, plus continuing instruments |
| Basis of authority | Possession of armies and the consent of the other strong | Conferred legal authority, however weak in practice |
| Treatment of small states | Consulted, lobbied, informed; occasionally compensated | Formally equal, practically variable |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the comparison is the reason Lesson 17 can be written without either overstating or dismissing the Concert's ancestry. Vienna produced *the practice* of multilateral management - continuous consultation, conference diplomacy, technical committees, a consolidating general instrument - without any of *the institutions* through which later systems tried to make that practice binding. Whatever one concludes about the value of the settlement, the procedural inheritance is real and is easier to defend than any claim about collective security.

### 7. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** If the great powers decided everything among themselves, why did the smaller states attend at all, and why does the settlement bother to consolidate their claims in the Final Act?

**Reply.** Because the settlement had to be *accepted* as well as imposed, and acceptance was cheaper than enforcement. A German princeling whose claim had been heard, adjudicated and recorded in a general treaty signed by all the powers had a strong interest in the stability of that treaty; a princeling dispossessed without a hearing had none. The consolidating form of the Final Act is therefore a method of manufacturing stakeholders. It also had a practical use that mattered later: because the settlement existed as a single recorded instrument, any subsequent change to it could be identified as a change, which is what allowed the powers to treat the Belgian question of 1830 as something requiring collective authorisation rather than as a fact to be accepted or resisted individually.

⚠️ **Residual.** How far the smaller states genuinely shaped outcomes rather than merely registering them cannot be determined from the instruments alone, and this session does not assert a view. What can be asserted is the procedural shape - great-power decision, general-treaty consolidation - and its consequences.

### UPSC application

This lesson supplies the actors and the method, and both are frequently the difference between a generic answer and a specific one. A question asking you to *explain the working of the Congress of Vienna*, to *assess Metternich's role*, or to *examine great-power diplomacy after Napoleon* is answered from here. The two moves that earn marks are naming the five negotiators with their objectives rather than listing them, and stating that the congress decided through great-power committees rather than through a plenary - because that single procedural fact is what makes the Concert of Europe intelligible as its continuation.

**Probable framing.** Questions on Metternich's role, on the nature of great-power diplomacy, on the position of France after defeat, or on the origins of conference diplomacy.

**Common traps.** Treating Metternich as the sole author of the settlement - he was dominant in central Europe, but Castlereagh, Alexander I and Hardenberg pursued their own objectives, and Talleyrand successfully forced France into the centre. Assuming the congress voted. Assuming France was excluded. Ignoring the technical instruments, which are the part of Vienna that genuinely survived.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The five: **Metternich** (Austria), **Castlereagh** (Britain), **Alexander I** (Russia, in person), **Hardenberg** (Prussia), **Talleyrand** (France); **Wellington** for Britain in Castlereagh's absence.
2. ✅ Metternich: foreign minister from **1809**, chancellor from **1821**. Castlereagh: Foreign Secretary **1812-1822**.
3. ⚠️ Objectives differed sharply: Austrian primacy and monarchical authority; British equilibrium without commitment; a Russian Poland; Prussian compensation; French re-entry.
4. ✅ Talleyrand used **legitimacy** - the rights of lawful monarchs, the King of Saxony above all - to break France's isolation.
5. ⚠️ Legitimacy was both a real principle and an instrument, applied selectively.
6. ⚠️ Method: no plenary decisions; concentric circles of powers; specialist committees; continuous informal negotiation.
7. ✅ **Slave-trade declaration, 8 February 1815** - a moral condemnation without enforcement machinery.
8. ✅ **Diplomatic precedence, 19 March 1815** - three classes; precedence by date of notification.
9. ✅ **Free navigation of international rivers**, including the Rhine and Danube.
10. ✅ **Swiss perpetual neutrality** recognised and guaranteed, **20 November 1815**.
11. ⚠️ The technical settlement outlived the political settlement; that contrast is a ready-made conclusion for a "what did Vienna achieve?" answer.
12. ⚠️ The congress created a *practice* of great-power management and no *institutions* - the fact that decides the non-equivalence argument of Lesson 17.

### Concept check

**Question:** France in 1814 was the defeated power, with no army in the field, no allies and no territory to trade. Explain, step by step, how Talleyrand nevertheless converted her into a party to the central bargaining at Vienna, and state precisely what this shows about the principle he used.

**Model answer:** The steps are four. First, he identified that the victors were not united: Russia wanted a Polish kingdom under the Tsar's crown and Prussia wanted the kingdom of Saxony, while Austria and Britain regarded both demands as a dangerous advance of Russian power into central Europe. Second, he identified that Austria and Britain could not comfortably state their objection in its true form, because "we oppose this because it makes Russia too strong" is an argument from interest that invites the reply that every power's gains are equally self-interested. Third, he supplied a form of the objection that they could state - that a lawful sovereign, the King of Saxony, could not be extinguished merely because he had chosen the losing side, and that a settlement founded on the extinction of lawful rulers would have no principle at all to distinguish it from Napoleon's own practice. Fourth, he made France indispensable to the coalition that this argument created, and in January 1815 France joined Britain and Austria in a secret defensive agreement against Russia and Prussia, which is the point at which the defeated power became a member of the inner circle. What this shows about legitimacy is that it operated at Vienna as a genuine principle and as a diplomatic instrument at the same time: genuine, because the negotiators had no other available basis for a stable order and did in fact protect Saxony by it; instrumental, because it was invoked where it served a balance-of-power objective and not invoked where it did not, which is why Saxony's king survived while the Venetian Republic was not restored and Poland received no restoration at all.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that because legitimacy was used instrumentally it was therefore insincere. The inference does not follow, and a reader who draws it cannot explain the settlement's actual outcomes: Saxony really was preserved, the Bourbons really were restored in France, Spain and Naples, and France really was readmitted as an equal in 1818. A principle can be both believed and deployed, and at Vienna legitimacy was both - which is why it produced real protection for some rulers and no protection at all for peoples who had no ruler to protect.

**Bridge to Lesson 3.** You now know who decided and how. What you do not yet have is the *criterion* they were applying. Legitimacy has appeared as a principle and as a lever; balance of power has appeared as an objective; compensation has been mentioned but not defined. The next lesson takes the three announced principles apart, shows how they interacted, and then names the fourth one, which was never printed in any instrument and nevertheless governed the map.

## Lesson 3 - The three announced principles, and the fourth that was never printed

Progress: 3/18 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: legitimacy, balance of power and compensation defined and distinguished; containment as the unstated operating rule; how the four interact and where they contradict each other

CA search: not sought - the single current linkage for this topic is held in Lesson 17
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four principles, and what each one is *for*

```text
   THE QUESTION EVERY TERRITORIAL DECISION HAD TO ANSWER:
   "On what ground does this piece of land belong to this ruler?"

   +----------------------------------------------------------+
   | 1. LEGITIMACY            ANNOUNCED                        |
   |    Lawful dynastic right is the test of a valid claim.    |
   |    ANSWERS: who may rule?                                 |
   |    EVIDENCE: Bourbons restored in France; dynastic        |
   |    rulers restored across Europe; Saxony's king saved     |
   |    LIMIT: applied where it suited; no restoration for     |
   |    Venice, for the ecclesiastical states, or for Poland   |
   +----------------------------------------------------------+
                               |
   +----------------------------------------------------------+
   | 2. BALANCE OF POWER      ANNOUNCED                        |
   |    No single power may again dominate the continent.      |
   |    ANSWERS: how much may any one state hold?              |
   |    EVIDENCE: France contained but not destroyed;          |
   |    Netherlands united as a barrier; Swiss neutrality      |
   |    LIMIT: a balance among FIVE states says nothing        |
   |    about the peoples inside them                          |
   +----------------------------------------------------------+
                               |
   +----------------------------------------------------------+
   | 3. COMPENSATION          ANNOUNCED                        |
   |    A power that gives up a claim, or that bore the        |
   |    burden of the war, is paid in territory elsewhere.     |
   |    ANSWERS: how is the bargain balanced?                  |
   |    EVIDENCE: Prussia gains the Rhineland, Westphalia      |
   |    and part of Saxony; Austria takes Lombardy and         |
   |    Venetia; Piedmont gains Genoa; Russia gains Poland     |
   |    LIMIT: populations were transferred without consent    |
   +----------------------------------------------------------+
                               |
   +----------------------------------------------------------+
   | 4. CONTAINMENT           NEVER PRINTED AS A PRINCIPLE     |
   |    France must be ringed by states strong enough to       |
   |    hold her until the coalition can assemble.             |
   |    ANSWERS: where, exactly, does the territory go?        |
   |    EVIDENCE: the whole northern and eastern rim -         |
   |    Netherlands, Prussian Rhineland, enlarged Piedmont,    |
   |    guaranteed Swiss neutrality                            |
   |    WHY IT MATTERS: it is the rule that actually           |
   |    selects among options the other three leave open       |
   +----------------------------------------------------------+
```

*The fourth box is the one most answers omit, and it is the one that does the work. Legitimacy tells you that a lawful ruler must be found; balance tells you that nobody may become too strong; compensation tells you that losses must be paid for. None of the three tells you why the Rhineland should go to Prussia rather than to Bavaria, or why Genoa should go to Piedmont rather than be restored as a republic. Containment does.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Every square mile of Europe in 1814 was claimed by somebody, usually by two somebodies. Napoleon had abolished the Holy Roman Empire, destroyed or merged hundreds of German polities, extinguished ancient republics, created satellite kingdoms for his relatives, and moved dynasties around like furniture. When his empire collapsed, all those arrangements became simultaneously void and unrepealed.

So the negotiators faced a practical question in an extreme form: when two parties claim the same territory, by what test do you decide? Pure conquest would have meant that the strongest simply took what it wanted, which was Napoleon's principle and would have produced the same result as Napoleon. Pure restoration - putting everything back exactly as it stood in 1789 - was impossible, because the pre-revolutionary map included hundreds of units that no longer existed in any functioning sense, and because the victors themselves wanted territorial gains.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the three announced principles are the negotiators' answer, and the important thing about them is that they answer *different questions*. Legitimacy answers "who is entitled to rule?". Balance answers "how much may any one ruler hold?". Compensation answers "how do we make the bargain acceptable to everyone at the table?". Because they answer different questions, they do not compete for most decisions - and where they do compete, as over Poland and Saxony, the congress nearly collapsed. That is the structure of Lessons 3, 4 and 5 in one sentence.

### 2. Legitimacy, defined exactly

✅ **Fact:** The settlement worked through three famous principles - **legitimacy**, **balance of power** and **compensation**.

✅ **Fact:** Bourbon rule was restored in France, and dynastic rulers were restored in several parts of Europe.

**Definition.** Legitimacy at Vienna means that the valid title to rule a territory is *lawful dynastic right*, ordinarily the right of the family that held the territory before the revolutionary upheaval - not conquest, not popular choice, and not national composition.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the definition needs two qualifications that convert it from a slogan into an argument.

The first is that legitimacy was **not a programme of full restoration**. The Holy Roman Empire was not revived; the hundreds of German polities Napoleon had consolidated were not disinterred; the ancient republics of Venice and Genoa were not restored as republics; the ecclesiastical principalities did not return. Restoration was applied to the great dynasties and not to the older constitutional furniture of Europe. An answer that says "Vienna restored the old order" is therefore imprecise: it restored old *rulers* selectively and kept much of the revolutionary consolidation of territory because that consolidation was convenient.

The second is that legitimacy **did work where it was applied** and produced real outcomes against the interest of powerful states. Prussia wanted all of Saxony and did not get it. That result is not explicable on any balance-of-power calculation alone, because a Saxony divided between Prussia and its own king is not obviously more balanced than a Saxony wholly Prussian - it is explicable because a lawful king could not simply be extinguished in a settlement whose declared foundation was lawful right.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** Legitimacy at Vienna was the restoration of dynasties, not of the old constitutional order, and it was applied where the powers could afford to apply it - which is why it protected the King of Saxony and did nothing whatever for the Poles.

### 3. Balance of power, defined exactly

**Definition.** Balance of power at Vienna means a distribution of territory, population and strategic position among the great powers such that no one of them can dominate the others, and such that any attempt to do so provokes a combination against it.

✅ **Fact:** France was contained rather than destroyed. The Kingdom of the Netherlands was created by joining Belgium and Holland as a buffer against France. Switzerland's neutrality was recognised.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the crucial point about the Vienna balance, and the one that distinguishes an intelligent answer, is that it was a balance **among five states, deliberately including the defeated one**. That inclusion is not softness. A balance of four with France excluded would be unstable for an obvious reason: it would place France permanently outside the system with an incentive to overturn it, and it would leave the four to balance against each other with no shared object. Keeping France as a great power inside the balance is what makes the equilibrium self-correcting rather than a temporary coalition against a permanent enemy.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the second crucial point is the limit. A balance among states is silent about everything *inside* states. It tells you nothing about whether the populations of Lombardy or Venetia or Congress Poland consent to their rulers, because consent is not a variable in the calculation. This is not an oversight on the negotiators' part but a feature of the concept, and it is the origin of the most durable criticism of the settlement: Vienna solved a problem in the distribution of power and did not address, because its instruments could not address, a problem in the distribution of loyalty.

### 4. Compensation, defined exactly

**Definition.** Compensation at Vienna means that a power which surrenders a claim, or which bore a disproportionate share of the war, receives equivalent territory elsewhere, so that the bargain is balanced and each participant leaves with a recognisable gain.

✅ **Fact:** Prussia gained the **Rhineland, Westphalia and part of Saxony**. Austria received **Lombardy and Venetia** and influenced much of the Italian peninsula. **Sardinia-Piedmont** was strengthened by the addition of **Genoa**. Most of the Duchy of Warsaw passed under Russian control as **Congress Poland**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** compensation is the principle that reveals what the settlement actually was. It treats territory as a transferable quantity of power - so many souls, so many revenues, such a strategic position - to be moved between rulers so that the ledger balances. It is therefore incompatible, at the root, with any notion that a population has a claim to be consulted about its ruler. This is not a moral observation appended to the history; it is the mechanism that generates the grievances of Lessons 13 and 14. The Belgians, the Lombards, the Venetians, the Poles and the Genoese did not become discontented because the negotiators were cruel. They became discontented because the accounting unit of the settlement was the province and not the people.

⚠️ **Analytical inference: the irony worth an argument.** Compensation created the two states that later destroyed the settlement's central arrangements. Prussia, compensated with the Rhineland and Westphalia, acquired the coal, iron and population that made her the industrial and military leader of Germany, and thereby acquired the capacity to unify Germany against Austria. Sardinia-Piedmont, strengthened by Genoa, acquired the port and the maritime resources that made her the plausible nucleus of an Italian state. ⚠️ Vienna's compensations, designed to stabilise the balance, armed the two states whose later success dismantled it.

### 5. Containment: the principle that was never announced

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** now take the three announced principles and try to use them to decide an actual question. Belgium had belonged to Austria before the wars. Legitimacy would return it to Austria. Compensation would allow Austria to be paid elsewhere instead. Balance is indifferent, since Belgium is small. Why, then, was Belgium joined to Holland in a new Kingdom of the Netherlands, an arrangement with no dynastic precedent whatever?

The answer is containment. Austria did not want a distant province on the far side of Germany that she could not defend and that would drag her into every French war. What the powers wanted on France's northern frontier was a state with an interest in resisting France and a coastline that Britain could support. None of the three announced principles generates that answer; a fourth consideration does, and it governs the map.

**Definition.** Containment at Vienna means the deliberate construction of a ring of states on France's frontiers strong enough to delay a French advance until the coalition can assemble - a "barrier" rather than a wall.

| ✅ The barrier, clockwise from the sea | ⚠️ The containment function |
|---|---|
| **Kingdom of the Netherlands** - Belgium joined to Holland | A single state on the invasion route to the Channel, with a coast Britain can supply and reinforce |
| **Prussian Rhineland and Westphalia** | Places the most militarised German state directly on France's eastern frontier, so that any French advance into Germany is resisted at once rather than after negotiation among the German states |
| **Guaranteed Swiss neutrality** | Closes the Alpine passes to both sides by agreement rather than by garrison, removing a whole theatre from the calculation |
| **Enlarged Sardinia-Piedmont, with Genoa** | Blocks the Alpine and Mediterranean approach to Italy, and gives the barrier state a port |
| **Austria in Lombardy and Venetia** | Places a great power directly behind the Piedmontese barrier, so that an attack on northern Italy is an attack on Austria |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice what the table proves about the Prussian Rhineland. It is usually taught as compensation, and it was compensation; but it is also the single most consequential containment decision of the settlement, because it moved Prussia's centre of gravity westward and made her, rather than Austria, the German power that faced France. ⚠️ The decision that best served the containment of France in 1815 is the decision that most powerfully advanced Prussian primacy in Germany by 1866 - which is the sharpest available illustration of a settlement producing an outcome opposite to its design.

### 6. How the four principles interact - and where they collide

```text
   MOST DECISIONS: the principles AGREE, because they answer
   different questions
   -------------------------------------------------------------
   Bourbon restoration in France
     legitimacy: yes (lawful dynasty)
     balance:    yes (a France inside the system)
     containment: yes (a stable, non-expansionist France)
     -> easy decision

   COLLISION CASE 1: SAXONY
     legitimacy:   the king is lawful -> he must be preserved
     compensation: Prussia is owed for 1806 -> she must be paid
     balance:      a Prussia enlarged by all Saxony, backed by
                   Russia in Poland, is too strong in the centre
     -> LEGITIMACY + BALANCE beat COMPENSATION: Prussia gets
        PART of Saxony, the king keeps the rest

   COLLISION CASE 2: POLAND
     legitimacy:   Poland was partitioned before the wars;
                   there is no lawful Polish dynasty to restore
     compensation: Russia bore the decisive burden of 1812-14
                   and claims payment
     balance:      a Russian kingdom on the Vistula advances
                   Russia deep into central Europe
     -> COMPENSATION beats BALANCE, but only partly: Congress
        Poland is created under the Tsar, smaller than demanded

   COLLISION CASE 3: THE NATIONS
     legitimacy:   silent - nations are not dynasties
     balance:      silent - nations are not powers
     compensation: silent - nations are not claimants
     containment:  hostile - a national state on France's
                   frontier is unpredictable
     -> NO PRINCIPLE SPEAKS FOR THEM. This is the settlement's
        structural exclusion, and the origin of 1830 and 1848
```

*The third collision is not a collision at all, and that is the point. The national claim loses at Vienna not because it is outvoted but because the settlement has no vocabulary in which the claim can be stated. That is a much stronger criticism than "the powers ignored nationalism", and it is the form the criticism should take in an answer.*

### 7. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** Is "containment" not simply the balance of power applied to France? Introducing a fourth principle looks like multiplying entities without necessity.

**Reply.** The two are related but they are not the same, and the difference is testable. Balance of power is a general and symmetrical rule: it applies to every great power, including the victors, and would in principle license a combination against Russia or Prussia exactly as against France. Containment is particular and asymmetrical: it identifies one state as the standing danger and builds a permanent structure around that state specifically. The test that separates them is the treatment of Russia. On a strict balance-of-power reading, Russia's advance to the Vistula was at least as dangerous to the equilibrium as France's frontiers, and Castlereagh and Metternich said so at the time; yet no ring of barrier states was built around Russia, and no twenty-year alliance was directed at her. The settlement balanced against Russia by negotiation and contained France by construction. That asymmetry is exactly what "containment" names, and no general balance principle predicts it.

⚠️ **Residual.** How consciously the negotiators separated the two is not recoverable from the instruments, and this session does not claim that any of them used the word. What is recoverable is the asymmetry in what they built, and the asymmetry is what the analysis rests on.

### UPSC application

The syllabus clause owns this topic through **redrawal of national boundaries**, and this lesson is the part that converts a boundary list into an argument. The single highest-value move available on this topic is to state each principle, attach one territorial illustration to it, and then state the consequence it set up - principle, application, consequence, in one movement. An answer built that way cannot become a map list, which is the standard failure mode.

**Probable framing.** "Explain the principles and major territorial decisions of the Congress of Vienna." "Examine the redrawal of European boundaries after the Napoleonic wars." "How far did the Vienna settlement rest on principle and how far on great-power interest?"

**Common traps.** Reciting three principles without illustrations. Treating legitimacy as a complete restoration of the pre-revolutionary order. Treating compensation as a minor administrative rule rather than as the mechanism that transferred populations without consent. Omitting containment and therefore being unable to explain the Netherlands, the Prussian Rhineland or the enlargement of Piedmont. Writing that the powers "ignored nationalism" without explaining that the settlement's vocabulary had no place for a national claim.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Three announced principles: **legitimacy**, **balance of power**, **compensation**.
2. ✅ Legitimacy in action: **Bourbons restored in France**; dynastic rulers restored in several parts of Europe; the **King of Saxony preserved**.
3. ⚠️ Legitimacy restored *dynasties*, not the old constitutional order - no Holy Roman Empire, no Venetian or Genoese republic, no ecclesiastical states.
4. ✅ Balance in action: France **contained, not destroyed**; **Netherlands** created as a barrier; **Swiss neutrality** recognised.
5. ⚠️ The Vienna balance deliberately included the defeated power, which is what makes it self-correcting rather than a coalition against a permanent enemy.
6. ✅ Compensation in action: Prussia takes **Rhineland, Westphalia, part of Saxony**; Austria takes **Lombardy and Venetia**; **Piedmont gains Genoa**; Russia takes most of the Duchy of Warsaw as **Congress Poland**.
7. ⚠️ Compensation treats territory as transferable power and is therefore structurally incompatible with consent.
8. ⚠️ The fourth, unprinted principle is **containment**: the barrier ring of Netherlands, Prussian Rhineland, neutral Switzerland, enlarged Piedmont, with Austria behind it in Italy.
9. ⚠️ The Prussian Rhineland is simultaneously the best containment decision of 1815 and the decision that most advanced Prussian primacy in Germany later.
10. ⚠️ Collisions: Saxony - legitimacy plus balance beat compensation; Poland - compensation partly beats balance; the nations - **no principle speaks for them at all**.
11. ⚠️ The strongest form of the nationalism criticism is not "they ignored it" but "the settlement had no vocabulary in which a national claim could even be made".
12. ⚠️ Vienna's compensations built **Prussia** and **Sardinia-Piedmont** - the two states that later destroyed its central arrangements.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using one territorial illustration for each, show how legitimacy, compensation and containment could point to three different owners for the same disputed territory, and state which consideration in fact governed the Vienna map where the three diverged.

**Model answer:** Take the Austrian Netherlands, the territory that became Belgium. Legitimacy pointed to restoration to Austria, which had held it lawfully before the revolutionary wars and whose dynasty was among those the settlement was restoring. Compensation pointed to giving it to whichever power was owed most, or to allowing Austria to exchange it for a gain elsewhere, which is in substance what happened when Austria took Lombardy and Venetia instead. Containment pointed to neither, because what the powers required on that frontier was a state with a standing interest in resisting a French advance and a coastline that Britain could support, and neither a distant Austrian province nor an unattached duchy supplied that. The outcome shows which consideration governed: Belgium was joined to Holland in a new Kingdom of the Netherlands, an arrangement with no dynastic precedent, no claimant demanding it and no basis in the announced principles at all. The same pattern governs the Prussian Rhineland, awarded to the most militarised German state precisely because it placed serious force on France's eastern frontier, and the enlargement of Piedmont by Genoa, which converted a restored monarchy into a barrier with a port. Where the three announced principles diverged, the map was settled by the unstated requirement that France be ringed by states capable of holding her until a coalition could form.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that because containment was never printed as a principle it was therefore a modern historian's invention imposed on the evidence. The test is not whether the negotiators used the word but whether the pattern exists in what they built, and it does: the barrier states are contiguous, they lie on France's frontiers and nowhere else, and no comparable ring was constructed around Russia despite contemporaries saying that Russia's advance was equally dangerous to the balance. A rule that predicts the map better than the stated principles do is part of the explanation, whatever it was called at the time.

**Bridge to Lesson 4.** The principles are now defined and their collisions identified. The next step is the map itself - not as a list of transfers, but as a set of decisions each of which can be traced back to one of the four considerations, and each of which set up a consequence that the settlement's authors did not intend.

### Visual: the barrier ring, read as a defensive system

```text

   | borders |       readmitted 1818              | WESTPHALIA       |
   +---------+                                    | the most military |

        |                                                 |
   +------------------+                     +---------------------------+

        |                                   +---------------------------+
   +------------------+                                 |
```
