━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four congresses, and a system coming apart

```text
 1818  AIX-LA-CHAPELLE          UNANIMITY
 ----  called to settle: the end of the occupation of France
       decided: indemnity settled, occupation ended, FRANCE ADMITTED
       mood: the high point; five powers, one purpose
       BUT: with France inside, the alliance has no adversary left
                              |
                              | 1820: revolutions in SPAIN (January),
                              | NAPLES (July), PORTUGAL (August);
                              | PIEDMONT follows in March 1821
                              v
 1820  TROPPAU                 THE SPLIT OPENS
 ----  called to settle: what to do about revolution in Naples
       decided: the TROPPAU PROTOCOL, 19 November 1820 - states
                altered by revolution cease to be members of the
                European alliance and may be brought back by force
       SIGNED BY: Austria, Prussia, Russia
       REFUSED BY: Britain and France, present only as observers
                              |
                              v
 1821  LAIBACH                 THE SPLIT IS ACTED ON
 ----  called to settle: the execution of the Troppau principle
       decided: AUSTRIAN INTERVENTION in Naples authorised;
                Piedmont suppressed as well
       BRITAIN: refuses to participate or endorse; observes only
                              |
                              | Aug 1822: Castlereagh dies;
                              | CANNING becomes Foreign Secretary
                              v
 1822  VERONA                  THE RUPTURE
 ----  called to settle: what to do about revolution in Spain
       decided: FRENCH INTERVENTION in Spain authorised
       BRITAIN: Wellington WITHDRAWS on Canning's instructions
       result: the last major effective congress of the system
```

*The visual is a descent, and the descent has a cause visible in the first box. At Aix the five powers agreed because the question was the disposal of France, which all of them had an interest in settling. From Troppau onwards every question was about revolution inside a third state - and on that question the powers had never agreed and had never been asked to.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Article VI had said the powers would meet periodically. It had not said what they would meet about. In 1818 that omission cost nothing, because the agenda was obvious: the occupation of France had run its course and had to be wound up.

By 1820 the omission was fatal. A series of revolutions broke out in southern Europe within months of each other - in Spain in January, in Naples in July, in Portugal in August, and in Piedmont the following March. Each was a constitutionalist movement demanding a written constitution from an absolute monarch. Each was internal to the state concerned. None of them threatened any frontier established at Vienna.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** so the powers had to answer a question that the Quadruple Alliance had never posed: *does the alliance exist to protect the territorial settlement, or does it exist to protect the type of government the settlement assumed?* Metternich and Alexander answered the second; Castlereagh answered the first; and the answer given by each followed directly from the kind of state he served. A multinational dynastic empire and an autocracy had every reason to treat constitutionalism anywhere as a threat; a parliamentary monarchy that had itself limited its crown by law could not sign a document declaring constitutions to be a species of revolution.

### 2. Aix-la-Chapelle, 1818: the high point

✅ **Fact:** At the **Congress of Aix-la-Chapelle in 1818**, the indemnity was settled, the allied occupation of France was ended, and **France was admitted** to the alliance, marking the peak of consensus and of optimism about the system.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three things about Aix deserve emphasis.

First, it achieved exactly what it was called to achieve, which no subsequent congress did. Second, it demonstrated the congress method working at its best: a complex question - the winding up of an occupation, the discharge of an indemnity, the status of a former enemy - settled by negotiation among the powers concerned, without coercion and ahead of schedule. Third, and least noticed, it was the only congress whose subject matter lay squarely inside the competence everyone agreed the alliance possessed. Aix dealt with France; the Quadruple Alliance was about France; there was no jurisdictional question to quarrel over.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is why the "high point" description is more than a mood. Aix was the high point because it was the last occasion on which the powers met to decide something they all agreed they were entitled to decide.

### 3. Troppau, 1820: the split opens

✅ **Fact:** The **Congress of Troppau** met in 1820 to consider the revolutions in Naples and Spain and produced the **Troppau Protocol**, signed on **19 November 1820** by **Austria, Prussia and Russia**, asserting a right to intervene in states undergoing revolution in order to uphold legitimate monarchical government. **Britain and France refused to accept or sign the protocol**, sending only observers or representatives without authority to commit their governments.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Protocol's structure is worth stating carefully because it is a genuinely radical document and is usually summarised too loosely. Its claim is not merely that the powers *may* assist a monarch who asks for help. It is that a state whose government has been altered by revolution has thereby placed itself outside the European alliance, and that the other powers are entitled - collectively, and if necessary by force - to bring it back. That is a doctrine about membership of the European system itself, and its implication is that the internal constitution of every state is a matter of international competence.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the British and French refusal is equally significant. Note that they did not merely abstain from acting; they declined to accept the principle. And note that they were *present*, as observers. ⚠️ That combination - inside the room, outside the doctrine - is the exact description of the system from 1820 onwards, and it is why the system did not end at Troppau but became progressively less able to act as a single body.

### 4. Laibach, 1821: the principle executed

✅ **Fact:** The **Congress of Laibach** in 1821 authorised **Austrian intervention in Naples** and in **Piedmont**. Britain again refused to participate directly or to endorse the principle of intervention, and was present as an observer. Austria subsequently sent troops and suppressed the Neapolitan revolution.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Laibach converts a doctrine into an operation, and in doing so it reveals three features of the system that a good answer should name.

1. **The intervention was authorised collectively and executed nationally.** Austria acted, but she acted with the sanction of a congress. That is the system's characteristic form: a great power does what it wants to do anyway and acquires a European licence for it.
2. **The licence was worth having.** Austria could have marched into Naples without any congress at all; what the congress supplied was the assurance that the other powers would not treat the action as a unilateral extension of Austrian power in Italy. ⚠️ The congress system therefore functioned, among other things, as a device for making a great power's local dominance tolerable to its rivals.
3. **The cost was the system's unity.** Every authorised intervention widened the gap between the three eastern powers and Britain, because each one demonstrated in practice what Britain had refused to accept in principle.

### 5. Verona, 1822: the rupture

✅ **Fact:** The **Congress of Verona**, held from **October to December 1822**, considered the revolution in Spain and approved **French intervention**. The British delegate, the **Duke of Wellington**, **withdrew from the congress on the instructions of George Canning**, who had become Foreign Secretary after Castlereagh's death in **August 1822**. ✅ Verona was the last major effective congress of the system, and it solidified the division between constitutional Britain and the more conservative continental powers.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three things converge at Verona, and the convergence is what makes it the end.

First, the **subject**. Spain was not Naples. A French army crossing the Pyrenees to restore an absolute king raised, for Britain, the spectre of French power re-established in the peninsula that Britain had spent six years of war clearing - and, worse, raised the possibility that the restoration would be extended across the Atlantic to Spain's rebellious American colonies, where British commerce had by now established itself. The intervention therefore touched British interests directly, which the Italian interventions had not.

Second, the **personnel**. ✅ Castlereagh died in August 1822 and was succeeded by Canning. Castlereagh had dissented from the interventionist doctrine while valuing the alliance and working to keep Britain inside it; Canning was prepared to treat the divergence as a fact and act on it. ⚠️ The change of person did not create the division - the State Paper of 1820 had already stated it in full - but it changed Britain's management of the division from containment to separation.

Third, the **precedent**. Once a congress had authorised a French army to cross an international frontier to overturn a constitution, the meetings could no longer be represented as consultative gatherings of powers with a shared purpose. They had become an instrument by which three powers licensed each other's interventions while two looked on. ⚠️ A body of that kind does not need to be abolished; it simply stops being convened, which is what happened.

### 6. The pattern, in one table

| Congress | ✅ Year | ✅ Called to decide | ✅ Decided | ⚠️ Effect on the system |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Aix-la-Chapelle** | 1818 | The end of the occupation of France | Indemnity settled; occupation ended; **France admitted** | Peak of consensus - and removal of the alliance's adversary |
| **Troppau** | 1820 | Revolution in Naples and Spain | **Troppau Protocol, 19 November 1820**: right of intervention asserted by Austria, Prussia, Russia; Britain and France refuse | The doctrinal split opens |
| **Laibach** | 1821 | Execution of the Troppau principle | **Austrian intervention in Naples and Piedmont authorised** | The split is acted on; Britain observes only |
| **Verona** | 1822 | Revolution in Spain | **French intervention authorised**; Wellington withdraws on Canning's instructions | The rupture; the last major effective congress |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** read the table's third column downwards. The subject of the first congress was an interstate question - the disposal of a defeated power. The subject of every subsequent congress was an intrastate question - the form of government of a third country. ⚠️ **The congress system did not fail because the powers quarrelled; it failed because it ran out of the kind of question it had been designed to answer and moved on to a kind of question about which its members held incompatible convictions.** That is the sentence to carry into an answer on why the system declined.

### 7. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** If Verona was the last major effective congress, is it not simply true that the Concert of Europe ended in 1822 - and does the claim that it "survived" not amount to salvaging a system that had plainly stopped working?

**Reply.** The claim requires care and the standard caution is explicit: ✅ do not write that the Concert "collapsed immediately"; it weakened unevenly and survived in modified consultation even after the old congress rhythm faded. The distinction that saves the claim from vagueness is the one established in Lesson 7. The *congress system* - the formal, periodic, agenda-setting meetings under Article VI - did effectively end with Verona. The *Concert* - the practice of consulting before acting on matters affecting the general settlement - did not. ✅ The decisive evidence is Belgium: in 1830 the powers were confronted with an actual breach of the Vienna map, and instead of either ignoring it or fighting over it, they handled it collectively and accepted the outcome. ⚠️ A system capable of that in 1830 had not ended in 1822. What had ended was the attempt to run the practice through scheduled congresses with a doctrine of intervention attached.

⚠️ **Residual.** Whether the congress system could have survived had the revolutions of 1820 not occurred is a counterfactual taken up in Lesson 16 and not settled here.

### UPSC application

The four congresses are the skeleton of any answer on the working of the system, and the examiner's real test is whether you can explain the movement from 1818 to 1822 rather than list four meetings. The move that earns marks is stating the jurisdictional point: Aix decided a question everyone agreed the alliance owned, and every later congress decided a question nobody had agreed it owned. That single observation converts a chronology into an explanation of decline.

**Probable framing.** "Trace the working and decline of the congress system." "Examine the Concert of Europe as an instrument of peace or of repression." "Why did the congress system fail?"

**Common traps.** Listing the congresses without the split. Attributing the Troppau Protocol to all five powers. Saying Britain "left the Concert" at Verona - Britain withdrew from that congress and from the interventionist doctrine, not from great-power consultation, and was central to the Belgian settlement eight years later. Dating Canning's accession before Castlereagh's death in August 1822. Treating Verona as the end of the Concert rather than of the congress rhythm.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Aix-la-Chapelle, 1818** - indemnity settled, occupation ended, **France admitted**; peak of consensus.
2. ⚠️ Aix was the last congress whose subject lay inside the alliance's agreed competence.
3. ✅ Revolutions of 1820-21: **Spain (January 1820), Naples (July 1820), Portugal (August 1820), Piedmont (March 1821)**.
4. ✅ **Troppau, 1820** - the **Troppau Protocol of 19 November 1820**, signed by **Austria, Prussia and Russia**: a state altered by revolution leaves the European alliance and may be restored by force. **Britain and France refused**; both attended as observers only.
5. ✅ **Laibach, 1821** - authorised **Austrian intervention in Naples and Piedmont**; Britain observed and refused endorsement.
6. ✅ **Castlereagh died August 1822**; **Canning** succeeded him.
7. ✅ **Verona, October-December 1822** - authorised **French intervention in Spain**; **Wellington withdrew on Canning's instructions**; the last major effective congress.
8. ⚠️ The authorised-but-nationally-executed intervention is the system's characteristic form: a great power acts, and the congress supplies a European licence that makes the action tolerable to its rivals.
9. ⚠️ The system failed because it exhausted interstate questions and moved to intrastate ones on which its members held incompatible convictions.
10. ✅ The **Concert** survived the **congress system**: the powers handled Belgium collectively in 1830.
11. ❌ Do not write that the Concert "collapsed immediately" after 1822.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Congress of Aix-la-Chapelle in 1818 is described as the high point of the congress system and the congresses that followed as its decline. Explain the structural reason for that reversal, and say why the reason was built into the system from the start.

**Model answer:** The reversal follows from the kind of question each congress was asked to decide. Aix-la-Chapelle dealt with the winding up of the occupation of France, the discharge of her indemnity and her admission to the alliance - an interstate question, squarely within the competence that the Quadruple Alliance had been created to exercise, since that alliance existed to contain France and to uphold the territorial settlement. Every congress after Aix dealt with an intrastate question: whether the powers might act against a revolution inside Naples, inside Piedmont or inside Spain, none of which altered a single frontier drawn at Vienna. On that second kind of question the members held incompatible convictions determined by the kind of state each of them was: a multinational dynastic empire and an autocracy necessarily regarded constitutionalism anywhere as a danger, while a parliamentary monarchy could not subscribe to a doctrine declaring constitutions to be a form of revolution justifying foreign invasion. The reason was built in from the start because Article VI of the Quadruple Alliance provided for periodic meetings without defining their competence: it said that the powers would meet, and said nothing about what they might decide. So long as the shared adversary remained, the omission was harmless, because the agenda selected itself. The admission of France at Aix removed the adversary, the revolutions of 1820 supplied a new agenda, and the system was then required to answer a jurisdictional question it had never settled - with the result that Troppau split it doctrinally, Laibach split it in practice and Verona ended it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining the decline as a personal matter - Castlereagh's death, Canning's hostility, Metternich's ambition. The personalities changed the tempo and the style of the break, but the division existed before any of them acted on it: Britain's reservation against intervention in the internal affairs of other states was present in the alliance from 1815 and was stated formally in May 1820, before Troppau met and more than two years before Castlereagh died.

**Bridge to Lesson 9.** The congresses split over intervention, and that word has been used so far without being examined. What exactly was being claimed, by whom, on what ground, and what exactly was the counter-claim? The two documents that state the two positions were written within six months of each other in 1820, and reading them against each other is the fastest route to understanding why a settlement designed to prevent war among great powers ended by dividing them.

## Lesson 9 - The quarrel over intervention: the Troppau Protocol against the British State Paper

### Visual: two answers to one question

```text
   | sovereign destroys the         |   | constitutional monarchy whose  |
   | foundation of the whole order. |   | own liberties rest on exactly  |
   |                                |   | the kind of act the doctrine   |
   | LOGIC: internal change is an   |   | condemns; and no British       |
   | international fact.            |   | government could defend such a |
   |                                |   | commitment in Parliament.      |
   |                                |   |                                |
   | CONSEQUENCE: Naples 1821,      |   | LOGIC: the settlement is       |
   | Piedmont 1821, Spain 1823      |   | TERRITORIAL. Frontiers are     |
   |                                |   | the alliance's business;       |
   |                                |   | constitutions are not.         |
   |                                |   |                                |
   |                                |   | CONSEQUENCE: Britain observes  |
   |                                |   | and does not sign; Spanish     |
   |                                |   | America is shielded; Verona    |
   |                                |   | ends the congress system.      |
   +--------------------------------+   +--------------------------------+
                        \             /
                         \           /
                    THE UNDERLYING DISAGREEMENT:
            what was the alliance FOR - a MAP or a REGIME TYPE?
```

*The two documents were written within six months of each other and neither is a reply to the other: the British paper came first. That chronology matters, because it shows the division was not produced by Troppau - it was already fully stated before Troppau met.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Start with the concrete situation, because the abstraction is meaningless without it. In January 1820 a military revolt in Spain compelled Ferdinand VII to accept a constitution he had earlier repudiated. In July, a similar movement in Naples compelled Ferdinand I to do the same. In neither case was a frontier moved, a treaty broken, or a foreign power attacked. The kings remained on their thrones. What had changed was the terms on which they ruled.

Now ask the question that the Quadruple Alliance had never answered: is that a European question at all?

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two answers were available and each is coherent. On the first, the Vienna settlement is a distribution of territory among sovereigns, and it is breached only when a frontier changes; what a sovereign concedes to his own subjects is his affair. On the second, the settlement rests on a *principle* - that lawful monarchical right is the basis of a valid state - and a constitution extorted by force from a sovereign attacks that principle as directly as an invasion attacks a frontier, with the added danger that the example is contagious. ⚠️ Both readings can be derived from the settlement's own documents, which is precisely why the disagreement could not be resolved by argument.

### 2. The Troppau doctrine, analysed

✅ **Fact:** The **Troppau Protocol** of **19 November 1820**, signed by **Austria, Prussia and Russia**, declared collective action against states that had undergone revolution, justifying intervention to uphold legitimate monarchical governments. **Britain and France refused to accept or sign it.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the doctrine has three components and it is worth separating them, because each is attacked by a different objection.

1. **The membership claim.** A state whose government has been altered by revolution ceases to be a member of the European alliance. This converts the alliance from a treaty among states into a club with a qualification for membership - and the qualification is a form of government.
2. **The restoration claim.** The other powers may bring such a state back into the alliance, using force if necessary. This is an assertion of a collective right of armed intervention in a state that has attacked nobody.
3. **The collectivity claim.** The action is to be taken jointly, on behalf of Europe, rather than by any single interested power. This is the claim that makes the doctrine sound like law rather than like the aggrandisement of the intervening power.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third component is the one that gave the doctrine its plausibility and also its vulnerability. If intervention is collective and European, it is not Austrian expansion in Italy or French expansion in Spain; it is the enforcement of a general rule. But the collectivity was never real: from Troppau onwards, two of the five refused, and each intervention was in fact carried out by the single power with the strongest local interest in carrying it out. ⚠️ The doctrine therefore claimed a European authority it did not possess, and supplied a European vocabulary for actions each intervening power had independent reasons to take - which is the strongest form of the "repression" charge and should be stated that way rather than as generalised disapproval.

### 3. The British State Paper of 5 May 1820

✅ **Fact:** Castlereagh's **State Paper of 5 May 1820** set out Britain's position: Britain would not be bound to military intervention in the internal affairs of sovereign states, and the alliance had been created against the military domination of Europe by France, not as a union for the government of the world or for the superintendence of the internal affairs of other states.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three features of the document are worth teaching exactly.

First, **its date**. It is written in May 1820 - after the Spanish revolution of January but before the Neapolitan revolution of July and more than six months before Troppau. It is therefore not a reaction to the Troppau doctrine. It is a pre-emptive statement of a position Britain already held, and it disposes of any reading that treats the split as an improvised British defection.

Second, **its ground**. The objection is not that revolution is good or that constitutions are desirable; Castlereagh's government was conducting a vigorous domestic campaign against radical agitation at the very time the paper was written. The objection is *constitutional and jurisdictional*: a British government cannot bind itself, in advance and indefinitely, to send armies into other countries to determine their form of government, because it cannot obtain or maintain parliamentary consent for such an undertaking, and because the British constitution itself is the product of exactly the kind of limitation on monarchy that the doctrine condemns.

Third, **its limit**. The paper does not say Britain will never intervene anywhere. It says Britain will not accept a *general obligation* to intervene against revolution as such, and reserves the judgement of each case to itself. ⚠️ That is the difference between refusing a doctrine and adopting a rival doctrine, and it explains something otherwise puzzling in Lesson 11: the same Britain that refused to act against constitutions in Naples and Spain did act, alongside Russia and France, on behalf of the Greek insurgents against their lawful sovereign. There is no inconsistency, because Britain never adopted non-intervention as a principle; she refused a standing commitment and kept each case in her own hands.

### 4. The two conceptions of the alliance, side by side

| Question | ✅ Troppau answer (Austria, Prussia, Russia) | ✅ British answer (State Paper, 5 May 1820) |
|---|---|---|
| What was the alliance made against? | Revolution, of which Napoleon was one form | The military domination of Europe by France |
| What does the settlement protect? | A principle of legitimate monarchical authority | A distribution of territory among states |
| Is internal change an international fact? | Yes - it removes a state from the alliance | No - it is the affair of the state concerned |
| Is there a standing obligation to act? | Yes, collectively and by force if required | No; each case is judged on its own merits |
| What is the system's proper business? | The maintenance of the European order in all its aspects | Frontiers, and the prevention of a single power dominating the continent |
| ⚠️ Why the answer was given | Multinational dynastic empire and autocracy: constitutionalism anywhere is contagious | Parliamentary monarchy: no government could defend such a commitment at home, and the doctrine condemns Britain's own constitution |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the final row is the key to the whole lesson, and it is the difference between a descriptive answer and an analytical one. The division is not a difference of temperament or of foreign-policy preference; it follows from the internal constitution of the states concerned. ⚠️ **Each power's position on intervention abroad was determined by the kind of state it was at home.** Austria could not concede the national and constitutional principle anywhere without conceding its own dissolution; Russia's autocracy admitted no theory of limited monarchy; Britain's government was itself limited by a representative assembly whose consent it required. The split of 1820 was therefore latent in the alliance from the day it was signed, and no diplomacy could have removed it.

### 5. The consequences, traced forward

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four consequences flow from the division, and they organise the three lessons that follow.

1. **Intervention proceeded, but as an eastern-power practice with a European label.** Naples and Piedmont in 1821, Spain in 1823 - each authorised by a congress from which two of the five dissented.
2. **The congresses became unusable.** A meeting whose decisions two members will not accept cannot function as the executive of a system, and Verona was the last major effective one.
3. **Britain acquired a distinct and increasingly attractive position.** By refusing the doctrine, Britain became the power to which threatened constitutional movements and new states looked - a position Canning exploited systematically in Spanish America.
4. **The eastern powers acquired an identity.** The three who signed at Troppau became a recognisable bloc, and the continuity of that alignment is visible for decades afterwards in the handling of Poland and of central European affairs.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** Is the British position not straightforwardly hypocritical? Britain refused to police other people's internal affairs in Europe while ruling a large empire by force, suppressing radical agitation at home, and intervening freely wherever her commercial interests were engaged.

**Reply.** The charge has real content and should be conceded in the form in which it is true, because conceding it makes the remaining argument stronger. It is true that the British refusal was not a doctrine of non-intervention, that Britain's conduct outside Europe was not governed by the principle stated in the State Paper, and that British commercial interest coincided remarkably often with British principle - the shielding of Spanish America being the clearest case. What the charge does not establish is that the British position was insincere as an account of the *alliance*. The State Paper makes a narrow claim: that this treaty, made for this purpose, cannot be converted into a general licence to superintend other states' constitutions. That claim is accurate about the instrument, was stated before the doctrine it is said to answer, was maintained consistently through three congresses, and was acted on at the cost of the system Britain had helped to found. ⚠️ A position may be self-interested and correct at the same time, and the correct treatment is to state the interest and the argument together rather than using one to dismiss the other.

⚠️ **Residual.** How far Castlereagh personally regretted the divergence, and whether a longer tenure would have kept Britain formally inside the congress framework without accepting the doctrine, cannot be settled here. The documents establish the positions, not the counterfactual.

### UPSC application

This lesson is the analytical core of the topic and the source of its best answer material. Any question asking whether the Concert was an instrument of peace or of repression is answered from here, because the honest answer is that it was both and that the two were performed by different members of the same system. The move that earns the highest marks is the structural one: explain that each power's position on intervention followed from its own form of government, so that the split was built into the alliance rather than caused by any event or person.

**Probable framing.** "Was the Concert of Europe an instrument of peace or of repression? Discuss." "Examine the doctrine of intervention in post-Napoleonic Europe." "Account for Britain's divergence from the continental powers after 1815."

**Common traps.** Treating the Troppau Protocol as the act of all five powers. Treating Britain's position as a principle of non-intervention - it was a refusal of a standing obligation. Dating the British State Paper after Troppau, which reverses the argument. Explaining the split by personality - Canning's hostility - when it was stated in full by Castlereagh two years before Canning took office. Writing that intervention was illegal, when the whole difficulty was that there was no law to be illegal under.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Troppau Protocol, 19 November 1820** - Austria, Prussia, Russia; a state altered by revolution leaves the European alliance and may be restored by force; **Britain and France refused**.
2. ⚠️ Three components of the doctrine: the **membership** claim, the **restoration** claim, the **collectivity** claim.
3. ⚠️ Collectivity was the doctrine's plausibility and its weakness: two of five dissented, and each intervention was executed by the power with the strongest local interest.
4. ✅ **British State Paper, 5 May 1820** - the alliance was made against the military domination of Europe by France, not as a union for the government of the world or the superintendence of other states' internal affairs.
5. ⚠️ The paper precedes the Neapolitan revolution and Troppau: the split was stated before the doctrine it is said to answer.
6. ⚠️ Britain refused a **standing obligation**, not intervention as such - which is why she could act for Greece in 1827 without inconsistency.
7. ⚠️ **The decisive structural point:** each power's position on intervention abroad followed from the kind of state it was at home - multinational empire, autocracy, parliamentary monarchy.
8. ⚠️ Four consequences: intervention as an eastern practice with a European label; congresses become unusable; Britain becomes the refuge of constitutional causes; the eastern three become a durable bloc.
9. ❌ Do not describe intervention as illegal; there was no superior law, which is exactly the settlement's design.

### Concept check

**Question:** Britain refused to sign the Troppau Protocol in 1820 and declined to act against constitutions in Naples and Spain, yet in 1827 joined Russia and France in armed action on behalf of Greek insurgents against their lawful sovereign. Show that this is not an inconsistency, and identify the principle Britain was actually defending.

**Model answer:** Britain never adopted non-intervention as a principle, and the State Paper of 5 May 1820 does not assert one. What it asserts is a narrower proposition: that the alliance had been created against the military domination of Europe by France, that it was not a union for the government of the world or for the superintendence of the internal affairs of other states, and that Britain would therefore not be bound to a standing, general obligation to intervene against revolution wherever it occurred. That is a refusal of an open-ended commitment, not an undertaking never to act; the corollary is that Britain reserved the judgement of each case to herself, which is exactly what she then did. In Naples, Piedmont and Spain the cases involved subjects compelling their own sovereigns to grant constitutions, no frontier was disturbed, and no British interest was engaged that could justify sending an army to reverse the result; so Britain observed and declined to endorse. In the Greek case the calculation was different on every count: the revolt raised the Eastern question and the fate of Ottoman power, which bore directly on the European balance; unilateral Russian action against the Ottomans threatened to upset that balance in Russia's favour; British opinion was strongly engaged; and joint action allowed Britain to remain a party to the outcome rather than watch Russia determine it alone. The principle Britain was defending throughout is therefore not the immunity of internal affairs but the freedom of her own decision: that no treaty should convert British force into an automatic instrument of a doctrine adopted by other governments, and that each case should be judged by whether British interests and the European balance required action. The apparent inconsistency dissolves once the refusal is read as jurisdictional rather than moral.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the British position as liberal sympathy with constitutional movements. The government that issued the State Paper was simultaneously suppressing radical agitation at home, and its objection to the Troppau doctrine was constitutional and jurisdictional rather than ideological: no British ministry could bind Parliament to indefinite foreign intervention, and a doctrine declaring constitutions extorted from monarchs to be revolutionary condemned the British constitution itself.

**Bridge to Lesson 10.** The doctrine has now been stated, and it was not merely stated - it was executed three times in three years, by two different powers, in three different countries. What intervention actually looked like on the ground, what it achieved and what it cost the system is the next lesson.

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one doctrine, three operations

```text
   THE COMMON PATTERN
   ------------------
   1. A military or constitutionalist movement compels a monarch
      to grant or restore a constitution
   2. The monarch appeals, openly or tacitly, to the powers
   3. A congress authorises intervention
   4. ONE power supplies the army - the one with the strongest
      local interest
   5. The constitution is abolished; the monarch is restored to
      unlimited authority; repression follows
   6. Britain declines to endorse or participate

   CASE 1: NAPLES                  CASE 2: PIEDMONT
   July 1820: revolution           March 1821: revolution
   Laibach 1821 authorises         Suppressed in the same
   AUSTRIA intervenes              Austrian operation
   Ferdinand I restored to         Constitutional movement
   absolute rule                   crushed
   INTERESTED POWER: Austria -     INTERESTED POWER: Austria -
   Naples is inside her Italian    Piedmont borders Lombardy
   sphere                          and is the barrier state

   CASE 3: SPAIN
   January 1820: Riego's revolt compels Ferdinand VII to accept
   the constitution of 1812
   Verona 1822 authorises; BRITAIN WITHDRAWS
   April 1823: a French army - "the Hundred Thousand Sons of
   Saint Louis" - crosses the Pyrenees
   Ferdinand VII restored to absolute rule; repression follows
   INTERESTED POWER: France - and note what this means:
   the defeated power of 1815 is now the settlement's
   ENFORCEMENT AGENT, eight years after Waterloo
   BUT: Britain blocks any extension across the Atlantic
```

*The pattern's fourth step is the analytical payload. In every case the intervening army belonged to the power with the strongest local interest in the outcome - which is why the "European" character of the authorisation is the part of the doctrine most open to challenge.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

It is easy to describe the interventions as repression and stop there, and an answer that does so will be accurate and shallow. The harder and more useful questions are: what exactly were the revolutionaries asking for; why did the powers regard those demands as dangerous rather than merely disagreeable; and what did the interventions actually accomplish?

The answer to the first question is narrower than students expect. The movements of 1820-21 were not republican and not, in the main, popular. They were led by army officers, professional men and sections of the nobility, and their demand was for a *written constitution* limiting the monarch - typically modelled on the Spanish constitution of 1812. They did not propose to depose their kings. They proposed to limit them.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that modesty is exactly what made them dangerous from the eastern powers' point of view, and the reason is worth stating because it is the hinge of the whole conservative case. A republican revolution can be dismissed as an aberration, an act of criminals and fanatics of the kind Europe had already defeated. A constitutionalist movement led by respectable men, demanding a limitation of monarchy by law, cannot be dismissed that way - and if it succeeded in one kingdom it supplied a model, a vocabulary and an argument to the same classes in every other. ⚠️ The powers intervened against the moderates precisely because the moderates were plausible.

### 2. Naples and Piedmont, 1820-21

✅ **Fact:** A revolution broke out in **Naples in July 1820** against **Ferdinand I of the Two Sicilies**, and another in **Piedmont in March 1821**. The **Congress of Laibach in 1821** authorised **Austrian intervention** in Naples and in Piedmont; Austria sent troops and suppressed the Neapolitan revolution. Britain refused to participate or to endorse the principle and was present only as an observer.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three features of the Italian interventions deserve attention.

**They were easy.** The constitutionalist movements had narrow social bases, no organised mass support, and armies of doubtful reliability. Against a great power's field army they had no prospect. ⚠️ This is worth stating because it establishes the real balance of forces in the 1820s and explains why the settlement survived that decade so comfortably - the challenge it faced was politically significant and militarily negligible.

**They were Austrian.** The authorisation was European; the army was Austrian; the outcome was the consolidation of Austrian predominance in Italy. Austria had been installed in Lombardy and Venetia in 1815 as a compensation and a supervisory presence, and the interventions converted that presence into an acknowledged right of policing the peninsula. ⚠️ For Italians this was the decisive experience: the settlement of 1815 had made Austria the master of northern Italy, and the interventions of 1821 demonstrated that Austrian arms would decide the internal politics of Italian states as well. It is from this, and not from the territorial arrangement alone, that the anti-Austrian character of Italian nationalism derives.

**They generated the next generation's politics.** Suppression of a constitutional movement does not destroy its personnel; it exiles, imprisons or radicalises them. ⚠️ The Italian movements of the following decades were more conspiratorial, more nationalist and less willing to settle for a charter from a king, and the experience of 1821 is part of the explanation. The political narrative that follows belongs to the topic on Italian unification; what this topic owns is the demonstration that the Vienna order could suppress a constitutional demand and could not remove it.

### 3. Spain, 1820-23: the intervention that broke the system

✅ **Fact:** In **January 1820** a liberal military uprising led by **Rafael del Riego** compelled **Ferdinand VII** to accept the **Constitution of 1812**. The **Congress of Verona in 1822** approved **French intervention**, and in **1823** a French army - known as the **Hundred Thousand Sons of Saint Louis** - entered Spain with the blessing of the conservative powers and restored Ferdinand VII's absolute authority.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Spanish case is the most consequential of the three for four distinct reasons, and an answer that uses only one of them has left marks on the table.

1. **The intervening power was France.** Eight years after Waterloo, the state whose containment the entire settlement had been designed around was carrying out an armed intervention on the settlement's behalf, with European authorisation. That is either the most striking vindication of the readmission policy of Lesson 6 or the most alarming demonstration of how completely the alliance's original purpose had dissolved - and the best answers say it is both.
2. **It provoked the British rupture.** Britain had dissented at Troppau and abstained at Laibach; at Verona she withdrew. Spain was where the doctrine touched British interests, because a France re-established in the peninsula reopened a strategic question Britain had fought a long war to close.
3. **It produced the limit that mattered.** ✅ Britain blocked the extension of intervention to Spanish America, and that refusal - reinforced from across the Atlantic within months - decided the fate of a hemisphere. Lesson 11 takes this up.
4. **It was the system's last collective act of this kind.** After 1823 no further congress authorised an intervention of this sort. The doctrine had been executed to its limit and had cost the system its unity.

### 4. What intervention achieved, and what it cost

| | ⚠️ Achieved | ⚠️ Cost |
|---|---|---|
| **In the target states** | Constitutions abolished; absolute monarchy restored in Naples and Spain; the constitutionalist movements dispersed; the local order stabilised for a decade or more | The moderate constitutional route was discredited by its own defeat, so the political initiative passed to more radical and more national movements |
| **For the intervening powers** | Austria's predominance in Italy confirmed as a right of policing; France's readmission proved by a service rendered to the settlement | Austria acquired the permanent obligation to garrison the consequences of her own doctrine; France acquired an expensive commitment of no benefit to her |
| **For the system** | Demonstrated that the powers could coordinate an armed response to a challenge to the settlement | Two of five members dissented; Britain withdrew from the congress; the congress system ceased to be usable; the Concert was left with the practice and without the machinery |
| **For the world outside Europe** | — | The attempt to extend the doctrine across the Atlantic failed, and the failure produced a standing limit on European intervention in the Americas |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the table supports the verdict that this topic's balanced answers require, and the verdict is worth stating in its sharpest form. **The interventions succeeded completely in the short term, at every point where they were attempted, and the system that carried them out was destroyed by the effort.** Both halves are true, and the combination is what makes the Concert's record genuinely difficult to score: it was effective and self-defeating in the same operations.

### 5. Comparison: intervention in 1821-23 against later doctrines

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the comparison is available and must be handled with discipline. The Troppau doctrine has been compared to later doctrines of intervention on ideological grounds, and the structural similarity is real: in each case a group of powers asserts a right to correct the internal politics of another state by force, and justifies it as the defence of a system rather than as an act of self-interest. The differences are, however, decisive and must accompany the comparison. The Troppau claim was made among a handful of monarchies with no written law to appeal to and no institution to authorise them; later claims are made in a world with a written prohibition on the use of force, a body empowered to authorise exceptions, and a general doctrine of sovereign equality. ⚠️ **The correct use of the comparison is to show that the problem is old and the framework is new**, and specifically that the nineteenth century's difficulty was the absence of any law under which an intervention could be judged illegal, which is the exact opposite of the modern difficulty. ❌ Do not present the Troppau doctrine as an early version of any specific modern doctrine, and do not import modern legal vocabulary into 1820.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** If the constitutionalist movements were militarily negligible and socially narrow, were the interventions not an overreaction - and does that not prove the powers were driven by ideological panic rather than by any real assessment of danger?

**Reply.** The objection mistakes the danger the powers were assessing. They were not assessing whether the Neapolitan constitutionalists could defeat an Austrian army; nobody thought they could. They were assessing whether a constitution granted under compulsion in one kingdom, and allowed to stand, would make the same demand irresistible in the next - and on that question their judgement was not obviously wrong. The evidence usually cited against them, the revolutions of 1830 and 1848, in fact cuts the other way: it shows that the demand did spread, that it did become irresistible in France in 1830 and across the continent in 1848, and that the powers' estimate of the contagion was accurate even if their remedy was ineffective. ⚠️ The correct criticism of the interventions is therefore not that the danger was imaginary but that the remedy addressed the symptom: suppressing a constitutional movement removes the movement and leaves the demand, and a demand that has been denied a legal route acquires an illegal one. That is a criticism of method and not of perception, and it is much harder to answer.

⚠️ **Residual.** Whether a different response - conceding limited constitutions in the smaller states while holding the frontier settlement firm - could have stabilised the order is a genuine counterfactual, taken up in Lesson 16.

### UPSC application

These three cases are the concrete evidence for the "repression" half of every balanced verdict on this topic, and they are far more persuasive than generalised statements about conservatism. An answer that names Naples and Piedmont in 1821 and Spain in 1823, attaches the authorising congress to each, identifies the intervening power, and notes Britain's position, has established the repression case with specificity in four lines and can then spend its remaining words on analysis.

**Probable framing.** "Examine the Concert of Europe as an instrument of intervention." "The Vienna system preserved peace among states by suppressing liberty within them. Discuss." "Assess the successes and failures of Metternich's system."

**Common traps.** Describing the 1820-21 movements as republican or popular revolutions - they were constitutionalist, led by officers and professional men. Attributing the Spanish intervention to Austria. Forgetting that Britain withdrew at Verona rather than merely dissenting. Omitting the limit: the failure to extend intervention across the Atlantic. Treating the interventions as failures - they succeeded, which is what makes the topic difficult.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Revolutions: **Spain, January 1820**, led by **Rafael del Riego**, restoring the **Constitution of 1812**; **Naples, July 1820**, against **Ferdinand I of the Two Sicilies**; **Portugal, August 1820**; **Piedmont, March 1821**.
2. ⚠️ The demand was a **written constitution limiting the monarch**, commonly on the Spanish 1812 model - not a republic.
3. ⚠️ Moderate, respectable constitutionalism was more dangerous to the eastern powers than republicanism, because it could not be dismissed as criminal fanaticism.
4. ✅ **Laibach 1821** authorised **Austrian intervention in Naples and Piedmont**; Austria suppressed the Neapolitan revolution; Britain observed only.
5. ✅ **Verona 1822** authorised **French intervention in Spain**; the **Hundred Thousand Sons of Saint Louis** entered Spain in **1823** and restored **Ferdinand VII** to absolute rule.
6. ⚠️ Every intervention was executed by the power with the strongest local interest - which undermines the claim that the action was European rather than national.
7. ⚠️ Italy: the interventions converted Austrian territorial predominance into an acknowledged right of policing, and made Italian nationalism anti-Austrian.
8. ⚠️ Spain: the defeated power of 1815 became the settlement's enforcement agent in 1823 - vindication of readmission and evidence of the alliance's lost purpose at once.
9. ✅ Britain **blocked the extension of intervention beyond Europe**, to Spanish America.
10. ⚠️ The verdict: the interventions succeeded completely and destroyed the system that carried them out.
11. ❌ Do not give troop numbers, casualty figures or the names of Italian constitutionalist leaders; none is held in this evidence base.

### Concept check

**Question:** The constitutionalist movements of 1820-21 were narrow in social base and militarily negligible. Explain why the eastern powers nevertheless treated them as a danger requiring armed intervention, and state the strongest criticism of the remedy they chose.

**Model answer:** The powers were not assessing the military capacity of the movements, which was plainly inadequate against a great power's field army, but their political character and their capacity to serve as a precedent. The demand of 1820-21 was not for a republic but for a written constitution limiting the monarch, usually on the model of the Spanish constitution of 1812, and it was advanced by army officers, professional men and sections of the nobility rather than by a revolutionary crowd. That respectability was precisely the threat. A republican rising could be represented as the criminal aberration Europe had already defeated and could be suppressed without conceding anything; a moderate constitutional demand made by the propertied and educated classes could not be represented that way, and if it succeeded in one kingdom it supplied a model, a vocabulary and a justification to the same classes everywhere else. For a multinational dynastic empire and an autocracy, a successful limitation of monarchy in Naples was therefore not a local event but the first instance of a general principle, and the Troppau Protocol of 19 November 1820 states exactly that reasoning when it treats a state altered by revolution as having left the European alliance. The strongest criticism of the remedy is not that the perception was wrong but that the method addressed the symptom. Suppressing a movement removes its personnel and leaves its demand in place; and a demand that has been shown to have no legal route to satisfaction acquires an illegal one, since the men who had tried to obtain a charter from a king by constitutional pressure concluded, reasonably, that nothing could be obtained from kings at all. The interventions therefore bought a decade of order in each target state at the price of converting a reformist opposition into a revolutionary and increasingly national one - which is why the next wave, in 1830 and 1848, was harder to contain and could not be settled by restoring a monarch to his throne.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that because the powers were correct about the contagion they were therefore justified in the intervention. The two questions are separate. Judging that a demand will spread is an assessment of fact and the evidence of 1830 and 1848 supports it; judging that the right response is to send an army across a frontier to abolish a constitution is a decision about method, and it can be wrong even where the assessment was right. Keeping the two apart is what allows an answer to be critical without being simplistic.

**Bridge to Lesson 11.** Three interventions, three successes. Yet in the same years the same system declined to intervene in the one place where the conservative case was legally strongest - the rebellious American colonies of the very Spanish king it had just restored - and then, a few years later, intervened on the side of rebels against a lawful sovereign in Greece. Those two cases are where the system's stated principle breaks, and they are the subject of the next lesson.

## Lesson 11 - Where the system broke its own rule: Spanish America and Greece

### Visual: the principle and its two failures

```text

  very king the powers restored by     Sultan, who is not a member of

        |                                    |

  intervene; restore the colonies      no assistance to rebels;
  to their lawful king                 Metternich takes exactly this
        |                              position
  WHAT HAPPENED:                             |
  BRITAIN BLOCKS IT at Verona;         WHAT HAPPENED:
  Canning cultivates the new           Britain, France and RUSSIA
  states as trading partners;          agree to intervene, 1827;

  warns Europe against                 the Ottoman-Egyptian fleet;

        |                              3 Feb 1830 recognises Greek
  RESULT: the principle is not         independence; Constantinople
  applied where a great power's        1832 fixes the borders
  interest forbids it                        |

                                       REVERSED where the balance,
                                       strategic interest and public

                          |
        THE CONCLUSION THE EVIDENCE FORCES:
        legitimacy governed the settlement's INTERNAL European
        business and did not govern its conduct where great-power
        interest, the Eastern question or non-European sovereigns
        were involved
```

*The two cases are the topic's decisive test, because they are the places where the system's stated principle and its members' interests pointed in opposite directions. In both, interest won - in one by inaction, in the other by action against the principle itself.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

By 1823 the conservative powers had a clean run of successes. Naples, Piedmont and Spain had all been restored to absolute monarchy by foreign armies acting under European authorisation. If the Troppau doctrine meant anything, the next case was obvious.

Ferdinand VII had just been restored to his full authority in Spain by a French army. That authority, in law, extended to his American possessions, where a long process of separation had removed most of them from Spanish control. The doctrine that entitled the powers to restore a king's authority over his Spanish subjects entitled them, on the same reasoning, to restore it over his American ones. Metternich's Europe had the principle; France had just demonstrated the willingness to supply an army.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** it did not happen, and the reason it did not happen is the most revealing single fact about the Vienna system. The obstacle was not a counter-principle. It was that one member of the system had a large and growing commercial interest in the independence of those colonies, possessed the navy that would have had to carry any expedition across the Atlantic, and refused.

### 2. Spanish America: the intervention that was not attempted

✅ **Fact:** Britain **blocked wider intervention in Spanish America at Verona**. The Concert was never ideologically uniform, and this was the point at which the divergence became a veto.

✅ **Fact:** In **December 1823** the **Monroe Doctrine**, articulated by the President of the United States, warned the European powers against intervention in the Americas, in support of the independence movements there.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three points must be kept straight here, and the third is the one that separates a good answer from a confused one.

**First, the ownership boundary.** The Latin American independence movements themselves - their causes, their leaders, their campaigns, their social composition and the character of the states they produced - belong to the topic that owns them. What this topic owns is the *European decision*: the proposal to extend the Troppau doctrine across the Atlantic and its defeat. Do not narrate the independence struggles here; supply the European half and hand over.

**Second, the mechanism of the veto.** Britain did not defeat the proposal by argument. She defeated it by the plain fact that no European expedition could reach the Americas against the opposition of the Royal Navy, and by making the commercial stake explicit: British trade with the new states was substantial and growing, and a restoration of Spanish monopoly would have destroyed it. ⚠️ The veto was therefore material rather than doctrinal, which is exactly why it held.

**Third, the relation between the British veto and the American declaration.** They are separate acts with a common effect, and the common effect is greater than either alone. The Monroe Doctrine by itself was a statement by a state with negligible capacity to enforce it against a European coalition. The British position by itself was the policy of a single European power that might change with a change of ministry. Together they produced a situation in which any European intervention in the Americas would face both the opposition of the strongest navy in the world and a declared American hostility. ⚠️ **The decisive point for an answer:** the Vienna order was a European system, and the first hard limit on it was drawn in the Atlantic by a combination of British interest and an extra-European power's declaration. ❌ Do not write that Britain acted to enforce the Monroe Doctrine, and ❌ do not write that the doctrine caused the British position; the two were independent and mutually reinforcing.

### 3. Greece: the system acting against its own principle

✅ **Fact:** The **Greek War of Independence ran from 1821 to 1832**. Greeks rebelled against Ottoman rule; the conflict drew in the great powers through a combination of strategic calculation and strong European sympathy for the Greek cause.

✅ **Fact:** In **1827** Britain, France and Russia agreed on intervention; at the **Battle of Navarino on 20 October 1827** their combined fleets destroyed the Ottoman-Egyptian fleet, tipping the balance decisively in favour of Greek independence. The **Treaty of Adrianople of 1829** ended the Russo-Turkish war of 1828-29, with the Ottoman Empire conceding Russian gains and further autonomy for Greece. The **London Protocol of 3 February 1830** recognised Greece as a fully independent and sovereign state, defining its borders and providing for a monarchy under the guarantee of the great powers. The **Treaty of Constantinople of 1832** completed the recognition and settled the kingdom's frontiers, with **Otto of Bavaria** installed as king. **George Canning** was central to shifting Britain away from the strict non-intervention favoured by Metternich's Austria and towards support for Greek autonomy.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** now measure that sequence against the Troppau doctrine. Subjects rose against their lawful sovereign. Three of the five great powers, including two who had signed the Troppau Protocol in 1820, sent fleets that destroyed that sovereign's navy. They then imposed a settlement detaching a province from his empire and erecting it into an independent kingdom, and chose its king. ⚠️ **Every element of the doctrine that had justified the restoration of Ferdinand I in Naples and Ferdinand VII in Spain is contradicted by what was done to the Ottoman Sultan between 1827 and 1832.**

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four reasons explain the reversal, and a complete answer gives all four rather than settling for the first.

1. **The sovereign was outside the club.** The Holy Alliance had excluded the Ottoman Sultan, and the principle of monarchical solidarity had always been understood, in practice, as solidarity among Christian dynasties. A non-Christian sovereign had no claim on the fraternity that protected the Bourbons.
2. **The Eastern question overrode the doctrine.** The decline of Ottoman power was the great structural problem of nineteenth-century European diplomacy, because the collapse or partition of that empire would transform the balance. Russia had a long-standing interest in advancing at Ottoman expense; Britain and Austria had a long-standing interest in preventing her from doing so unilaterally.
3. **Joint action was a method of restraining Russia.** ⚠️ This is the most important and least obvious point. If Russia acted alone on behalf of the Greeks, she would acquire the credit, the client state and the strategic gain. If Britain and France acted with her, the outcome would be settled collectively and Russian gains would be bounded. ⚠️ Intervening for the Greeks was therefore, for Britain, partly a means of intervening against Russia - which is an application of the balance of power in a case where the doctrine of legitimacy pointed the opposite way.
4. **Public opinion was engaged.** The Greek cause commanded extraordinary sympathy across educated Europe, and Britain's government was answerable to an assembly that reflected that sympathy. ⚠️ This is the first appearance in this topic of public opinion as an independent force in great-power decision-making, and it marks a real change: at Vienna in 1814-15 no negotiator had to consider it, and by 1827 a British foreign secretary did.

### 4. The two cases compared

| | ✅ Spanish America | ✅ Greece |
|---|---|---|
| The legitimate sovereign | Ferdinand VII of Spain, restored by the powers in 1823 | The Ottoman Sultan |
| What the doctrine required | Restoration of the colonies to Spain | No assistance to rebels; Metternich took this position |
| What the powers did | Nothing; Britain blocked the proposal at Verona | Britain, France and Russia intervened militarily in 1827 |
| Decisive factor | British commercial interest and naval capacity; reinforced by the Monroe Doctrine of December 1823 | The Eastern question, the restraint of Russia, and European opinion |
| Instrument recording the outcome | None - the outcome was an absence of action | **London Protocol, 3 February 1830**; **Treaty of Constantinople, 1832** |
| ⚠️ What it proves | The doctrine stopped where a great power's material interest opposed it | The doctrine was reversed where the balance and strategic interest required it |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the conclusion that follows is one of the most useful sentences available on this topic, and it should be stated carefully rather than as generalised cynicism. **Legitimacy governed the Vienna order's internal European business, where the powers' interests were broadly aligned, and ceased to govern it wherever a great power's material interest or the Eastern question cut across it.** That is a claim with evidence on both sides - Saxony's king really was protected by the principle, and the Ottoman Sultan really was not - and it is a much more defensible position than either "the settlement was principled" or "the settlement was pure hypocrisy".

### 5. The consequence for the system

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Greece did something to the Concert that the interventions of 1821-23 had not done. Those had divided the powers into two camps, but each camp had a consistent position. Greece scrambled the camps: Russia, a Troppau signatory, was the power most eager to act against a legitimate sovereign; Austria, the doctrine's author, was isolated; Britain, which had refused to act against constitutions, acted for a national insurrection. ⚠️ After 1830 no power could plausibly claim that the Concert stood for a principle, because each of them had by then acted against whichever principle it had professed. What remained was the practice - consultation, conference, joint guarantee - and it is precisely the practice that survived. The Greek settlement is therefore a double lesson: the end of the Concert as an ideology and a demonstration of the Concert working as a method, since a dangerous crisis on the Eastern question was closed by collective negotiation and a jointly guaranteed settlement rather than by a general war.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and there is a boundary to observe. The Eastern question runs from here through the rest of the century and belongs, in its later phases, to the topics that own the road to 1914. This topic owns the Greek case as the point at which the Concert's stated principle broke; it does not narrate the Eastern question beyond it.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** If the powers abandoned the legitimacy principle whenever it was inconvenient, was it a principle at all? Why not conclude simply that the Vienna order was great-power interest with a moral vocabulary attached?

**Reply.** Because that conclusion cannot account for the cases where the principle bound the powers against their preferences. Prussia wanted the whole of Saxony and was refused on legitimist grounds; the King of Saxony kept his throne and part of his kingdom, and no balance-of-power calculation required that particular outcome. France, a defeated aggressor, was restored to her dynasty and her great-power status rather than partitioned, at a moment when the victors had the capacity to do otherwise. Austrian, Prussian and Russian governments organised their domestic and foreign policy for a generation around a doctrine that cost them money and troops and produced no territorial gain. ⚠️ The accurate formulation is therefore that legitimacy operated as a genuine constraint within the European circle and lapsed at the circle's edge - where the sovereign was not a member of the club, where an extra-European interest was engaged, or where the Eastern question raised a balance-of-power stake that overrode it. A principle with a boundary is not the same thing as a pretext, and the analytical task is to locate the boundary rather than to deny the principle.

⚠️ **Residual.** The relative weight of the four explanations for the Greek reversal - the Sultan's exclusion, the Eastern question, the restraint of Russia and public opinion - cannot be fixed from the evidence held here, and this session does not rank them.

### UPSC application

These two cases are where a good answer separates itself from a competent one. Most candidates can state that the Concert suppressed revolutions; few can state that it declined to suppress one in the Americas and actively assisted one in Greece, and fewer still can explain the difference. The cases also supply the most efficient route to a graded verdict: a system that applied its principle at home and abandoned it at the edges is neither the principled order its defenders claimed nor the pure hypocrisy its critics alleged, and saying so with these two cases as evidence is a complete conclusion.

**Probable framing.** "The Concert of Europe defended legitimacy only where it suited the great powers. Examine." "Assess the role of the great powers in the Greek War of Independence." "Discuss the limits of the Vienna system beyond Europe."

**Common traps.** Narrating the Latin American independence movements, which belong to another owner. Writing that Britain enforced the Monroe Doctrine, or that the doctrine caused the British position. Treating Greece as a straightforward humanitarian intervention and omitting the balance-of-power calculation. Forgetting that Russia, a Troppau signatory, was the power most eager to act against a legitimate sovereign. Dating Greek independence to Navarino rather than to the instruments of 1830 and 1832.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Britain blocked the extension of intervention to Spanish America at Verona**; the Concert was never ideologically uniform.
2. ✅ **Monroe Doctrine, December 1823** - warns the European powers against intervention in the Americas.
3. ⚠️ The British veto and the American declaration were **independent acts with a common effect**; neither caused the other.
4. ⚠️ The Latin American independence movements themselves belong to their own topic; this topic owns the European decision not to intervene.
5. ✅ **Greek War of Independence, 1821-1832**.
6. ✅ **1827:** Britain, France and Russia agree on intervention; **Navarino, 20 October 1827** destroys the Ottoman-Egyptian fleet.
7. ✅ **Treaty of Adrianople, 1829**; **London Protocol, 3 February 1830** recognises Greek independence and provides for a monarchy under great-power guarantee; **Treaty of Constantinople, 1832** fixes the borders; **Otto of Bavaria** becomes king.
8. ✅ **Canning** moved Britain away from the strict non-intervention favoured by Metternich's Austria.
9. ⚠️ Four reasons for the reversal: the Sultan was outside the Christian monarchical club; the Eastern question overrode the doctrine; joint action bounded Russian gains; European public opinion was engaged.
10. ⚠️ Public opinion enters great-power calculation here for the first time in this topic - absent in 1814-15, decisive by 1827.
11. ⚠️ The conclusion: legitimacy bound the powers **within** the European circle and lapsed **at its edge**.
12. ⚠️ Greece ends the Concert as an ideology and demonstrates it working as a method.

### Concept check

**Question:** In 1823 the powers restored Ferdinand VII of Spain to absolute authority by armed intervention; within a decade three of them had helped Greek rebels detach a province from their lawful sovereign and had chosen the new state's king. Explain how both actions could be taken by the same system, and state what the pair establishes about the principle of legitimacy.

**Model answer:** The two actions are compatible because the principle of legitimacy operated inside a boundary, and the Greek case lay outside it in three respects. First, the sovereign concerned was the Ottoman Sultan, whom the Holy Alliance had excluded and who stood outside the community of Christian dynasties within which monarchical solidarity was actually practised, so the fraternity that protected the Bourbons of Spain and Naples did not extend to him. Second, the case raised the Eastern question, the structural problem of a declining Ottoman power whose collapse or partition would alter the European balance, and on that question the powers' balance-of-power interests were engaged in a way that the internal constitution of Naples never was. Third, joint intervention was itself a method of restraint: if Russia acted alone on the Greeks' behalf she would take the credit, the client and the strategic gain, whereas acting with her allowed Britain and France to bound the outcome, which is why two powers that had refused to act against constitutions in Italy and Spain were willing to act against a sovereign in the Aegean. Strong European sympathy for the Greek cause supplied the political permission, and Canning's Britain moved deliberately away from the strict non-intervention that Metternich's Austria still urged. What the pair establishes is that legitimacy was a genuine constraint with a limited jurisdiction rather than either a governing rule or a pretext: it really did protect the King of Saxony against Prussia at Vienna and really did shape the treatment of France, but it ceased to operate wherever the sovereign was not a member of the European monarchical circle, wherever an extra-European commercial interest was at stake, as in Spanish America, and wherever the balance of power cut across it, as in the Eastern question. The analytical task is therefore to locate the boundary of the principle, not to assert or deny the principle as a whole.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Greek intervention as an early humanitarian intervention and stopping there. Sympathy was real and was politically necessary, especially in Britain, but it does not explain why Russia was the most eager party, why Austria - the power most committed to the legitimist doctrine - was isolated, or why the outcome took the form of a jointly guaranteed monarchy with a foreign prince. The balance-of-power calculation explains all three, and an answer that omits it has described the motive and missed the mechanism.

**Bridge to Lesson 12.** Britain has now appeared four times as the exception: refusing the Troppau doctrine, abstaining at Laibach, withdrawing at Verona, and acting in Greece on grounds none of the others could use. That recurrence is not a series of separate decisions. It follows from something structural about the British position, and the next lesson sets it out - because a question on why the Concert failed is very often, in substance, a question about why one of its five members was never inside it.

## Lesson 12 - Britain in the Concert and never of it

### Visual: four structural sources of one policy
