*Read the diagram as four independent pressures converging on one policy. That convergence is the reason the British position was stable across changes of minister: it did not depend on who held the seals.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

Britain is the puzzle of this topic. She was one of the four architects of the settlement, a signatory of Chaumont and of the Quadruple Alliance, the author of the clause under which the congresses met, and the power that did most to secure a moderate peace for France. She was also the power that refused the Holy Alliance, refused the Troppau Protocol, abstained at Laibach, withdrew from Verona, and then blocked the doctrine's extension across the Atlantic.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the tempting explanation is that Britain was "the liberal power" and the others were reactionary. That explanation is wrong in a way worth dwelling on, because getting it wrong produces a whole answer that misses the mechanism. The British government of 1815-22 was not liberal in its domestic conduct; it faced serious radical agitation and responded with repression. Its refusal of the Troppau doctrine was not sympathy for Neapolitan constitutionalists. The divergence has structural sources, and the four of them are independent of any minister's opinions.

### 2. The four sources

**(a) The constitution.** ⚠️ A British ministry could not bind itself to an indefinite commitment to intervene abroad because it could not guarantee the consent of Parliament for the money and the forces such a commitment would require, and because the commitment would have to be honoured by future ministries with different majorities. ⚠️ More fundamentally, a doctrine declaring that a constitution extorted from a monarch is a revolutionary act which entitles foreign powers to intervene was, applied to Britain's own history, a condemnation of the British constitution itself. No British government could subscribe to that in a public instrument.

**(b) Geography.** ⚠️ Britain could not be invaded so long as she held command of the sea, and she held it. The consequence for policy is exact: Britain's requirement from Europe was not protection but equilibrium - a continent in which no single power could concentrate enough force to threaten the sea approaches or to close the continent to British trade. ⚠️ An equilibrium can be maintained by intervening occasionally and decisively; it does not require a permanent directorate policing the internal politics of every state, and the second is far more expensive than the first.

**(c) Commerce.** ⚠️ Britain's prosperity depended on access to markets, and market access is indifferent to the internal constitution of the customer. A restored Spanish colonial monopoly in America would have closed a large and growing market; independent American states would not. ⚠️ Where the legitimist doctrine required Britain to help restore a closed trading system, the doctrine lost - and this is the reason the Spanish American case, rather than any European one, produced the open British veto.

**(d) Empire.** ⚠️ Britain's field of expansion lay outside Europe. What she needed from Europe was quiet, obtained as cheaply as possible, so that resources could be directed elsewhere. ⚠️ A settlement that stabilised the continent was therefore of great value to her - which is why Britain remained inside the Concert - while an ideological programme that would commit British troops to continental policing was of negative value, which is why she refused the programme.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** Britain wanted the Vienna settlement to succeed and wanted no part of the politics by which the other powers proposed to make it succeed. That is not inconsistency; it is the difference between valuing a territorial equilibrium and subscribing to a theory of monarchical authority.

### 3. Castlereagh and Canning: continuity and change

| | ✅ Castlereagh (Foreign Secretary 1812-1822) | ✅ Canning (from 1822) |
|---|---|---|
| Attitude to the alliance | Valued it; worked to keep Britain inside its framework | Treated the divergence as a fact and acted on it |
| Attitude to intervention | Refused the doctrine; **State Paper, 5 May 1820** | Same refusal, applied more aggressively |
| Method | Dissent from within; attendance, abstention, argument | Separation; **withdrawal from Verona**; independent action |
| Spanish America | Resisted extension of the doctrine | Actively cultivated the new states; blocked intervention |
| Greece | — | ✅ Moved Britain away from the strict non-intervention favoured by Metternich's Austria |
| ⚠️ Continuity | The substance of the position is identical | Only its management differs |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the distinction matters because candidates routinely explain the collapse of the congress system by Canning's arrival, which inverts the causation. The position Canning acted on had been set out in full by his predecessor in May 1820, two years before Canning took office and six months before Troppau. ⚠️ What changed in 1822 was the judgement about whether the divergence should be managed inside the alliance or acknowledged outside it. Castlereagh's method preserved the appearance of a five-power system; Canning's method ended the appearance and left the reality visible. ⚠️ The reality was the same in both cases.

### 4. What Britain contributed, which the "obstruction" reading misses

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** an answer that presents Britain purely as the spoiler of the Concert has taken half the evidence. Four positive British contributions should be recorded.

1. **The moderate peace with France.** British policy worked consistently towards a settlement that France could accept, and the readmission of 1818 is in part a British achievement. ⚠️ The power with the least to fear from France argued most consistently for treating her as a future partner.
2. **The alliance's own machinery.** Article VI, which created the congresses, was part of an instrument Britain co-authored. The system Britain later refused to let become an interventionist directorate was one she had helped design as a consultative one.
3. **The Belgian settlement of 1830.** ✅ The Concert accepted Belgian independence in 1830, and the arrangement was worked out in a conference in which Britain was central. ⚠️ Here Britain is not obstructing the Concert but using it, which is the clearest demonstration that her objection was to the doctrine and not to the practice.
4. **The limit on the system's reach.** ⚠️ The British veto on intervention in Spanish America is usually scored against the Concert. It can equally be scored as a contribution to the settlement's survival: a transatlantic expedition to restore Spanish colonial rule would have been a vast and probably unsuccessful undertaking that would have divided the powers far more violently than the refusal did.

### 5. The consequence for the system

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the presence of a permanently dissenting great power inside the Concert had a specific and double effect.

**It prevented the Concert from becoming a monarchical directorate.** With Britain outside the doctrine, no intervention could be represented as the united will of Europe, and the powers could not construct a standing enforcement machinery. ⚠️ This is the origin of the Concert's most attractive feature and its most serious weakness at once: it could not become an instrument of general repression, and it could not become an instrument of anything else either.

**It gave every challenger a potential patron.** ⚠️ A state or movement resisting the conservative order could look to Britain - not for reliable support, since Britain acted on her own calculation, but for the possibility of support, which was enough to change the odds. The Greeks received it; the Belgians received it; the Poles, in 1830-31, did not. ⚠️ The unreliability is the point: British support was available where British interest or opinion pointed, and its availability shaped the behaviour of everyone else in the system.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** Was Britain not simply a free rider? She enjoyed the stability the eastern powers paid for in troops and money, refused to share the burden, and took the commercial profits of the peace while criticising the means by which it was maintained.

**Reply.** The charge is serious and has a real basis, and an answer gains by conceding it precisely. Britain did benefit from continental stability without contributing to the policing that partly produced it, and she did profit commercially from arrangements she declined to defend. Three qualifications, however, limit the charge. First, Britain had already paid disproportionately for the outcome: she had financed the coalitions and fought the war at sea and in the peninsula for the better part of two decades, and the settlement she was accused of enjoying was substantially her own creation. Second, the burden the eastern powers bore was incurred for a purpose Britain had declined to adopt and had said so in advance; a state is not a free rider on a policy it publicly refused. Third, Britain did carry burdens of the kind she accepted: she was central to the Belgian settlement, and she committed a fleet in the Greek case. ⚠️ The accurate formulation is that Britain paid for the parts of the system she valued - the territorial equilibrium and the management of crises - and declined to pay for the part she rejected, which was the suppression of constitutional government.

⚠️ **Residual.** Whether a Britain that had stayed formally inside the interventionist framework could have moderated it from within, as Castlereagh appears to have hoped, is a counterfactual and is left open here.

### UPSC application

Britain's position is the most reliable route to a graded verdict on the Concert, and graded verdicts are what the directive words on this topic almost always demand. A candidate who can say that the Concert contained, from its first day, one member whose form of government made the system's declared purpose unacceptable to it has explained the failure structurally, which is worth considerably more than a narrative of congresses.

**Probable framing.** "Account for Britain's divergence from the other powers of the Concert of Europe." "The Concert of Europe failed because it was never united. Examine." "Assess the contribution of British policy to the post-Napoleonic order."

**Common traps.** Describing Britain as the liberal power - the same government was repressing radical agitation at home. Attributing the divergence to Canning. Presenting Britain purely as an obstacle, and omitting the moderate peace with France, Article VI, the Belgian settlement and the naval commitment for Greece. Saying Britain left the Concert - she withdrew from a congress and from a doctrine, and remained central to great-power consultation for decades.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ Four structural sources of British divergence: **constitution, geography, commerce, empire** - all independent of any minister's opinions.
2. ⚠️ The constitutional source is decisive: a doctrine condemning constitutions extorted from monarchs condemned Britain's own constitutional history.
3. ⚠️ Britain needed **equilibrium**, not a directorate; equilibrium is maintained by occasional decisive action, not permanent policing.
4. ⚠️ Commerce: independent American states were customers; a restored Spanish monopoly was a closed market.
5. ✅ **Castlereagh, Foreign Secretary 1812-1822**, set the position in the **State Paper of 5 May 1820**; **Canning** applied it more aggressively from 1822.
6. ⚠️ The **substance** was continuous; only the management changed - dissent from within, then acknowledged separation.
7. ⚠️ Four British contributions: the moderate peace with France; Article VI and the consultative machinery; the **Belgian settlement of 1830**; the limit on the system's transatlantic reach.
8. ⚠️ Effect on the system: the Concert could not become a monarchical directorate, and could not become anything else either.
9. ⚠️ Every challenger had a potential patron - the Greeks and Belgians benefited, the Poles of 1830-31 did not.
10. ❌ Do not call Britain "the liberal power" or date the divergence to Canning.

### Concept check

**Question:** Britain refused the Holy Alliance, refused the Troppau Protocol, abstained at Laibach and withdrew from Verona, yet she was a founder of the Quadruple Alliance, the co-author of the clause that created the congresses, and central to the Belgian settlement of 1830. State the single distinction that makes these facts consistent, and identify the structural source that made the distinction unavoidable.

**Model answer:** The distinction is between the territorial settlement and its consultative practice on one side, and the doctrine of monarchical authority and armed intervention against revolution on the other. Britain valued the first and refused the second, and every item in the list falls cleanly on one side or the other: she co-authored the Quadruple Alliance of 20 November 1815 and its Article VI because a periodic consultation among the powers served an equilibrium she wanted; she was central to the Belgian conference of 1830 because a threatened breach of the territorial settlement was exactly the kind of question the practice existed to resolve; and she refused the Holy Alliance, the Troppau Protocol, the Laibach authorisation and the Verona decision because each of them asserted a right to determine the internal constitution of third states. The structural source that made the distinction unavoidable is Britain's own form of government. A ministry answerable to an elected assembly could not bind itself, or its successors, to an indefinite commitment of money and troops for the policing of other countries, because it could not guarantee the consent required; and, more fundamentally, a doctrine holding that a constitution extorted from a monarch is a revolutionary act justifying foreign intervention was a direct condemnation of the process by which the British constitution had itself been formed. Three further pressures pointed the same way - an island position that required equilibrium rather than protection, a commercial interest indifferent to the internal constitution of its customers, and an imperial field of expansion outside Europe that made a cheaply quiet continent the object of policy. Because all four pressures were structural rather than personal, the position survived the change of foreign secretary in 1822: Castlereagh had already stated it in the State Paper of 5 May 1820, and Canning altered only its management, from dissent inside the alliance to acknowledged separation outside it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining Britain's conduct by liberal sympathy. The ministry that issued the State Paper was simultaneously suppressing radical agitation at home, and its objection was jurisdictional and constitutional rather than ideological. Reading it as liberalism also makes the Greek intervention inexplicable, since there Britain acted with autocratic Russia against a lawful sovereign, for reasons of balance, strategy and public opinion rather than of principle.

**Bridge to Lesson 13.** So far the system has been examined from the outside - treaties, congresses, interventions, the relations of five states. But the Vienna order had an inside, and for most Europeans it was the only part they experienced: censorship, surveillance, control of universities and the policing of opinion. That order is the subject of the next lesson, and it is where the phrase "the Age of Metternich" earns its meaning.

## Lesson 13 - The order inside the states: Carlsbad, censorship and the Age of Metternich

### Visual: the machinery, from the trigger to the instrument

```text

   THE FRAMING: a single crime is treated as evidence of an

   student associations
                    |
   THE INSTRUMENT: CARLSBAD DECREES, adopted by the Federal
   Diet, 20 September 1819
                    |
   +------------+-------------+-------------+--------------+
   |            |             |             |              |
 PRESS       UNIVERSITY    STUDENT       CENTRAL         THE EFFECT
 CENSORSHIP  CURATORS      SOCIETIES     INVESTIGATION   ON POLITICS
   |            |          DISSOLVED     COMMISSION        |
 publications  a government  the          at Mainz,      the ONLY
 supervised;   agent placed  Burschen-    to investigate  places where
 the printed   in each       schaften     revolutionary   liberal and
 discussion    university    are banned   activity across national
 of politics   to watch                   the whole       opinion could
 is closed     teaching and               Confederation   form are
               students                                   closed at once
                    |
   THE COMPLETION: VIENNA FINAL ACT of the German
   Confederation, 15 May 1820 - completes the federal
   constitution, reinforces the monarchical order and
   permits FEDERAL INTERVENTION against revolutionary
   activity inside a member state
                    |
   CAUTION: this 1820 "Final Act" is the Confederation's
   constitutional document. It is NOT the Final Act of the
   Congress of Vienna of 9 June 1815. Two different
   instruments, five years apart.
```

*The diagram's four branches are the reason the Carlsbad system is worth teaching in detail. It did not attack a party or a conspiracy; it attacked the institutions in which political opinion is formed - the press, the university, the student association - and added a permanent investigating body operating across state frontiers. That is a distinctive object, and naming it is what converts "repression" from an adjective into an analysis.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

The Vienna settlement had a foreign policy, examined in the last six lessons. It also had a domestic policy, and for most Europeans the domestic policy was the settlement. A peasant in Lombardy or a student in Jena did not experience the balance of power; he experienced a censor, a police report, a dissolved society and a curriculum under supervision.

The domestic policy had a specific target, and identifying it correctly is the whole of this lesson. It was not aimed at the poor, who were not politically organised; it was not aimed principally at the old revolutionary republicanism, which had few adherents; and it was not, in the main, a response to violence. It was aimed at the *formation of opinion among the educated* - at the press, the universities and the student associations, because those were the places where liberal and national ideas were generated, circulated and turned into a political programme.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that targeting tells you exactly what the rulers of 1815-48 believed. They believed that the revolutionary danger was a *doctrine* rather than a class, and that a doctrine can be prevented from spreading by controlling its channels. The belief was reasonable in the short run and wrong in the long run, and the reasons for both are worth setting out, because they supply the most searching criticism of the Vienna order that can be made from within its own assumptions.

### 2. The Carlsbad Decrees, 20 September 1819

✅ **Fact:** The **Carlsbad Decrees** were issued in **September 1819** - adopted on **20 September 1819** - following the assassination of the writer **August von Kotzebue** on **23 March 1819** by **Karl Sand**, a student. They imposed strict **press censorship**, appointed government agents as **curators in the universities**, **dissolved the student associations** known as the **Burschenschaften**, and established a **Central Investigation Commission at Mainz** to monitor and suppress revolutionary activity across the German Confederation. They were the work of the Confederation, heavily influenced by Metternich, and aimed at **liberal and nationalist tendencies in the German states**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four analytical points follow, and each is usable.

1. **The occasion was disproportionate to the response.** A single murder by a single student produced a system of surveillance covering the press, the universities and the student bodies of thirty-nine states. ⚠️ That disproportion is evidence about the rulers' state of mind: the crime was not the cause but the opportunity, and the machinery had been wanted before the occasion arose.
2. **The instrument was federal, not national.** The decrees were adopted by the Confederation and applied across its member states. ⚠️ The German Confederation, created in 1815 as a loose association for mutual defence and internal order, was thereby converted into an instrument for the coordinated policing of opinion - which is the strongest possible illustration of the argument in Lesson 4 that the Confederation was designed to make united German action impossible except in the one direction its sovereigns wanted.
3. **The target was institutional.** Press, university, student society - three institutions rather than a set of individuals. ⚠️ A state that arrests conspirators is responding to a plot; a state that places a curator in every university is responding to a *milieu*.
4. **The commission was transnational.** An investigating body at Mainz, examining revolutionary activity across the whole Confederation, is a police institution operating above the member states. ⚠️ It is a striking fact about this order that the one genuinely supranational executive institution the German Confederation acquired was an investigating commission.

### 3. The Vienna Final Act of 1820, and a trap worth naming

✅ **Fact:** The **Vienna Final Act** (*Wiener Schlussakte*) of **15 May 1820** completed and supplemented the federal constitution of the German Confederation, reinforcing its conservative monarchical order and permitting **federal intervention against revolutionary activity** inside member states.

> 🔑 **Trap:** there are **two** documents called a Final Act in this topic, five years apart. The **Final Act of the Congress of Vienna, 9 June 1815**, is the general treaty consolidating the European settlement. The **Vienna Final Act of 15 May 1820** is the German Confederation's own constitutional instrument. Confusing them produces an error that looks like carelessness and is in fact a substantive mistake about what was decided when and by whom.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the 1820 instrument matters because it closes the circle. Carlsbad supplied the machinery of surveillance; the Vienna Final Act supplied the constitutional authority for the Confederation to intervene inside a member state against revolutionary activity. ⚠️ Taken together, they replicate at the German level exactly the doctrine the Troppau Protocol asserted at the European level - that internal constitutional change is a matter for collective action by the other members. The domestic and the international halves of the Vienna order are therefore not merely consistent; they are the same doctrine applied at two scales.

### 4. "The Age of Metternich": what the phrase names

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the period from 1815 to 1848 is habitually called the Age of Metternich, and the label needs both endorsement and qualification.

**What justifies it.** ✅ Metternich was the dominant statesman at Vienna; ✅ he shaped Germany and Italy through influence and intervention; ✅ the repression tools of the period - censorship, university control, investigation - bear his imprint; ✅ nationalism was treated as a police problem. Austria was the geographical and institutional centre of the conservative order: president of the German Confederation, direct ruler of Lombardy and Venetia, the intervening power in Naples and Piedmont, and the author of the Troppau doctrine. One man held the foreign ministry of that state from 1809 and its chancellorship from 1821 until 1848.

**What qualifies it.** ✅ Metternich did not control all Europe; he was dominant in central Europe, but ⚠️ Britain and Russia had their own priorities and pursued them - Britain against the doctrine, Russia in the Eastern question and eventually against the Sultan in Greece. ⚠️ Nor was the system his invention alone: the settlement was a bargain among five, and the domestic order rested on the willingness of thirty-nine German sovereigns, and of rulers in Italy and beyond, to use the machinery for their own reasons.

⚠️ **Analytical inference: the formulation to use.** The Age of Metternich names a *system* of which Metternich was the most articulate exponent and Austria the necessary centre, not a personal dominion. The label is accurate as a description of who spoke for the order and where its weight lay; it is inaccurate if it implies a single controlling will.

### 5. Why the system worked for a generation - and why it failed

| ⚠️ Why it worked, 1819-1848 | ⚠️ Why it could not hold |
|---|---|
| The opposition was small, educated and legal-minded - the very groups most exposed to censorship and university control | Suppression removed the moderates' legal route, so the initiative passed to conspiratorial and later to mass movements |
| It had a federal instrument and a transnational commission - coordination across thirty-nine states | It could police publication and association; it could not police the economy, the railway, the market or the migration of people and ideas |
| The great powers backed it, and Britain, though dissenting, would not act against it inside Germany | It depended on the alliance of the three eastern powers, which the Greek crisis showed to be less solid than it looked |
| It coincided with two decades of general exhaustion after a generation of war | Exhaustion is temporary, and the generation that had not fought the wars reached adulthood in the 1830s and 1840s |
| It denied nationalism a political outlet | Denied a political outlet, national feeling migrated into literature, scholarship, philology and history - where it could not be policed and where it grew |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the last row is the deepest criticism of the Carlsbad system and the one most worth writing. The machinery was designed on the assumption that a doctrine spreads through identifiable channels and can be stopped by closing them. What it failed to anticipate is that a national idea denied a political channel does not disappear; it relocates into cultural forms that a censorship aimed at political publication is poorly equipped to reach - dictionaries and grammars, collections of folk tales, histories of the medieval past, philological scholarship, music. ⚠️ **The Vienna order suppressed nationalism as a politics and thereby helped to establish it as a culture, which is a far more durable thing to be.** When the political opportunity came in 1848, the national movements were not starting from nothing; they had spent thirty years acquiring a literature.

### 6. Comparison: the international and domestic orders as one doctrine

| Level | ✅ Instrument | ✅ Claim asserted | ⚠️ Who dissented |
|---|---|---|---|
| **European** | Troppau Protocol, 19 November 1820 | A state altered by revolution leaves the alliance and may be restored by force | Britain and France refused |
| **German** | Carlsbad Decrees, 20 September 1819; Vienna Final Act, 15 May 1820 | The Confederation may supervise opinion in member states and intervene against revolutionary activity | Effective dissent was impossible; the dissenting constituency had no institutional voice |
| ⚠️ The relation | The same doctrine at two scales, and the German version came **first** | | ⚠️ Where a great power could refuse, the doctrine was limited; where no such power existed, it was complete |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the chronology in that table is worth a sentence in an answer. Carlsbad precedes Troppau by fourteen months. The doctrine of collective intervention against internal constitutional change was therefore developed and applied inside Germany before it was proposed for Europe - which supports the reading that the European doctrine of 1820 was the generalisation of an Austrian and German practice rather than an improvised response to the Neapolitan revolution.

### 7. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** Is it not anachronistic to judge the Carlsbad system by later standards? Censorship was normal in most European states in 1819, including in states not usually described as reactionary, and the period saw no mass imprisonment or mass killing. Calling this "repression" imports a vocabulary from a different century.

**Reply.** The objection identifies a real distinction and fails to save the system. It is true that the Carlsbad order was not violent by the standards of either the preceding or the following century; its instruments were the censor, the curator and the police report rather than the firing squad, and the number of people directly coerced was small. But the criticism does not depend on the scale of the coercion. It depends on two points that the objection leaves untouched. The first is the *object*: the machinery was directed at the institutions through which political opinion is formed, and its declared purpose was to prevent a body of opinion from becoming a politics at all - which is a different and more ambitious thing than punishing sedition. The second is the *contemporary* nature of the criticism: the men who objected to it were not later historians but Germans of the time, and the settlement's own architects knew exactly what they were preventing. ⚠️ A judgement made from within the period, by people the period itself produced, is not anachronism.

⚠️ **Residual.** The practical effectiveness of the machinery - how completely censorship was enforced, how far the Mainz commission's work reached, how many careers it ended - cannot be quantified from the evidence held here, and no figure is asserted anywhere in this session.

### UPSC application

This lesson supplies the evidence for the "repression" verdict in its most specific and least rhetorical form, and specificity is what distinguishes a good answer here. Naming the Carlsbad Decrees with their date, their four instruments and their target, and adding the Confederation's Vienna Final Act of 1820, converts a general claim about conservatism into a documented one. The lesson also supplies the topic's best paradox for a conclusion: a system that suppressed national politics and thereby fostered national culture.

**Probable framing.** "The Vienna order maintained peace between states and repression within them. Examine." "Assess the Age of Metternich." "How far did the conservative order of 1815-48 succeed in containing liberalism and nationalism?"

**Common traps.** Confusing the two Final Acts. Dating the Carlsbad Decrees to 1815 or to the congress. Attributing them to the Congress of Vienna rather than to the German Confederation. Treating Metternich as the ruler of Europe. Asserting the numbers affected - none is available. Describing the decrees as aimed at the working class, when the target was the press, the universities and the student associations.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ **Carlsbad Decrees, 20 September 1819**, following the assassination of **August von Kotzebue** on **23 March 1819** by the student **Karl Sand**: **press censorship**, **university curators**, **dissolution of the Burschenschaften**, **Central Investigation Commission at Mainz**.
2. ✅ Their target: **liberal and nationalist tendencies in the German states**; ⚠️ nationalism treated as a police problem.
3. ⚠️ The target was institutional - press, university, student society - not a conspiracy or a class.
4. ✅ **Vienna Final Act of the German Confederation, 15 May 1820** - completes the federal constitution and permits **federal intervention against revolutionary activity**.
5. > 🔑 Two Final Acts: **Congress of Vienna, 9 June 1815** (European settlement) and **German Confederation, 15 May 1820** (federal constitution). Do not confuse them.
6. ⚠️ Carlsbad (1819) precedes Troppau (1820): the German practice was generalised into a European doctrine, not the reverse.
7. ⚠️ The Age of Metternich names a system with Austria at its centre, not a personal dominion; ✅ Britain and Russia pursued their own priorities.
8. ⚠️ Why it worked: a small, legal-minded opposition; federal coordination; great-power backing; post-war exhaustion.
9. ⚠️ Why it failed: it removed the moderate legal route; it could not police economic and social change; it rested on an eastern solidarity that Greece strained; exhaustion passed with the generation.
10. ⚠️ **The central paradox:** by denying nationalism a political outlet, the system pushed it into literature, scholarship and history - where it could not be policed and where it grew.
11. ❌ Do not assert numbers of people censored, investigated or imprisoned; none is held in this evidence base.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Carlsbad machinery was aimed at the press, the universities and the student associations rather than at conspirators or at the poor. Explain what that choice of target reveals about the rulers' theory of revolution, and identify the specific way in which the theory was mistaken.

**Model answer:** The choice of target reveals a theory in which revolution is the political expression of a doctrine rather than of a social condition. On that theory, revolutions occur because a set of ideas - constitutional limitation of monarchy, the nation as the basis of the state - is generated, circulated and organised among the educated, and the way to prevent revolution is therefore to close the channels of generation and circulation. Hence the four instruments adopted on 20 September 1819: censorship of the press, which closes publication; government curators in the universities, which supervises teaching and students where the ideas are formed; dissolution of the Burschenschaften, which removes the organisations through which students could act collectively; and a Central Investigation Commission at Mainz, which tracks the residue across the frontiers of thirty-nine states. The theory was not absurd, and in the short run it worked: the opposition of the 1820s was small, educated and legal-minded, and it was precisely such a body that censorship and university control could reach. The specific mistake lay in the assumption that ideas denied a political channel cease to develop. National feeling denied a political outlet migrated into forms that a censorship aimed at political publication was poorly equipped to reach - the collection of folk tales, the writing of dictionaries and grammars, philological and historical scholarship on the medieval past, music and the popular literature of a common language. Those activities were not seditious on their face, could not easily be prohibited, and had the effect of equipping a national idea with a literature, a past and a claim to cultural authenticity. The system therefore suppressed nationalism as a politics and helped to establish it as a culture, so that when the political opportunity arrived in 1848 the national movements were not beginning from nothing but drawing on thirty years of accumulated cultural capital.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that because the Carlsbad system eventually failed it was ineffective at the time. It was highly effective for roughly a generation, which is why the challenges of the 1820s were suppressed with such ease and why the order survived to be shaken only in 1830 and broken in 1848. The criticism that matters is not that the machinery failed to work but that what it did successfully - closing the legal and political route - changed the character of the opposition rather than removing it.

**Bridge to Lesson 14.** The order has now been described from outside and inside. What remains is to watch it being tested. Three waves of revolution struck it - in 1820, in 1830 and in 1848 - and the system's response to each was different. Reading the three together is the fastest way to see what the settlement could and could not do.

## Lesson 14 - Three tests: 1820, 1830 and 1848

### Visual: three waves, three different results

```text
 from the monarch     Poland, national        SOCIAL programme
                      separation

                        neutrality guaranteed
                        by treaty in 1839

                        intervening
                      - Italy/Germany:

                      arrangement it could    reasons of their own
                      no longer hold
```

*The middle column is the one that decides the topic's verdict. 1820 shows a system that suppresses; 1848 shows a system that is overwhelmed; 1830 shows a system that chooses - conceding in Brussels and Paris, refusing in Warsaw. A settlement that chooses is neither rigid nor liberal; it is a great-power arrangement doing what great-power arrangements do.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

It is tempting to read the three waves as a single rising curve - pressure building until the dam breaks in 1848. That reading is wrong in an instructive way, because the three waves were made of different materials, demanded different things and met different responses.

The 1820 wave was constitutional and narrow, and it was suppressed by foreign armies without difficulty. The 1848 wave was constitutional, national and social all at once, was far broader, succeeded almost everywhere for a few months, and then failed. Between them, 1830 was the most revealing of the three, because it was the occasion on which the system had to *choose* - and what it chose tells you what it actually was.

### 2. 1820-21, restated as a test

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Lesson 10 covered the events; what matters here is what they proved. Three things.

1. **The settlement could be defended by force, cheaply and quickly.** Two interventions, in Italy and in Spain, restored the position within three years.
2. **Defending it cost the system its unity.** Britain dissented, abstained and withdrew; the congress machinery became unusable.
3. **The suppression was of a constitutional demand, and the demand did not die.** ⚠️ It returned in 1830 attached to a national demand, and in 1848 attached to a social one, each time harder to contain than before.

### 3. 1830: the year of choices

✅ **Fact:** **Belgian independence was accepted in 1830**, showing that the Concert could adapt when necessary and that the Vienna order lasted not because it was rigid but because it was quietly flexible.

✅ **Fact:** The **Treaty of London of 19 April 1839** guaranteed the neutrality and independence of Belgium.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** take the three principal cases of 1830 in turn, because their differences are the argument.

**France, July 1830.** A revolution in Paris replaced one king with another and altered the constitutional basis of the monarchy. On the Troppau doctrine this was precisely the kind of event requiring collective restoration. Nothing of the sort was attempted, and the new government was recognised. ⚠️ Why? Because intervention in France meant war with France, and France in 1830 was a great power with an army - not Naples. ⚠️ The doctrine had always been enforceable only against the weak, and 1830 demonstrated it.

**Belgium, from August 1830.** The southern provinces rose against the Kingdom of the Netherlands - an arrangement created by the settlement itself, in Lesson 4, purely for containment. ✅ The Concert accepted the independence of Belgium. ✅ Nine years later the powers guaranteed its neutrality and independence by the Treaty of London of 19 April 1839. ⚠️ The handling is the clearest single demonstration of the Concert working as a method: an actual breach of the Vienna map was referred to collective consultation, settled by negotiation, and secured by a joint guarantee that converted a revolutionary act into part of the public law of Europe. ⚠️ It is also a demonstration of the limits of the settlement's own principles, since the powers conceded a national secession in a case where legitimacy plainly pointed the other way - because the alternative was a general war over a state none of them valued enough to fight for.

**Poland, 1830-31.** A rising against Russian rule in Congress Poland was crushed by Russian forces, and no great power intervened on the Poles' behalf. ⚠️ The comparison with Belgium is the sharpest single piece of evidence about the character of the system. Both were national risings against a foreign-imposed arrangement of 1815; one was conceded and guaranteed, the other crushed without a hand raised. The difference is not in the merits and not in the principle: it is that Belgium lay in a region where the balance could be preserved by agreement, and Poland lay inside the sphere of a great power that would have had to be fought.

⚠️ **Analytical inference: the formulation.** **The Concert conceded where concession cost nothing and refused where refusal cost nothing, and its principles determined neither outcome.** That is a hard sentence, and it is the one the Belgium-Poland pair supports. It should be written with the qualification attached: the same flexibility that makes the system look cynical in Warsaw is what allowed it to resolve the Belgian crisis without a European war, which was a real achievement and a real service to the peoples of Europe as well as to their rulers.

### 4. 1848: the deepest challenge

✅ **Fact:** The **revolutions of 1848** across Europe were the deepest challenge faced by the Vienna order. ✅ **Metternich fell in March 1848** as the revolutions swept across Europe, resigning and fleeing Austria. ✅ Repression could delay but not extinguish nationalism.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** 1848 differed from its predecessors in four ways, and this topic owns the comparison while the detailed narrative of the German and Italian outcomes belongs to the unification topic.

1. **Scale and simultaneity.** The wave affected much of the continent at once, so that no power could concentrate against any single outbreak in the way Austria had concentrated against Naples.
2. **Combination of demands.** ⚠️ Constitutional, national and social demands arrived together. A constitution could be conceded; national unification could not be conceded by a multinational dynastic state without dissolving it; and the social demands frightened the propertied liberals who were leading the constitutional movement.
3. **The removal of the system's central figure.** ✅ Metternich's fall in March 1848 was not merely symbolic: the man who had articulated the order and coordinated it from Vienna for thirty-nine years was removed by the very force he had spent that time denying.
4. **The revolutions nevertheless failed.** ⚠️ And the reasons are internal to the revolutions rather than to the system: the coalition contained incompatible demands, so that each success threatened its own partners; national claims in multinational regions were mutually destructive, since one nation's self-determination was another's dismemberment; the dynastic armies remained intact and obedient, and revolutions that capture capitals but not armies are reversible; and liberal nationalism possessed no instrument of force, which is why the same national goals were achieved in the following two decades by statecraft and war rather than by assemblies.

⚠️ **Analytical inference: the verdict sentence.** 1848 failed as a revolution and succeeded as a demonstration. It proved that the Vienna order could no longer command consent, and that liberal nationalism could not by itself command force - which is why the next generation achieved national unification by abandoning the first half of that formula and borrowing the second from the conservative states themselves.

⚠️ **Boundary:** the course of the German and Italian revolutions of 1848, the Frankfurt attempt at German unification and the subsequent turn to statecraft and war belong to the topic that owns Italian and German unification. This topic uses 1848 to test the settlement and hands the sequel over.

### 5. The three waves in one table

| | ✅ 1820-21 | ✅ 1830-31 | ✅ 1848 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Character of demand | Constitutional | Constitutional **and national** | Constitutional, national **and social** |
| Social base | Officers, professionals, some nobility | Urban middle classes, with artisan support in places | Broad: liberals, nationalists, urban workers, peasants in some regions |
| System's response | Collective authorisation; foreign armies; suppression | **Selective**: concede in France and Belgium, crush in Poland, suppress in Italy and Germany | Overwhelmed at first; then defeated by the revolutions' own divisions and by intact dynastic armies |
| Damage to the system | Split it doctrinally; ended the congress rhythm | Made the original form of the Concert obsolete, along with 1848 and the later unifications | Removed its central figure; ended its claim to command consent |
| ⚠️ What it proved | The settlement could be defended by force against weak opponents | The settlement's principles did not determine its conduct; interest and capability did | Repression could delay but not extinguish nationalism |

✅ **Fact:** The revolutions of 1830 and 1848, together with the later Italian and German unifications, made the original form of the Concert obsolete.

### 6. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** If the system survived 1820 comfortably, chose its battles successfully in 1830 and defeated the revolutions of 1848 as well, in what sense did it fail at all? The dynasties were still in place in 1849 and the settlement's central arrangements held for another decade.

**Reply.** The objection is correct as to the immediate outcome and mistaken as to what the outcome shows. A system's strength is measured by the resources required to sustain it, and by that measure the trend across the three waves is unambiguous. In 1820-21 the order was maintained by two limited expeditions authorised by a congress that still met; in 1830 it was maintained by conceding two of its own arrangements and by a Russian army in Poland; in 1848 it was maintained only by the collapse of the revolutions' own coalitions, after every major capital in central Europe had been lost and the order's central statesman had fled. ⚠️ The order survived 1848 in the way a wall survives when the thing pushing it stops pushing, and within two decades the central European arrangements of 1815 - the German Confederation and the Austrian position in Italy - had been dismantled by states the settlement itself had created. ⚠️ Survival in 1849 and obsolescence by 1871 are the same story told at two points.

⚠️ **Residual.** How far the revolutions of 1848 would have succeeded had the social and national demands not divided their own coalitions is a genuine counterfactual, and it is not settled here. This session records the four mechanisms of failure without ranking them.

### UPSC application

The three waves are the topic's evidence for every "how far did it succeed?" or "postponement or suppression?" demand. The single most valuable item in the lesson is the Belgium-Poland comparison in 1830, because it is compact, dated, and decisive: two national risings against arrangements of 1815, opposite responses, and the difference explained by capability rather than by principle. An answer that uses that pair has demonstrated analytical control without spending many words.

**Probable framing.** "How far did the Vienna settlement postpone, rather than resolve, the national question?" "Examine the capacity of the Concert of Europe to adapt." "The revolutions of 1848 destroyed the Vienna order. Comment."

**Common traps.** Treating the three waves as one rising curve of the same thing. Claiming the powers intervened against the French revolution of 1830 - they did not. Omitting Poland, which is where the Belgian concession acquires its meaning. Narrating the German and Italian revolutions of 1848 in detail, which belongs to another owner. Saying Metternich was overthrown by a foreign power rather than by revolution in Vienna. Saying 1848 destroyed the settlement outright - it broke the order's authority; the central arrangements were dismantled over the following two decades.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ Three waves, three different materials: **constitutional (1820)**; **constitutional plus national (1830)**; **constitutional plus national plus social (1848)**.
2. ✅ 1820-21: suppressed by foreign armies under congress authorisation; the system split doing it.
3. ✅ **1830, France:** the July Revolution replaced the king; the powers did not intervene - intervention meant war with a great power.
4. ✅ **1830, Belgium:** independence **accepted** by the Concert; ✅ neutrality and independence **guaranteed by the Treaty of London, 19 April 1839**.
5. ✅ **1830-31, Poland:** the rising was crushed by Russia; no power intervened.
6. ⚠️ Belgium against Poland is the decisive pair: the same kind of rising, opposite outcomes, explained by capability rather than principle.
7. ✅ **1848:** the deepest challenge; ✅ **Metternich fell in March 1848** and fled.
8. ⚠️ Four reasons 1848 failed: incompatible demands within the coalition; national claims mutually destructive in multinational regions; dynastic armies intact and obedient; liberal nationalism had no instrument of force.
9. ⚠️ Verdict sentence: 1848 failed as a revolution and succeeded as a demonstration.
10. ✅ The revolutions of 1830 and 1848, with the later unifications, made the **original form of the Concert obsolete**.
11. ⚠️ Trend across the waves: the cost of maintaining the order rose from two expeditions, to two concessions plus an army, to the collapse of the challengers' own coalitions.
12. ❌ Do not narrate the German and Italian revolutions of 1848 or the Frankfurt assembly; they belong to the unification owner.

### Concept check

**Question:** In 1830 the Concert accepted the independence of Belgium and, in 1839, guaranteed her neutrality; in 1830-31 it allowed Russia to crush the Polish rising without a word of protest. Both were national risings against arrangements made in 1815. Explain the difference, and state what the pair establishes about the Vienna system.

**Model answer:** The difference is not one of principle, because on the settlement's own principles the two cases were indistinguishable and indeed the Belgian case was the weaker of the two: the Kingdom of the Netherlands was an arrangement the powers had themselves created for containment, its dismemberment was a direct breach of the map of 1815, and legitimacy pointed unambiguously to upholding the Dutch king. The difference lies in what each outcome would have cost. Belgium lay in a region where every great power had an interest in the result and none had a decisive local preponderance, so a collective settlement was available: the powers referred the crisis to consultation, accepted the independence of Belgium in 1830, and in the Treaty of London of 19 April 1839 guaranteed her neutrality and independence, converting a revolutionary act into part of the public law of Europe and removing a possible cause of general war. Poland lay inside the sphere of a great power. To assist the Polish rising would have required war with Russia, which no power wanted and none could undertake for a cause in which its own interests were not engaged, so the rising was crushed and nothing was said. What the pair establishes is that the Vienna system's conduct was determined by capability and interest rather than by its stated principles: it conceded where concession cost nothing and could be made collectively, and it refused where refusal cost nothing because the alternative was war with a great power. That conclusion should be stated with its qualification, however, since the same flexibility that looks cynical in Warsaw is what allowed a breach of the settlement in Brussels to be resolved without a European war - which was a real service to peace and not merely to rulers.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the acceptance of Belgian independence as evidence that the Concert had become liberal, or as a concession to the principle of nationality. It was neither. The powers conceded a fact they were unwilling to fight over and then bound it into the treaty system by a joint guarantee, which is a conservative operation, not a liberal one: the object was to remove the disturbance from the field of great-power competition, and the guarantee of 1839 is the proof, since its purpose was to make Belgium permanently neutral and therefore permanently removed from the balance.

**Bridge to Lesson 15.** The tests are complete, and the record is mixed in a way that resists a simple verdict. It is time to confront the claim on which the settlement's reputation mainly rests: that it gave Europe an unusually long peace. That claim is true in a precise sense and false in several loose ones, and stating it precisely is the difference between a defensible conclusion and a slogan.

## Lesson 15 - Did it keep the peace? Stating the claim so that it survives

### Visual: what the peace claim covers, and what it does not

```text
   THE CLAIM, STATED LOOSELY (and therefore indefensible):
   "The Congress of Vienna gave Europe a hundred years of peace."
                              |
                              X   FAILS on four counts
                              |
   +--------------------------+---------------------------+
   | 1. There WAS a war among great powers: the Crimean   |
   |    War of 1853-56                                     |
   | 2. There were wars of national unification in the     |
   |    1860s that dismantled the settlement itself        |
   | 3. There were revolutions, interventions and          |
   |    repressions throughout - the system USED force     |
   | 4. Europe's peace coexisted with expanding violence   |
   |    outside Europe                                     |
   +-------------------------------------------------------+

   THE CLAIM, STATED PRECISELY (and defensible):
   "Between 1815 and the Crimean War of 1853-56 no general war
    was fought among the great powers of Europe - an interval
    without parallel in the century preceding it - and even
    after 1856 no continent-wide coalition war comparable to
    the Napoleonic wars occurred before 1914."
                              |
                              v
   WHAT THE PRECISE CLAIM ALLOWS YOU TO SAY
   - the settlement solved the problem it set itself
   - the consultative practice handled crises that had
     previously produced coalition wars: Belgium 1830,
     Greece 1827-32
   - the interval is long by any historical standard
                              |
   WHAT IT STILL DOES NOT ALLOW YOU TO SAY
   - that the peace was general, or that it was
     peace for the peoples rather than among the powers
   - that the Concert alone caused it - exhaustion,
     dynastic fear of revolution and the absence of a
     hegemonic challenger all contributed
```

*This is the single most important visual in the topic for examination purposes, because nearly every question on the Vienna settlement eventually asks for a verdict, and the verdict turns on how the peace claim is stated.*

### 1. The problem, in ordinary language

The Vienna settlement's reputation rests principally on one proposition: it worked. Europe had been at war almost continuously from 1792 to 1815; after 1815 it was not. That contrast is real and it is the strongest thing that can be said for the settlement.

But the proposition is stated carelessly more often than not, and careless statements are easy to demolish. A candidate who writes that Vienna gave Europe a century of peace will be met with the Crimean War, the wars of Italian and German unification, the Russian suppression of Poland, three interventions in the 1820s, and the revolutions of 1830 and 1848. The claim then collapses, and with it the answer's credibility.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the discipline required is therefore precision about three things: what kind of peace is being claimed, among whom, and for how long. The claim survives when all three are specified and fails when any of them is left vague.

### 2. The claim stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** The great-power framework survived for decades without a general war comparable to the Napoleonic wars. ✅ No general European war occurred between 1815 and the Crimean War of 1853-56.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the precise formulation has three specifications.

1. **What kind of peace?** A peace among great powers - the absence of a general coalition war - not the absence of armed force in Europe. ✅ The standard caution is explicit: the Concert prevented a general war, not intervention or regional war.
2. **Among whom?** Among the five recognised great powers. Wars involving smaller states, colonial campaigns and internal repression fall outside the claim.
3. **For how long?** The tight and defensible interval is 1815 to 1853-56. A looser interval extending to 1914 can be defended only for the narrower proposition that no continent-wide coalition war comparable to the Napoleonic wars occurred in that longer span - and even that requires acknowledging the unification wars of the 1860s.

> 🔑 **Answer line:** Between 1815 and 1853 the great powers of Europe did not fight a general war among themselves. That is an interval without parallel in the century before it, and it is the settlement's genuine achievement - stated in the only form that survives cross-examination.

### 3. What the settlement actually contributed

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** granting the interval, how much of it is attributable to the settlement? Four contributions can be defended and one over-claim must be refused.

**Contribution 1: the removal of the specific cause of the previous wars.** The wars of 1792-1815 had a definite origin: a single power attempting to dominate the continent. The settlement contained that power by a barrier ring, readmitted it as a partner, and removed its incentive to revise the arrangement. ⚠️ Whatever else it did, it solved the problem it was set.

**Contribution 2: an equilibrium with no obvious revisionist.** After 1815 no great power held a position so unsatisfactory as to make a general war worth risking. ⚠️ That is a genuine property of the territorial arrangement and not merely a description of the outcome: the powers' gains at Vienna were distributed so that each had more to lose than to win from a general war.

**Contribution 3: a practice of consultation that handled crises which had previously produced wars.** ✅ The Belgian question of 1830 was a breach of the settlement resolved by collective negotiation and later by joint guarantee; ✅ the Greek question of 1821-32, an Eastern crisis with a Russian interest in it, was settled by tripartite action and a collectively guaranteed independence rather than by unilateral Russian expansion. ⚠️ Both are cases in which the eighteenth-century pattern would very probably have produced a coalition war, and the difference is the consultative habit.

**Contribution 4: a shared conservative interest in avoiding war.** ⚠️ The dynastic monarchies had learned in 1792-1815 that general war produces revolution. That is the connection between the domestic order of Lesson 13 and the international peace: the rulers feared war partly because they feared what war does to thrones.

**The over-claim to refuse.** ❌ The settlement did not create a mechanism for preventing war. There was no obligation, no procedure, no sanction and no organ. ⚠️ What it created was a distribution of territory in which general war was unattractive to everybody, plus a habit of talking before acting. Those are real, and they are not a mechanism - which is the point Lesson 17 needs.

### 4. The competing explanations, which an honest answer concedes

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three explanations for the long interval are independent of the settlement, and a strong answer states them rather than leaving them for an examiner to supply.

1. **Exhaustion.** A generation of continuous war left every participant depleted in men, money and appetite. ⚠️ Post-war exhaustion is a recurrent phenomenon and produces intervals of peace irrespective of the quality of the settlement that follows. The Vienna interval overlaps with the exhaustion period, and the two cannot be fully separated.
2. **The absence of a hegemonic challenger.** ⚠️ A general war in Europe requires a power with both the capacity and the motive to attempt continental domination. Between 1815 and the 1850s no such power existed: France was contained and internally unstable, Russia was vast but unmodernised, Austria was defensive and preoccupied, Prussia was still a second-rank power, and Britain wanted no continental territory at all. The absence of a candidate is an explanation that owes nothing to the settlement.
3. **Preoccupation elsewhere.** ⚠️ The energies of the most dynamic European states in this period were directed to industrialisation and to expansion outside Europe. Whatever else those processes did, they absorbed attention and resources that earlier generations had spent on continental war.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the honest conclusion is that the settlement was a substantial contributing cause of the long interval and not its sole cause, and that the strongest evidence for its contribution is not the absence of war in general but the specific crises that were resolved without war and would previously have caused one.

### 5. The Crimean War as the terminus

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Crimean War of 1853-56 is the natural end-point of the claim and is worth understanding as a diagnosis rather than a date. Several of the powers that had constructed and operated the Concert fought one another over a question - the Eastern question - that the Concert had handled successfully in the Greek crisis a quarter of a century earlier. ⚠️ What had changed was not the question but the system's capacity to handle it: the shared conservative purpose that had made consultation work had been eroded by 1830 and destroyed by 1848, and the members no longer regarded a collective solution as preferable to a national one. ⚠️ **The Concert did not end because a war broke out; a war broke out because the Concert had ended.**

⚠️ **Boundary:** the causes, course and consequences of the Crimean War belong to the topics that own the later nineteenth century. This topic uses it only to date the end of the long interval and to diagnose the exhaustion of the Concert's shared purpose.

### 6. The graded verdict, in three usable forms

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the topic's directives almost always demand a verdict, and three formulations serve different question types. Each contains a concession, which is what makes it a verdict rather than an opinion.

**The dual verdict.** "Vienna succeeded remarkably at its own stated objective - preventing another continent-wide war among the great powers - and failed at the objective it refused to acknowledge, the political claims of nations and liberals."

**The adaptation verdict.** "The Vienna order lasted not because it was rigid but because it was quietly flexible: it restored dynasties where it could and accepted change, as in Belgium in 1830, where it had to."

**The deferral verdict.** "Vienna did not answer the national question; it postponed it, and the interest on that postponement was paid in 1830, 1848 and finally in the unifications of 1859-71."

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and one further formulation is available for the highest-level questions, which combines the peace claim with its cost: **"the same great-power discipline that prevented war among states was used to prevent change within them."** That sentence is the most economical statement of the peace-or-repression problem available, because it does not choose between the two; it identifies them as one mechanism producing two results.

### 7. Objection, reply and residual

**Objection.** Is a peace among five monarchies, maintained by suppressing constitutional movements and by disposing of populations without their consent, worth calling a peace at all? Does the claim not simply define peace as the absence of the kind of violence that inconveniences great powers?

**Reply.** The objection has real force and should be conceded in substance, then bounded. It is conceded that the interval was a peace *among the powers* and not for the peoples; that force was used continuously within states and at the system's margins; and that the Polish case shows what the peace was worth to a nation inside a great power's sphere. What the objection cannot do is dismiss the achievement, and the reason is the comparison that actually matters. The alternative to a great-power peace is not a liberal Europe; it is a great-power war. ⚠️ The wars of 1792-1815 had killed on a scale that no censorship regime approaches, had destroyed states, ruined economies and produced the very conditions in which authoritarian government becomes attractive. A settlement that prevented the recurrence of *that* for four decades delivered something of real value to the populations of Europe as well as to their rulers, whatever else it denied them. ⚠️ The correct treatment is therefore to hold both: the peace was real, it was purchased by repression, and the two facts belong in the same sentence rather than in competing paragraphs.

⚠️ **Residual.** The relative weight of the settlement, exhaustion, the absence of a hegemonic challenger and extra-European preoccupation in producing the long interval cannot be fixed from the evidence held here, and no ranking is asserted.

### UPSC application

This lesson is where most answers on this topic are won or lost, because almost every directive - *examine*, *assess*, *how far*, *critically analyse* - is asking for the verdict this lesson constructs. The decisive skill is stating the peace claim in its precise form in the answer's opening or closing sentence, because a precise claim cannot be demolished by a counter-example and a loose one can.

**Probable framing.** "The Congress of Vienna created peace among states but not legitimacy among peoples. Examine." "Was the Concert of Europe an instrument of peace or of repression?" "How far was the Vienna settlement a success?"

**Common traps.** Claiming a century of peace. Forgetting the Crimean War. Attributing the whole interval to the settlement without conceding exhaustion and the absence of a challenger. Writing an ungraded conclusion that simply says "it was partly successful". Treating peace among the powers and peace for the peoples as the same claim.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The defensible claim: **no general war among the great powers between 1815 and the Crimean War of 1853-56**.
2. ✅ Standard caution: the Concert prevented a **general war**, not intervention or regional war.
3. ⚠️ Three specifications: what kind of peace, among whom, for how long. Leave any of the three vague and the claim fails.
4. ⚠️ Four defensible contributions: the specific cause of the previous wars removed; an equilibrium with no obvious revisionist; a consultative practice that resolved Belgium in 1830 and Greece in 1827-32; a shared dynastic fear that war produces revolution.
5. ❌ The over-claim to refuse: the settlement created **no mechanism** - no obligation, procedure, sanction or organ.
6. ⚠️ Three competing explanations to concede: exhaustion; the absence of a hegemonic challenger; preoccupation with industrialisation and extra-European expansion.
7. ⚠️ The Crimean War is the terminus: **the Concert did not end because a war broke out; a war broke out because the Concert had ended.**
8. ⚠️ Three verdict scaffolds: the **dual verdict**; the **adaptation verdict**; the **deferral verdict**.
9. ⚠️ The economical formulation: the same great-power discipline that prevented war among states was used to prevent change within them.
10. ⚠️ The reply to the sharpest objection: the alternative to a great-power peace is not a liberal Europe but a great-power war.
11. ❌ Do not give casualty, army or expenditure figures for any war in this period; none is held in this evidence base.

### Concept check

**Question:** "The Congress of Vienna gave Europe a hundred years of peace." State four specific objections that demolish this sentence, then rewrite the claim in a form that survives all four while preserving what is genuinely true in it.

**Model answer:** The four objections are these. First, a war was fought among great powers within the period: the Crimean War of 1853-56 set several of the Concert's own members against one another. Second, the wars of Italian and German unification in the 1860s were fought in Europe by European powers and dismantled the settlement's central arrangements, so the period contains not merely war but war that destroyed the thing said to have produced the peace. Third, force was used continuously throughout the period by the system itself - Austrian intervention in Naples and Piedmont in 1821, French intervention in Spain in 1823, the Russian suppression of the Polish rising in 1830-31, and armed repression during the revolutions of 1848 - so the claim confuses the absence of a general war with the absence of organised violence. Fourth, the century in question was one of expanding European violence outside Europe, so even a peaceful Europe does not make the sentence true of the powers' conduct generally. The claim that survives all four is narrower and still substantial: between 1815 and the Crimean War of 1853-56 no general war was fought among the great powers of Europe, an interval without parallel in the century preceding it, and the consultative practice established after 1815 resolved without war at least two crises - the Belgian secession of 1830 and the Greek question between 1827 and 1832 - which under the eighteenth-century pattern would very probably have produced a coalition war. That formulation specifies the kind of peace as peace among great powers, specifies the parties as the five recognised great powers, and specifies the term as 1815 to 1853-56; and it concedes, as any defensible version must, that exhaustion after a generation of war and the absence of any power capable of attempting continental domination contributed to the same result.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating precision as weakness. A candidate who narrows the claim is not conceding the settlement's achievement but making it defensible, and the narrowed claim is in fact the more impressive one, because it identifies a specific historical rarity - four decades without a general war among the European great powers - instead of an unspecific assertion that an examiner can dismantle with a single date.

**Bridge to Lesson 16.** The verdict now rests on a comparison: was this order a reactionary imposition that delayed the inevitable, or a sophisticated equilibrium that managed change? Historians have taken both positions and a third that is more interesting than either. The next lesson sets out the quarrel, and then asks the question that decides how much weight any verdict can bear - what would have happened had the settlement been made differently.

## Lesson 16 - The historians' quarrel, and the counterfactual that tests it

### Visual: three readings of the same settlement

```text

 social order
              |

 ----------------------

 reads Metternich and Castlereagh

              |
 READING 3: TRANSFORMATION               The furthest revision
 -------------------------
 Vienna was not a restoration and
 not merely a balance of power. It
 was the first modern attempt at a

 and consultation, capable of
 managing change into the 1850s.
 EVIDENCE: the practice of
 consultation; the Belgian and
 Greek settlements; the durability
 of the framework
```
