# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks rather than within a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - Instruments, actors and principles, Lessons 1 to 3

### Cumulative check A1 - from a chain of instruments to a governing criterion

**Question:** Reconstruct the settlement of 1814-15 as a sequence of instruments, and then show how the four operating considerations - legitimacy, balance, compensation and containment - divide the work of deciding among them.

**Model answer:** The sequence has six stages. The Treaty of Chaumont of 1 March 1814 converted the war coalition into a twenty-year alliance of Britain, Austria, Prussia and Russia, each pledging 150,000 men and none to make a separate peace, which made the four jointly and indefinitely responsible for the outcome. The first Treaty of Paris of 30 May 1814 disposed of France, restoring Louis XVIII, leaving her the frontiers of 1792 without indemnity or occupation, and summoning by its Article 32 a general congress at Vienna. The congress assembled from late September 1814 and worked through committees rather than plenaries. The German Federal Act of 8 June 1815 created the Confederation of thirty-nine states with its Diet at Frankfurt under Austrian presidency, and the Final Act of 9 June 1815 consolidated every separate agreement into one general treaty. The Hundred Days interrupted the implementation rather than the work - Napoleon landed on 1 March 1815 and was declared an outlaw by the assembled powers on 13 March, and was defeated at Waterloo on 18 June. The second Treaty of Paris of 20 November 1815 then repriced the settlement with France, reducing her to the frontiers of 1790, imposing 700 million francs and providing for an occupation of up to five years which ended in 1818. The four considerations divide the work because they answer different questions. Legitimacy answers who is entitled to rule and produced the Bourbon restoration and the preservation of the King of Saxony. Balance answers how much any one ruler may hold and produced a France contained rather than destroyed and a Switzerland whose neutrality was guaranteed. Compensation answers how the bargain is made acceptable to everyone at the table and produced Prussia's Rhineland, Westphalia and part of Saxony, Austria's Lombardy and Venetia, Piedmont's Genoa and Russia's Congress Poland. Containment, which was never announced, answers where precisely the territory should go, and it is the only one of the four that explains the Kingdom of the Netherlands, the placing of Prussia on the French frontier and the enlargement of Piedmont.

**Misconception to avoid:** Collapsing the six instruments into "the Congress of Vienna". The indemnity, the occupation and the provision for periodic meetings were all imposed by instruments other than the congress, and an answer that cannot name which document did what will also be unable to explain why the terms with France changed between 1814 and 1815.

### Cumulative check A2 - the uses and limits of a principle

**Question:** Show how legitimacy operated at Vienna as both a genuine constraint and a diplomatic instrument, using two cases in which it bound the powers and two in which it did not apply.

**Model answer:** It bound the powers in two clear cases. Prussia demanded the whole kingdom of Saxony as compensation for her losses of 1806 and was refused; Frederick Augustus I remained King of Saxony over the portion Prussia did not receive, an outcome that no calculation of the balance of power by itself required, since a Saxony wholly Prussian is not obviously less balanced than a Saxony divided. And France, a defeated aggressor against the whole continent, was restored to her lawful dynasty and retained as a great power rather than partitioned, at a moment when the victors had the capacity to do otherwise. It operated as an instrument in the same episode: Talleyrand, representing a defeated power with nothing to trade, championed the rights of lawful monarchs precisely because that argument supplied Austria and Britain with a principled form for an objection they held on grounds of the balance of power, and it made France a party to the secret defensive alliance of 3 January 1815 with Britain and Austria against Prussia and Russia. It did not apply in two other kinds of case. It did not apply to institutions rather than dynasties: the Holy Roman Empire was not revived, the republics of Venice and Genoa were not restored, and the ecclesiastical principalities did not return, because the consolidation Napoleon had carried out was convenient and was kept. And it did not apply to nations without a dynasty: Poland had been partitioned before the revolutionary wars by three of the powers at the table, so there was no lawful Polish sovereign whose restoration the principle could require, and most of the Duchy of Warsaw passed instead under Russian control as Congress Poland. The general formulation is that legitimacy was a real constraint with a limited jurisdiction - binding inside the circle of recognised European dynasties, silent about institutions the victors preferred not to restore, and wholly absent where a people had no ruler to be restored.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the selective application as proof of insincerity. A principle that is genuinely applied in some cases and inapplicable in others is not a pretext; the analytical task is to locate its jurisdiction, and the jurisdiction here is the community of European dynasties.

## Block B - The map and its crises, Lessons 4 to 6

### Cumulative check B1 - the map as a set of decisions

**Question:** Take four territorial decisions of 1815 and, for each, state the governing consideration and the long consequence. Then state what the four together establish about the settlement's method.

**Model answer:** The Kingdom of the Netherlands, created by joining Belgium to Holland, was governed by containment: it closed the invasion route to the Channel with a state whose coast Britain could supply, and its long consequence was a union of two peoples divided by religion, language and economic interest, which the Belgians broke in 1830 and which the powers then accepted rather than defend. The transfer of the Rhineland and Westphalia to Prussia was governed by compensation and containment together: Prussia had to be paid for the Saxony she did not receive, and placing the most militarised German state on the French frontier gave the barrier ring its eastern weight; its long consequence was that Prussia acquired the coal, iron and population of the future industrial heartland of Germany and the patriotic credential of facing France, which is the foundation of Prussian primacy. The grant of Lombardy and Venetia to Austria was governed by compensation and by the requirement that a great power stand behind the Italian barrier; its long consequence was that Austria became the visible foreign ruler of northern Italy, so that Austrian predominance there could be contested only by war rather than politically. The creation of Congress Poland under the Russian crown was governed by compensation to the power whose armies had carried the decisive weight of 1812-14, restrained but not prevented by the balance objections of Austria and Britain; its long consequence was the clearest case of a nation subordinated to great-power bargaining and a rising in 1830-31 that was crushed without any power intervening. Together the four establish that the settlement's method was to treat territory as a transferable quantity of power, allocated so that every participant with an army left the table satisfied - which is why the arrangement was stable among the powers and illegitimate in the eyes of the populations it moved.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the consequences back into the intentions. The negotiators were solving a problem in the distribution of power in 1815; the industrial significance of the Rhine coalfield and the future role of Piedmont formed no part of any calculation available to them, and the point is not that they blundered but that a territorial settlement distributes future potential it cannot price.

### Cumulative check B2 - crisis and the treatment of the defeated

**Question:** Explain how the Polish-Saxon crisis and the treatment of France, taken together, show that the Vienna settlement was a general equilibrium rather than a coalition against a single enemy.

**Model answer:** The Polish-Saxon crisis shows the balance principle being enforced against the victors. Russia demanded a large Polish kingdom under the Tsar's crown and Prussia demanded the whole of Saxony, and because Prussia's surrender of her Polish lands was the price of Russia's kingdom the two demands formed a single transaction. Austria objected because a Russian kingdom on the Vistula would place the largest army in Europe on her northern flank and expose Galicia; Britain objected because a Russia advanced into central Europe combined with a Prussia enlarged by a whole kingdom would form a bloc that nothing in the west could balance. Talleyrand supplied the legitimist argument that a lawful king could not simply be extinguished, and on 3 January 1815 Britain, Austria and France signed a secret defensive alliance against Prussia and Russia, designed to become known rather than to be used. The compromise gave Russia a smaller Congress Poland, left Austria her Galicia, gave Prussia part of Saxony with Frederick Augustus I retained over the remainder, and redirected Prussia's compensation westward. The treatment of France shows the same equilibrium logic applied to the defeated power: in 1814 she kept her 1792 frontiers with no indemnity and no occupation, in 1815 she was reduced to her 1790 frontiers with 700 million francs and an occupation of up to five years, and in 1818 at Aix-la-Chapelle the obligations having been met, the occupation ended and she was readmitted to great-power consultation. Taken together the two episodes make the case. A coalition against a single enemy would have concentrated its restraints on that enemy and distributed the spoils among its members; the Vienna powers instead restrained each other, threatened each other within eight months of the common victory, and readmitted the defeated enemy as an equal within three years of her final defeat. The settlement's object was therefore a distribution in which no power - victor or vanquished - could dominate, which is what a general equilibrium means.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding from the secret alliance that the settlement was a fiction and the powers merely rival states as before. The test of a system is not whether conflict occurs inside it but whether conflict is resolved without war; the Polish-Saxon quarrel was resolved by negotiation within weeks, written into a general instrument signed by all, and followed by machinery for repeating the process.

## Block C - The machinery and the split, Lessons 7 to 9

### Cumulative check C1 - sorting the machinery

**Question:** Using the test "what does this oblige anyone to do?", distinguish the Holy Alliance, the Quadruple Alliance, the congress system and the Concert of Europe, and explain why the distinction is necessary in order to describe the events of 1820 accurately.

**Model answer:** The Holy Alliance, signed on 26 September 1815 by Russia, Austria and Prussia, obliges its signatories to govern by Christian moral principles of justice, charity and peace - which is to say, in operative terms, to do nothing measurable; Britain did not join, the Prince Regent declining to sign, and the Pope and the Ottoman Sultan were excluded. Its practical importance is limited, and its function was to supply a vocabulary of legitimation rather than an obligation. The Quadruple Alliance, signed on 20 November 1815 by Britain, Austria, Prussia and Russia, obliges its signatories to maintain forces against a French relapse, to uphold the Vienna settlement and, by Article VI, to meet periodically; France was added at Aix-la-Chapelle in 1818, making it a Quintuple Alliance and shifting the object from containing France to maintaining the European order generally. The congress system is the practice generated by Article VI - four meetings at Aix-la-Chapelle in 1818, Troppau in 1820, Laibach in 1821 and Verona in 1822 - and it obliges nobody to any outcome, because Article VI provided for meetings and said nothing about their competence. The Concert of Europe obliges nobody to anything, because it is not an instrument at all but the practice of great-power consultation to preserve the territorial and political status quo. The distinction is necessary for 1820 because the events of that year cannot otherwise be stated correctly: the Troppau Protocol of 19 November 1820 was signed by three powers and refused by two, so it was neither an act of the Holy Alliance, which had only three members and no operative machinery, nor an act of the Quadruple Alliance, which had four and could not act against the will of one; it was a claim advanced by three of the five inside a meeting held under a clause that had never defined what such meetings might decide. Getting that right is also what allows the correct statement that the congress system ended with Verona while the Concert survived to settle the Belgian question in 1830.

**Misconception to avoid:** Using "the Holy Alliance" as a general name for the conservative order because contemporaries and later liberal writers did. The usage records how the system appeared to those it acted upon and is historically meaningful, but an answer must be able to name the instrument that actually authorised each action, and the Holy Alliance authorised none.

### Cumulative check C2 - the structural source of the split

**Question:** State the two rival conceptions of what the alliance was for, identify the documents in which each was stated, and explain why the disagreement could not have been resolved by better diplomacy.

**Model answer:** The first conception holds that the settlement rests on a principle of legitimate monarchical authority, so that a constitution extorted from a sovereign attacks the settlement as directly as an invasion attacks a frontier; on this view a state altered by revolution has left the European alliance and may be restored to it by force. It was stated in the Troppau Protocol of 19 November 1820, signed by Austria, Prussia and Russia. The second holds that the alliance was made against the military domination of Europe by France, that it is not a union for the government of the world or for the superintendence of other states' internal affairs, and that the settlement is territorial, so that frontiers are the alliance's business and constitutions are not. It was stated in Castlereagh's State Paper of 5 May 1820 - written after the Spanish revolution of January but before the Neapolitan revolution of July and more than six months before Troppau, which disposes of any reading that treats the British position as an improvised reaction. The disagreement could not have been resolved by diplomacy because each position followed from the internal constitution of the state that held it. Austria was a multinational dynastic empire that could not concede the constitutional or national principle anywhere without conceding the principle that would dissolve it; Russia was an autocracy with no theory of limited monarchy; Britain was a parliamentary monarchy whose ministers could not bind Parliament or their successors to an indefinite commitment of money and troops for the policing of other countries, and whose own constitution was the product of exactly the kind of limitation on monarchy that the doctrine condemned. No concession available to any negotiator could alter those facts, which is why the divergence was present in the alliance from 1815, was stated formally in May 1820, and survived the change of British foreign secretary in August 1822 with its substance unchanged.

**Misconception to avoid:** Explaining the split by the arrival of Canning. The substance of the British position had been set out in full by Castlereagh two years earlier; what changed in 1822 was only the judgement about whether the divergence should be managed inside the alliance or acknowledged outside it, and Canning's withdrawal of Wellington from Verona made a pre-existing division visible rather than creating it.

## Block D - The system in action and under test, Lessons 10 to 14

### Cumulative check D1 - the jurisdiction of the doctrine

**Question:** Between 1821 and 1832 the same system suppressed constitutional movements in Naples, Piedmont and Spain, declined to act in Spanish America, accepted a revolution in France and a secession in Belgium, crushed a rising in Poland, and helped rebels detach Greece from the Ottoman Empire. Derive from that record the rule that actually governed the system's conduct.

**Model answer:** The record is consistent once the rule is stated as a rule about capability and interest rather than about principle. The system acted against a constitutional movement where the target was weak, where a great power had a strong local interest in acting, and where no other great power's material interest was engaged against it - which describes Naples and Piedmont in 1821, suppressed by Austria under the authorisation of Laibach, and Spain in 1823, where a French army acted under the authorisation of Verona. It declined to act where a great power's material interest opposed action and that power possessed the means to prevent it, which describes Spanish America, where Britain blocked the extension of intervention at Verona and where the Monroe Doctrine of December 1823 added an independent American warning to the same effect. It declined to act where action meant war with a great power, which describes France in July 1830, when a revolution replaced one king with another and was accepted without protest because intervention in France meant war with France. It conceded where concession removed a disturbance and could be made collectively, which describes Belgium in 1830, accepted by the Concert and secured by the joint guarantee of neutrality in the Treaty of London of 19 April 1839. It refused to intervene where intervention on behalf of the rising meant war with a great power inside whose sphere the rising had occurred, which describes Poland in 1830-31, crushed by Russia with no protest. And it acted against a legitimate sovereign where that sovereign was outside the European monarchical circle, where the Eastern question raised a balance-of-power stake and where joint action bounded the gains of the power most eager to act alone, which describes Greece between the tripartite agreement of 1827, Navarino on 20 October 1827, the London Protocol of 3 February 1830 and the Treaty of Constantinople of 1832. The rule is therefore: the system enforced its declared principle where enforcement was cheap and its members' interests aligned, suspended it where a member's material interest opposed enforcement, and reversed it where the balance of power required the opposite result.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the principle was therefore meaningless. The same period supplies cases in which the principle constrained the powers against their preferences - Saxony in 1815 is the clearest - and the correct formulation is jurisdictional: legitimacy bound the powers inside the European dynastic circle and lapsed at its edges.

### Cumulative check D2 - the three waves as a single argument

**Question:** Using the waves of 1820-21, 1830-31 and 1848, construct an argument about the trend in the cost of maintaining the Vienna order, and state what the trend establishes.

**Model answer:** In 1820-21 the order was maintained at trivial cost. The movements were constitutionalist, led by officers, professional men and sections of the nobility, militarily negligible, and were suppressed by two limited expeditions - Austrian in Naples and Piedmont, French in Spain - authorised by congresses that were still meeting. The only price was internal to the system: Britain refused the Troppau Protocol, abstained at Laibach and withdrew from Verona, so the congress machinery ceased to be usable. In 1830-31 the cost rose and took a different form. The order was maintained not by suppression alone but by surrendering two of its own arrangements: the powers accepted a revolution in France because intervention there meant war with a great power, and accepted the secession of Belgium from a kingdom the settlement itself had created, later binding the result into the treaty system by guaranteeing Belgian neutrality in 1839. Suppression still occurred, but in Poland it required a Russian army and in Italy and Germany local force. In 1848 the cost could no longer be paid by the system at all. The wave was continental and simultaneous, so no power could concentrate against a single outbreak; it combined constitutional, national and social demands, of which the national could not be conceded by a multinational dynastic state and the social frightened the liberals who were leading the constitutional movement; and it removed the order's central figure, since Metternich fell in March 1848 and fled. What defeated the revolutions was not the system's strength but their own divisions, the mutual destructiveness of national claims in multinational regions, the fact that the dynastic armies remained intact and obedient, and the absence of any instrument of force in the hands of liberal nationalism. The trend establishes that the order's capacity to enforce itself declined continuously while the demands made on it grew: from two expeditions, to two concessions plus an army, to survival by the collapse of the challengers' own coalitions. Survival in 1849 and the dismantling of the settlement's central European arrangements between 1859 and 1871 are therefore the same story told at two points.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the three waves as one rising curve of the same demand. They were made of different materials - constitutional, then constitutional and national, then constitutional, national and social - and the change in material is what explains both the change in the system's response and the change in the reasons for the revolutions' failure.

## Block E - Judgement, historiography and legacy, Lessons 15 to 18

### Cumulative check E1 - a verdict that survives cross-examination

**Question:** Write a graded verdict on the Vienna settlement in not more than eight sentences, stating the peace claim precisely, conceding the competing explanations, and identifying the cost.

**Model answer:** Between 1815 and the Crimean War of 1853-56 no general war was fought among the great powers of Europe, an interval without parallel in the century preceding it, and even after 1856 no continent-wide coalition war comparable to the Napoleonic wars occurred before 1914. The settlement contributed substantially to that result: it removed the specific cause of the previous wars by containing and then reintegrating France, it distributed territory so that no power found revision worth a general war, it established a consultative practice that resolved the Belgian secession of 1830 and the Greek question of 1827-32 without war where the eighteenth-century pattern would probably have produced one, and it rested on a shared dynastic understanding that general war produces revolution. The contribution must be stated alongside three competing explanations that owe it nothing: a generation of war left every participant exhausted; no power between 1815 and the 1850s combined the capacity and the motive to attempt continental domination; and the most dynamic European states directed their energies to industrialisation and to expansion outside Europe. The settlement also created no mechanism - no obligation, no procedure, no sanction, no organ - so what it bequeathed was a distribution and a habit rather than an institution. The cost was borne by those the settlement disposed of: populations transferred without consent in Belgium, Lombardy, Venetia and Congress Poland; constitutional movements suppressed by foreign armies in Naples, Piedmont and Spain; and opinion policed in the German states by the Carlsbad machinery of 20 September 1819. The correct verdict therefore refuses the choice the question usually offers, because the same great-power discipline that prevented war among states was used to prevent change within them, and the two are one mechanism with two outputs. Judged by its own criteria the settlement was a substantial success; judged by the criteria of the century that followed it mistook the absence of war for the presence of stability. And its own compensations armed the two states, Prussia and Sardinia-Piedmont, that dismantled its central European arrangements between 1859 and 1871.

**Misconception to avoid:** Producing a balanced-sounding conclusion without the specifications. "It was partly successful and partly a failure" is not a graded verdict; a graded verdict names the kind of peace, the parties, the interval, the competing causes and the specific cost, and it is the specification rather than the balance that earns the marks.

### Cumulative check E2 - the legacy question, handled without over-claiming

**Question:** Explain what the Concert of Europe genuinely bequeathed to later international order, what it did not, and why the difference is categorical rather than one of degree.

**Model answer:** It bequeathed four things. Conference diplomacy as a peacetime routine, established by Article VI of the Quadruple Alliance of 20 November 1815 and practised at four congresses and afterwards in the conference that settled Belgium. The joint guarantee as a technique for removing a status from the competition of powers, used for Swiss neutrality in 1815, for Greek independence under the London Protocol of 3 February 1830, and for Belgian neutrality in the Treaty of London of 19 April 1839. The consolidated general settlement, recorded in the Final Act of 9 June 1815, which makes any subsequent change identifiable as a change and therefore a matter for collective decision. And technical international law - the diplomatic ranks of 19 March 1815, the free navigation of international rivers, and the slave-trade declaration of 8 February 1815 - which survives in recognisable form. It did not bequeath any of the constitutive features of collective security: there was no charter, no defined membership with rules of admission, no permanent organ or secretariat, no obligation to act against a breach, no sanction for inaction, no decision procedure and no prohibition on the use of force, which the system in fact authorised three times between 1821 and 1823. Two absences make the difference categorical rather than one of degree. The Concert had no non-European member and no principle of the equality of states, so its organising premise was that a handful of European monarchies decide the affairs of the continent. And its declared purpose included the maintenance of monarchical authority against constitutional change, which is the opposite of the premise on which later organisations were founded. A system is not an early version of an institution designed to replace it. What the Concert did demonstrate, and what every later system has silently conceded, is that an arrangement the strongest powers do not accept will not hold - so its real bequest is the permanent problem of great-power management, together with the unanswered question of what sustains great-power restraint when the powers no longer share a fear.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that refusing the collective-security label means refusing the comparison. The comparison is where the marks are; the discipline is to concede the inherited method in full, refuse the institution with a specific list, and close on the portable proposition rather than on a flat denial.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

Six original questions, written to the directives this topic's answer architecture predicts. Each model observes the repository's word ceilings - 150 words at 10 marks, 250 words at 15 and 20 marks - and each carries a distinct scoring note. Attempt the question before reading the model.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

> Explain the principles on which the Congress of Vienna redrew the map of Europe, illustrating each with one territorial decision. (Answer in 150 words)

**Model answer:** Vienna applied three announced principles and one unannounced rule. Legitimacy made lawful dynastic right the test of a valid claim: the Bourbons were restored in France, and Frederick Augustus I was preserved as King of Saxony against Prussia's demand. Balance of power prevented any state dominating the continent: France was contained rather than destroyed, and Swiss neutrality was recognised on 20 November 1815. Compensation paid those who surrendered claims or bore the war's burden: Prussia received the Rhineland, Westphalia and part of Saxony, Austria took Lombardy and Venetia, Piedmont gained Genoa, and most of the Duchy of Warsaw became Congress Poland under the Tsar. The unannounced rule was containment, which alone explains the Kingdom of the Netherlands. The settlement's limit follows from its method: territory was treated as transferable power, so no principle spoke for the populations transferred - the omission that produced 1830 and 1848.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** at ten marks the marks sit in the pairing of principle with illustration, and the fourth consideration is what lifts the answer above the standard three-principle recital. Do not attempt the Concert, the congresses or the historiography here; there is no room, and adding them displaces the illustrations that carry the credit.

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

> Distinguish the Holy Alliance from the Concert of Europe, and explain why the distinction matters. (Answer in 150 words)

**Model answer:** The Holy Alliance, signed on 26 September 1815 by Russia, Austria and Prussia, declared that rulers would govern by Christian moral principles. Britain did not join - the Prince Regent declined to sign - and the Pope and the Sultan were excluded. It imposed no enforceable obligation; its function was to supply conservative policy with a moral vocabulary. The Concert of Europe was not an instrument but the practice of great-power consultation to preserve the status quo, working through periodic congresses. It included Britain throughout and France from 1818. The distinction matters for three reasons. It identifies the correct authorising instrument: the congresses arose from Article VI of the Quadruple Alliance of 20 November 1815, not from the Holy Alliance. It explains the split of 1820, since only three powers signed the Troppau Protocol. And it explains why the Concert outlived the congresses, settling Belgium in 1830.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** the directive is *distinguish and explain why it matters*, so a two-part structure is compulsory - the distinction alone answers half the question. The three reasons in the closing sentences are what convert a definitional answer into an analytical one, and each is a single clause because the word ceiling permits nothing more.

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

> "The Congress of Vienna created peace among states but not legitimacy among peoples." Examine. (Answer in 250 words)

**Model answer:** Both halves are accurate, and they share a cause. The peace claim, stated precisely, is that no general war was fought among the European great powers between 1815 and the Crimean War of 1853-56, an interval without parallel in the preceding century. The settlement contributed directly: France was contained by a barrier ring - the Netherlands, the Prussian Rhineland, neutral Switzerland, an enlarged Piedmont - then readmitted at Aix-la-Chapelle in 1818; territory was distributed so that no power found revision worth a general war; and the practice established under Article VI resolved the Belgian secession of 1830 and the Greek question of 1827-32 without war. The deficit has the same origin. The settlement's accounting unit was the province and the dynasty, so compensation transferred populations without consent - Belgians into a Dutch-dominated kingdom, Lombards and Venetians to Austria, Poles into a kingdom under the Russian crown, Germans into a Confederation of thirty-nine states. No principle available to the negotiators could state a national claim. The machinery built to contain it proves the powers understood it: the Carlsbad Decrees of 20 September 1819 censored the press, placed university curators, dissolved the Burschenschaften and created an investigating commission at Mainz. The verdict must refuse the implied contrast. The same great-power discipline that prevented war among states was used to prevent change within them; Vienna solved the French problem and postponed the nationality problem, and the interest on that postponement was paid in 1830, 1848 and the unifications of 1859-71.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** the examiner's test is whether the candidate treats the two clauses as opposed or as connected. Asserting that they share a cause, and then proving it from compensation and Carlsbad, is the move that distinguishes the answer; conceding the precise peace interval, including the Crimean terminus, protects the first clause from an obvious counter-example.

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

> Account for the divergence between Britain and the continental powers within the Concert of Europe. (Answer in 250 words)

**Model answer:** The divergence was structural and present from the beginning. Its clearest statement is Castlereagh's State Paper of 5 May 1820, which held that the alliance had been made against the military domination of Europe by France and was not a union for the government of the world or for the superintendence of other states' internal affairs. That paper preceded the Neapolitan revolution of July 1820 and the Troppau Protocol of 19 November 1820, so the British position was a prior refusal of the interventionist doctrine rather than a reaction to it. Four sources explain it. Constitutionally, no British ministry could bind Parliament or its successors to indefinite foreign intervention, and a doctrine treating constitutions extorted from monarchs as revolutionary condemned Britain's own history. Geographically, an island with naval command needed equilibrium, not protection, and equilibrium is maintained by occasional decisive action rather than permanent policing. Commercially, market access was indifferent to a customer's government, which is why Britain blocked the extension of intervention to Spanish America at Verona. Imperially, Britain's field of expansion lay outside Europe, so a cheaply quiet continent was the object of policy. The consequences are visible throughout: refusal of the Holy Alliance and of the Troppau Protocol, abstention at Laibach, Wellington's withdrawal from Verona on Canning's instructions in 1822. Yet Britain was no obstacle to the Concert as such - she co-authored Article VI, worked for a moderate peace with France, committed a fleet at Navarino and was central to the Belgian settlement of 1830.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** the directive *account for* requires causes rather than description, so the four structural sources must be named as causes and not merely listed as features. The chronological point - that the State Paper preceded Troppau - is the single detail that defeats the common explanation by Canning's personality, and the closing sentence is compulsory because an answer that presents Britain purely as a spoiler has used half the evidence.

## Question 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

> "The Vienna settlement was undone by forces it had itself created." Critically examine. (Answer in 250 words)

**Model answer:** The proposition is true in a specific sense and overstated as a general claim. Its strongest support lies in compensation. Prussia, refused the whole of Saxony on legitimist grounds, was paid instead with the Rhineland and Westphalia, acquiring the coal, iron and population of Germany's future industrial heartland and the position of the German power facing France - the material and patriotic basis of the primacy that dismantled the German Confederation between 1864 and 1871. Sardinia-Piedmont, enlarged by Genoa to block the approaches to Italy, became the only Italian state with the port, army and administration to serve as the nucleus of unification against Austria, whom the same settlement had installed in Lombardy and Venetia. The Kingdom of the Netherlands, created purely for containment, generated the Belgian secession of 1830. And the suppression of constitutional demand - Carlsbad in 1819, Naples and Piedmont in 1821, Spain in 1823 - denied moderates a legal route and drove them towards the national and radical movements that broke the order in 1848. The claim is overstated because forces the settlement did not create were equally decisive. Industrialisation, population growth and literacy transformed European society independently of any treaty. National feeling had been generated by the revolutionary and Napoleonic wars before 1815. And its collapse required the erosion of the shared conservative purpose, which the Greek crisis strained and 1848 destroyed. The accurate verdict is that Vienna armed its own gravediggers with territory and with grievance, while the weapons they used were forged elsewhere.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** *critically examine* requires the proposition to be tested rather than illustrated, so the answer must contain a genuine limitation and not merely a concessive clause. The four supports are arranged from strongest to weakest and the three counter-forces are arranged from most to least independent of the settlement, which is what allows the closing verdict to be graded rather than balanced.

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

> Evaluate the Concert of Europe as a system of international order, and assess its relevance to later attempts at collective security. (Answer in 250 words)

**Model answer:** As a system of order the Concert must be judged on two records. Its achievements were real: no general war among the great powers between 1815 and 1853-56; a defeated power readmitted at Aix-la-Chapelle within three years of Waterloo; a secession at Brussels in 1830 absorbed by conference and secured by the guarantee of Belgian neutrality in 1839; and an Eastern crisis closed by tripartite action at Navarino in 1827 and by the settlement of Greek independence in 1830 and 1832. Its record of repression is equally documented: the Troppau Protocol of 19 November 1820, Austrian intervention in Naples and Piedmont in 1821, French intervention in Spain in 1823, and the Carlsbad machinery of 1819. The two records are one mechanism with two outputs, since the discipline that restrained the powers abroad was exercised to restrain their subjects at home. Its relevance to collective security must be stated exactly. It bequeathed method - conference diplomacy, the joint guarantee, the consolidated general settlement, and technical international law on diplomatic ranks, river navigation and the slave trade. It bequeathed no institution: no charter, membership rule, organ, obligation, sanction, decision procedure or prohibition on force, and it authorised force three times in three years. Two absences make the difference categorical - no non-European member and no equality of states, and a declared purpose that included preventing constitutional government. The Concert is therefore an ancestor of method and not of institution, and its durable legacy is the unsolved problem of great-power management.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** the question contains two directives, *evaluate* and *assess*, and the two halves must be given roughly equal weight; an answer that spends 200 words on the Concert's record and 50 on the legacy has answered one. The categorical-difference argument in the closing sentences is the highest-value element, because it interrogates the framing the question offers instead of accepting it.

# REMEDIATION

## Error map

The forty errors below are the ones this topic actually generates. Each is paired with the correction and the lesson that establishes it.

| # | ❌ Error | ✅ Correction | Established in |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | "The Congress of Vienna imposed an indemnity on France" | The indemnity of 700 million francs was imposed by the second Treaty of Paris, 20 November 1815 | Lesson 1 |
| 2 | "The Congress of Vienna occupied France" | The allied occupation of up to five years was provided by the second Treaty of Paris; it ended in 1818 | Lesson 1 |
| 3 | "The congress was held in 1815" | It ran from late September 1814 to June 1815; some accounts date a formal opening to November 1814 | Lesson 1 |
| 4 | "The Final Act was signed in 1814" | 9 June 1815, nine days before Waterloo | Lesson 1 |
| 5 | "The congress ended when Napoleon returned" | It completed its work during the Hundred Days and declared him an outlaw on 13 March 1815 | Lesson 1 |
| 6 | "France was excluded from the settlement" | France was represented throughout by Talleyrand and readmitted to the directorate in 1818 | Lessons 2, 6 |
| 7 | "Chaumont was a peace treaty" | Chaumont of 1 March 1814 was an alliance of twenty years among the four victors, not a peace with France | Lesson 1 |
| 8 | "The first Paris treaty punished France" | It imposed no indemnity and no occupation and left her the 1792 frontiers | Lesson 1 |
| 9 | "The congress voted on its decisions" | It never sat as a deciding body; business ran through great-power committees | Lesson 2 |
| 10 | "Metternich decided the settlement" | He was dominant in central Europe; Castlereagh, Alexander I and Hardenberg pursued their own objectives and Talleyrand forced France into the centre | Lessons 2, 13 |
| 11 | "Vienna restored the old order" | It restored dynasties selectively; the Holy Roman Empire, the Venetian and Genoese republics and the ecclesiastical states were not restored | Lesson 3 |
| 12 | "Legitimacy was applied consistently" | It protected the King of Saxony and did nothing for Poland, which had no dynasty to restore | Lessons 3, 5 |
| 13 | "Compensation was a minor administrative rule" | It was the mechanism that transferred populations without consent, and the source of the settlement's grievances | Lesson 3 |
| 14 | "Three principles governed the map" | Three were announced; containment, never printed, decided the cases the three left open | Lesson 3 |
| 15 | "Belgium was restored to Austria" | Belgium was joined to Holland in the Kingdom of the Netherlands, an arrangement with no dynastic precedent | Lesson 4 |
| 16 | "The German Confederation was a German state" | It was an association of thirty-nine sovereign states with a Diet at Frankfurt under Austrian presidency | Lesson 4 |
| 17 | "The Confederation had a number of states other than 39" | Thirty-nine, created by the German Federal Act of 8 June 1815 | Lesson 4 |
| 18 | "Austria ruled Italy" | Austria held Lombardy and Venetia directly and influenced much of the peninsula | Lesson 4 |
| 19 | "Poland was abolished" | A Kingdom of Poland was created under the Russian emperor as its king, with its own constitution and administration | Lessons 4, 5 |
| 20 | "Piedmont was enlarged to prepare Italian unification" | Genoa was added to make Piedmont a barrier state; the unification consequence was unintended | Lessons 4, 5 |
| 21 | "Poland and Saxony were separate disputes" | They were one transaction: Russian Poland required Prussia's surrender of Polish lands, and Saxony was the payment | Lesson 5 |
| 22 | "Prussia was denied Saxony" | Prussia received part of Saxony; the king retained the remainder | Lesson 5 |
| 23 | "War nearly broke out over Poland" | What is recorded is the secret defensive alliance of 3 January 1815 and the compromise that followed; no mobilisation is asserted | Lesson 5 |
| 24 | "Vienna succeeded where Versailles failed" | Vienna handled the defeated power better and the subject peoples worse, for the same reason - its criterion of legitimacy | Lesson 6 |
| 25 | "Britain joined the Holy Alliance" | The Prince Regent declined to sign; the Holy Alliance was Russia, Austria and Prussia | Lesson 7 |
| 26 | "The Holy Alliance created the congress system" | Article VI of the Quadruple Alliance of 20 November 1815 provided for periodic meetings | Lesson 7 |
| 27 | "The Holy Alliance is the Concert of Europe" | The Holy Alliance was a symbolic declaration of three monarchs; the Concert was the broader practice of great-power consultation | Lesson 7 |
| 28 | "France founded the Quadruple Alliance" | France was added at Aix-la-Chapelle in 1818, making it a Quintuple Alliance | Lesson 7 |
| 29 | "The Concert was created by treaty" | It was a practice, not an instrument; it had no charter, membership rule or organ | Lessons 7, 17 |
| 30 | "All five powers signed the Troppau Protocol" | Austria, Prussia and Russia signed; Britain and France refused and attended as observers | Lessons 8, 9 |
| 31 | "Canning caused the split with the continental powers" | Castlereagh's State Paper of 5 May 1820 stated the British position before Troppau and before Canning took office in 1822 | Lessons 9, 12 |
| 32 | "Britain left the Concert at Verona" | Wellington withdrew from that congress on Canning's instructions; Britain remained central to great-power consultation, including the Belgian settlement | Lessons 8, 12 |
| 33 | "Britain's position was a principle of non-intervention" | She refused a standing obligation and reserved each case, which is why she acted for Greece in 1827 | Lessons 9, 11 |
| 34 | "The 1820-21 movements were republican risings" | They were constitutionalist, led by officers, professional men and sections of the nobility | Lesson 10 |
| 35 | "Austria intervened in Spain" | France intervened in Spain in 1823, authorised at Verona; Austria intervened in Naples and Piedmont in 1821 | Lesson 10 |
| 36 | "Britain enforced the Monroe Doctrine" | The British veto at Verona and the Monroe Doctrine of December 1823 were independent acts with a common effect | Lesson 11 |
| 37 | "The Carlsbad Decrees were issued by the Congress of Vienna" | They were adopted by the German Confederation on 20 September 1819 | Lesson 13 |
| 38 | "The Vienna Final Act is the Congress's general treaty" | There are two: the Congress's Final Act of 9 June 1815 and the Confederation's Vienna Final Act of 15 May 1820 | Lesson 13 |
| 39 | "The powers intervened against the French revolution of 1830" | They did not; intervention in France meant war with a great power, and the new king was accepted | Lesson 14 |
| 40 | "Vienna gave Europe a century of peace" | No general war among the great powers between 1815 and the Crimean War of 1853-56; the claim fails in any looser form | Lesson 15 |

## Corrective drills

Each drill states the error, the diagnosis and the rewritten sentence. Work through them by covering the right-hand column.

**Drill 1 - instrument attribution.** ❌ "At Vienna the powers forced France to pay a huge indemnity and accept occupation." ⚠️ Diagnosis: the wrong instrument, and therefore the wrong date and the wrong explanation. ✅ Rewrite: "The second Treaty of Paris of 20 November 1815 imposed an indemnity of 700 million francs and an allied occupation of up to five years, terms adopted only after the Hundred Days had shown the leniency of 1814 to be unsafe."

**Drill 2 - the dating convention.** ❌ "The Congress of Vienna opened on 1 November 1814." ⚠️ Diagnosis: asserting a precision the event does not permit, since the congress never held a single opening plenary. ✅ Rewrite: "Delegates assembled from late September 1814 and the congress ran to June 1815; a formal opening in November is given in some accounts, and exact days belong to the instruments rather than to the congress."

**Drill 3 - the decision method.** ❌ "The assembled states of Europe voted the settlement into existence." ⚠️ Diagnosis: importing a parliamentary model into a great-power conference. ✅ Rewrite: "The congress never sat as a deciding body; questions were settled in committees of the powers concerned and consolidated into the Final Act, which is the origin of the great-power practice later called the Concert."

**Drill 4 - Talleyrand's leverage.** ❌ "Talleyrand persuaded the powers to treat France generously." ⚠️ Diagnosis: attributing an outcome to persuasion when the mechanism was a quarrel exploited. ✅ Rewrite: "Talleyrand supplied Austria and Britain with the legitimist argument they needed against Russia's Polish and Prussia's Saxon demands, and on 3 January 1815 France joined them in a secret defensive alliance - which is how the defeated power entered the inner circle."

**Drill 5 - the missing principle.** ❌ "Vienna applied legitimacy, balance and compensation." ⚠️ Diagnosis: true but incomplete, and the incompleteness makes several decisions inexplicable. ✅ Rewrite: "Vienna announced legitimacy, balance and compensation, and operated a fourth rule it never printed - the containment of France - which alone explains the Kingdom of the Netherlands, the placing of Prussia on the Rhine and the enlargement of Piedmont."

**Drill 6 - restoration, stated exactly.** ❌ "The settlement restored the old order." ⚠️ Diagnosis: a slogan that the map refutes. ✅ Rewrite: "The settlement restored dynasties selectively while keeping Napoleon's consolidation of territory: the Holy Roman Empire was not revived, Venice and Genoa were not restored as republics, and the ecclesiastical principalities did not return."

**Drill 7 - the Netherlands.** ❌ "Belgium was given to Holland as a reward." ⚠️ Diagnosis: the wrong category - this was not compensation. ✅ Rewrite: "Belgium was joined to Holland to close France's invasion route to the Channel with a state whose coast Britain could supply; the union ignored the religious, linguistic and economic division of its two halves and was broken by the Belgians in 1830."

**Drill 8 - the Rhineland.** ❌ "Prussia was compensated with the Rhineland, a minor gain." ⚠️ Diagnosis: missing the most consequential decision of the settlement. ✅ Rewrite: "Prussia, refused the whole of Saxony, was compensated with the Rhineland and Westphalia, which placed the most militarised German state on the French frontier and, unintentionally, gave her the industrial base of later German primacy."

**Drill 9 - the Confederation.** ❌ "Vienna created a weak German state." ⚠️ Diagnosis: a category error. ✅ Rewrite: "The German Federal Act of 8 June 1815 created a Confederation of thirty-nine sovereign states with a Diet at Frankfurt under Austrian presidency - not a state, but the only design compatible with containment, balance and legitimacy at once."

**Drill 10 - Austria in Italy.** ❌ "Austria dominated Italy as she dominated Germany." ⚠️ Diagnosis: the same word covering two different mechanisms. ✅ Rewrite: "In Germany Austria's predominance was constitutional - a presidency in an assembly of sovereigns - and could be contested politically; in Italy it was territorial, resting on the direct possession of Lombardy and Venetia, and could be contested only by war."

**Drill 11 - Poland.** ❌ "Poland was handed over to Russia." ⚠️ Diagnosis: imprecise, and the imprecision weakens the criticism. ✅ Rewrite: "Most of the Duchy of Warsaw became Congress Poland, a kingdom with its own constitution and administration whose king was the Russian emperor - a form of subordination more damaging to the settlement's claims than outright annexation would have been."

**Drill 12 - the linked crisis.** ❌ "The powers quarrelled over Poland, and separately over Saxony." ⚠️ Diagnosis: severing the link that makes the crisis intelligible. ✅ Rewrite: "Poland and Saxony were one transaction, because a Russian Poland required Prussia to surrender her Polish lands and Saxony was the compensation - which is why opposing either demand meant opposing both."

**Drill 13 - the secret treaty.** ❌ "In January 1815 Europe was on the brink of a new war." ⚠️ Diagnosis: asserting more than the evidence supports. ✅ Rewrite: "On 3 January 1815 Britain, Austria and France signed a secret defensive alliance against Prussia and Russia, intended to become known rather than to be used; the compromise on Poland and Saxony followed."

**Drill 14 - the two treaties with France.** ❌ "The victors were harsh with France." ⚠️ Diagnosis: one treaty mistaken for the settlement. ✅ Rewrite: "The victors were lenient in 1814 and firmer in 1815, and both sets of terms served the same object: France kept her great-power status, her dynasty and her army throughout, the indemnity was finite, the occupation was bounded, and she was readmitted to great-power consultation in 1818."

**Drill 15 - the 1919 comparison.** ❌ "Vienna succeeded where Versailles failed." ⚠️ Diagnosis: a verdict masquerading as a comparison. ✅ Rewrite: "Vienna handled the defeated power better - present at the negotiation, finite terms, readmission within three years - and handled the subject peoples worse, and both follow from its criterion of dynastic legitimacy."

**Drill 16 - the alliances.** ❌ "The Holy Alliance bound the powers to intervene against revolution." ⚠️ Diagnosis: the wrong instrument again, and the error that this topic tests most often. ✅ Rewrite: "The Holy Alliance of 26 September 1815 bound Russia, Austria and Prussia to nothing enforceable; the claim of a right to intervene was made in the Troppau Protocol of 19 November 1820, under meetings provided for by Article VI of the Quadruple Alliance."

**Drill 17 - Article VI.** ❌ "The Quadruple Alliance created an institution for maintaining peace." ⚠️ Diagnosis: reading an institution into a clause. ✅ Rewrite: "Article VI provided for periodic meetings and said nothing about their competence, which is why the powers could disagree fundamentally in 1820 about whether those meetings existed to manage relations between states or revolutions inside them."

**Drill 18 - the readmission.** ❌ "Aix-la-Chapelle was a routine follow-up congress." ⚠️ Diagnosis: missing both the achievement and the consequence. ✅ Rewrite: "Aix-la-Chapelle in 1818 ended the occupation, settled the indemnity and admitted France - the system's high point, and the moment at which the alliance lost the adversary that had supplied its purpose."

**Drill 19 - Troppau's membership.** ❌ "At Troppau the powers asserted a right of intervention." ⚠️ Diagnosis: "the powers" conceals the split that is the point. ✅ Rewrite: "At Troppau three powers - Austria, Prussia and Russia - asserted the right in the Protocol of 19 November 1820, while Britain and France refused to accept or sign it and attended as observers."

**Drill 20 - the British paper.** ❌ "Britain replied to Troppau with the State Paper." ⚠️ Diagnosis: reversing the chronology and therefore the argument. ✅ Rewrite: "Castlereagh's State Paper of 5 May 1820 preceded both the Neapolitan revolution of July and the Troppau Protocol of November, so the British refusal was a prior position rather than a reaction."

**Drill 21 - the character of the movements.** ❌ "Revolutionaries tried to overthrow the monarchies of southern Europe." ⚠️ Diagnosis: misdescribing the demand, and thereby missing why the powers feared it. ✅ Rewrite: "The movements of 1820-21 demanded written constitutions limiting their monarchs, commonly on the Spanish model of 1812, and were led by army officers, professional men and sections of the nobility - which is exactly why they could not be dismissed as criminal fanaticism."

**Drill 22 - the intervening powers.** ❌ "The Holy Alliance invaded Naples and Spain." ⚠️ Diagnosis: two errors in one clause - the wrong agent and a concealed distinction. ✅ Rewrite: "Austria intervened in Naples and Piedmont in 1821 under the authorisation of Laibach, and France intervened in Spain in 1823 under the authorisation of Verona; in each case the army belonged to the power with the strongest local interest."

**Drill 23 - the verdict on intervention.** ❌ "The interventions failed to stop the spread of revolution." ⚠️ Diagnosis: the opposite of what happened, and the error erases the topic's central difficulty. ✅ Rewrite: "The interventions succeeded completely wherever they were attempted and destroyed the system that carried them out, since Britain's dissent made the congresses unusable after Verona."

**Drill 24 - Spanish America.** ❌ "The Monroe Doctrine stopped European intervention in Latin America." ⚠️ Diagnosis: a single cause where there were two independent ones. ✅ Rewrite: "Britain blocked the extension of intervention at Verona and possessed the navy that any transatlantic expedition would have had to pass; the Monroe Doctrine of December 1823 added an independent American warning, and the two together closed the question."

**Drill 25 - Greece.** ❌ "The powers intervened in Greece out of sympathy for the Greek cause." ⚠️ Diagnosis: the motive without the mechanism. ✅ Rewrite: "Sympathy supplied the political permission; the mechanism was the Eastern question - the Sultan stood outside the European monarchical circle, and joint action at Navarino in 1827 allowed Britain and France to bound the gains Russia would otherwise have taken alone."

**Drill 26 - Greek independence.** ❌ "Greece became independent at Navarino in 1827." ⚠️ Diagnosis: a battle mistaken for a settlement. ✅ Rewrite: "Navarino on 20 October 1827 destroyed the Ottoman-Egyptian fleet; independence was recognised by the London Protocol of 3 February 1830 and completed by the Treaty of Constantinople of 1832, with Otto of Bavaria as king."

**Drill 27 - Britain's identity.** ❌ "Britain was the liberal power in the Concert." ⚠️ Diagnosis: importing an ideology where the causes were structural. ✅ Rewrite: "Britain's refusal was constitutional and jurisdictional rather than liberal - the same ministry was suppressing radical agitation at home - and rested on four structural sources: the constitution, the island position, commerce and an extra-European field of expansion."

**Drill 28 - the Carlsbad target.** ❌ "The Carlsbad Decrees suppressed the German working class." ⚠️ Diagnosis: the wrong target, and therefore the wrong theory of the period. ✅ Rewrite: "The Carlsbad Decrees of 20 September 1819 targeted the institutions in which educated opinion was formed - the press, the universities and the student associations - and added a central investigating commission at Mainz."

**Drill 29 - the two Final Acts.** ❌ "The Vienna Final Act completed the European settlement in 1820." ⚠️ Diagnosis: two instruments merged. ✅ Rewrite: "The Final Act of the Congress of Vienna of 9 June 1815 consolidated the European settlement; the Vienna Final Act of 15 May 1820 completed the German Confederation's own constitution and permitted federal intervention against revolutionary activity."

**Drill 30 - the order of the doctrines.** ❌ "The Carlsbad system applied the Troppau doctrine inside Germany." ⚠️ Diagnosis: the chronology reversed. ✅ Rewrite: "Carlsbad in September 1819 preceded Troppau by fourteen months, so the European doctrine of collective intervention against internal change generalised a German practice rather than the reverse."

**Drill 31 - the Age of Metternich.** ❌ "Metternich controlled Europe for thirty years." ⚠️ Diagnosis: a system mistaken for a person. ✅ Rewrite: "The Age of Metternich names a system with Austria at its centre and Metternich as its most articulate exponent; Britain refused its doctrine throughout and Russia pursued her own priorities in the Eastern question."

**Drill 32 - 1830 in France.** ❌ "The Concert crushed the revolutions of 1830." ⚠️ Diagnosis: a generalisation that three cases contradict. ✅ Rewrite: "In 1830 the Concert accepted a new king in France, accepted the secession of Belgium, and allowed Russia to crush the Polish rising - the pattern of a system choosing by capability rather than by principle."

**Drill 33 - Belgium and Poland.** ❌ "The powers supported Belgian independence because they had come to accept nationality." ⚠️ Diagnosis: a liberal reading of a conservative operation. ✅ Rewrite: "The powers conceded a fact they would not fight over and then removed it from competition by guaranteeing Belgian neutrality in the Treaty of London of 19 April 1839 - while allowing an identical national rising in Poland to be crushed, because that one lay inside a great power's sphere."

**Drill 34 - 1848.** ❌ "The revolutions of 1848 destroyed the Vienna settlement." ⚠️ Diagnosis: collapsing two stages into one. ✅ Rewrite: "1848 broke the order's authority and removed Metternich in March, but the revolutions themselves failed; the settlement's central European arrangements were dismantled afterwards, between 1859 and 1871, by states the settlement had itself strengthened."

**Drill 35 - why 1848 failed.** ❌ "The revolutions of 1848 failed because the powers suppressed them." ⚠️ Diagnosis: attributing to the system what the revolutions did to themselves. ✅ Rewrite: "They failed because their coalitions contained incompatible constitutional, national and social demands, because national claims were mutually destructive in multinational regions, because the dynastic armies remained intact and obedient, and because liberal nationalism had no instrument of force."

**Drill 36 - the peace claim.** ❌ "Vienna gave Europe a hundred years of peace." ⚠️ Diagnosis: a claim that a single date demolishes. ✅ Rewrite: "No general war was fought among the European great powers between 1815 and the Crimean War of 1853-56 - an interval without parallel in the century before it, and the only form of the claim that survives cross-examination."

**Drill 37 - the causes of the peace.** ❌ "The Concert kept the peace for forty years." ⚠️ Diagnosis: a single cause claimed for a multiply-caused outcome. ✅ Rewrite: "The settlement contributed substantially to the long interval, alongside post-war exhaustion, the absence of any power capable of attempting continental domination, and the diversion of European energies into industrialisation and extra-European expansion."

**Drill 38 - the Crimean terminus.** ❌ "The Crimean War destroyed the Concert of Europe." ⚠️ Diagnosis: the causation reversed. ✅ Rewrite: "The Concert did not end because a war broke out; a war broke out because the shared conservative purpose that made consultation work had been eroded by 1830 and destroyed by 1848."

**Drill 39 - the historiography.** ❌ "Historians now agree that Vienna was a success." ⚠️ Diagnosis: a live debate presented as settled. ✅ Rewrite: "The reading has moved from reaction, in the manner of Nicolson's 1946 narrative, towards equilibrium in Webster and in Kissinger's A World Restored of 1957, and further still in Schroeder's transformation thesis of 1994 - but the criticism that the settlement contained rather than resolved national demands remains standing."

**Drill 40 - the legacy.** ❌ "The Concert of Europe was an early United Nations." ⚠️ Diagnosis: method mistaken for institution. ✅ Rewrite: "The Concert bequeathed conference diplomacy, the joint guarantee, the consolidated general settlement and technical international law, and bequeathed no charter, membership rule, organ, obligation, sanction or prohibition on force - and its declared purpose included preventing constitutional government, which makes the difference categorical."

# MASTER COMPARISON, CAUSAL AND ARGUMENT MAPS

## Master chronology

```text
 1814  1 MARCH      Treaty of Chaumont - Britain, Austria, Prussia, Russia;
                    20-year alliance; 150,000 men each; no separate peace
       6 APRIL      Napoleon's first abdication
       30 MAY       FIRST TREATY OF PARIS - France to 1792 frontiers;
                    Louis XVIII restored; no indemnity, no occupation;
                    Article 32 summons a general congress at Vienna
       LATE SEPT    Delegates assemble at Vienna; some accounts date a
                    formal opening to November 1814
 -------------------------------------------------------------------------
 1815  3 JANUARY    SECRET TREATY - Britain, Austria, France; defensive
                    alliance against Prussia and Russia over Poland-Saxony
       8 FEBRUARY   Declaration on the abolition of the slave trade
       1 MARCH      Napoleon lands in France - the Hundred Days begin
       13 MARCH     The Congress declares Napoleon an outlaw
       19 MARCH     Regulation on diplomatic precedence - three classes;
                    precedence by date of notification
       8 JUNE       GERMAN FEDERAL ACT - Confederation of 39 states;
                    Diet at Frankfurt; Austrian presidency
       9 JUNE       FINAL ACT OF THE CONGRESS OF VIENNA (General Treaty)
       18 JUNE      Waterloo
       26 SEPT      HOLY ALLIANCE - Russia, Austria, Prussia; Britain
                    declines; Pope and Ottoman Sultan excluded
       20 NOV       SECOND TREATY OF PARIS - 1790 frontiers; 700 million
                    francs; occupation up to five years
       20 NOV       QUADRUPLE ALLIANCE - Britain, Austria, Prussia,
                    Russia; ARTICLE VI provides periodic meetings
       20 NOV       Swiss perpetual neutrality recognised and guaranteed
 -------------------------------------------------------------------------
 1818               CONGRESS OF AIX-LA-CHAPELLE - indemnity settled;
                    occupation ended early; FRANCE ADMITTED -> Quintuple
 1819  23 MARCH     August von Kotzebue assassinated by Karl Sand
       20 SEPT      CARLSBAD DECREES - press censorship; university
                    curators; Burschenschaften dissolved; Central
                    Investigation Commission at Mainz
 -------------------------------------------------------------------------
 1820  JANUARY      Revolution in Spain
       5 MAY        BRITISH STATE PAPER - the alliance was made against
                    the military domination of Europe by France
       15 MAY       Vienna Final Act of the GERMAN CONFEDERATION -
                    federal constitution completed; federal intervention
                    against revolutionary activity permitted
       JULY         Revolution in Naples
       AUGUST       Revolution in Portugal
       19 NOV       TROPPAU PROTOCOL - Austria, Prussia, Russia;
                    Britain and France refuse
 1821  MARCH        Revolution in Piedmont
                    CONGRESS OF LAIBACH - Austrian intervention in
                    Naples and Piedmont authorised and executed
                    Greek War of Independence begins
 1822  AUGUST       Castlereagh dies; Canning succeeds
       OCT-DEC      CONGRESS OF VERONA - French intervention in Spain
                    authorised; Wellington withdraws on Canning's
                    instructions; the last major effective congress
 1823  APRIL        French intervention in Spain - the Hundred Thousand
                    Sons of Saint Louis; Ferdinand VII's absolute
                    authority restored
       DECEMBER     MONROE DOCTRINE warns Europe against intervention
                    in the Americas
 -------------------------------------------------------------------------
 1827  1827         Britain, France and Russia agree on intervention
       20 OCTOBER   NAVARINO - the Ottoman-Egyptian fleet destroyed
 1829               TREATY OF ADRIANOPLE ends the Russo-Turkish war
 1830  3 FEBRUARY   LONDON PROTOCOL - Greece recognised as fully
                    independent and sovereign
       JULY         Revolution in France; the new king accepted
       AUGUST       Belgian revolt; independence accepted by the Concert
       NOV          Polish rising begins; crushed by Russia in 1831
                    with no great-power intervention
 1832               TREATY OF CONSTANTINOPLE completes Greek
                    recognition; Otto of Bavaria becomes king
 -------------------------------------------------------------------------
 1839  19 APRIL     TREATY OF LONDON guarantees Belgian neutrality
                    and independence
 1848  MARCH        METTERNICH FALLS and flees; revolutions across
                    Europe; the settlement's deepest challenge
 1853-56            CRIMEAN WAR - the first general war among great
                    powers since 1815; the terminus of the long peace
 1859-71            Italian and German unification dismantle the
                    settlement's central European arrangements
```

## The causal engine

```text
  TWENTY-THREE YEARS OF WAR (1792-1815)
  teaches the victors that DEFEATING a hegemon is possible but
  ruinous - so the object becomes a settlement that need not be
  defended by another such war
                     |
                     v
  FOUR OPERATING CONSIDERATIONS
  legitimacy (who may rule) | balance (how much any may hold)
  compensation (how the bargain closes) | containment (where it goes)
                     |
       +-------------+--------------+
       |                            |
       v                            v
  A MAP built on PROVINCES     A PRACTICE built on GREAT-POWER
  and DYNASTIES                CONSULTATION
  -> populations transferred   -> Article VI creates periodic
     without consent              meetings with UNDEFINED competence
       |                            |
       v                            v
  GRIEVANCE with a specific    DISAGREEMENT about what the
  address: Belgians, Poles,    alliance is FOR - a map, or a
  Italians, Germans            regime type
       |                            |
       |                            v
       |                     1820: TROPPAU vs THE STATE PAPER
       |                     the split follows from each power's
       |                     OWN FORM OF GOVERNMENT
       |                            |
       |                     +------+-------+
       |                     |              |
       |              INTERVENTION     BRITAIN REFUSES
       |              Naples, Piedmont  -> congresses unusable
       |              1821; Spain 1823  -> every challenger gains
       |                     |              a potential patron
       |                     v              |
       |              MODERATES LOSE        |
       |              THEIR LEGAL ROUTE     |
       |                     |              |
       +----------+----------+--------------+
                  |
                  v
        THE COALITION OF 1848: constitutional + national + social
                  |
       +----------+----------+
       |                     |
  ORDER SURVIVES       AUTHORITY DOES NOT
  (revolutions'        (Metternich falls, March 1848)
  own divisions;             |
  armies intact)             v
       |               1859-71: PRUSSIA and PIEDMONT - the two
       |               states VIENNA'S OWN COMPENSATIONS created -
       |               dismantle the central European settlement
       +---------------------+
                  |
                  v
        WHAT SURVIVES: not the order, but the METHOD -
        conference, joint guarantee, consolidated settlement,
        and the permanent problem of great-power management
```

## The instruments, in one table

| Instrument | ✅ Date | ✅ Parties | ✅ What it did |
|---|---|---|---|
| Treaty of Chaumont | 1 March 1814 | Britain, Austria, Prussia, Russia | 20-year alliance; 150,000 men each; no separate peace |
| First Treaty of Paris | 30 May 1814 | France and the coalition | 1792 frontiers; Louis XVIII; no indemnity or occupation; Article 32 summons the congress |
| Secret treaty | 3 January 1815 | Britain, Austria, France | Defensive alliance against Prussia and Russia over Poland-Saxony |
| Slave-trade declaration | 8 February 1815 | The congress | Condemns the trade; no enforcement machinery |
| Outlawry declaration | 13 March 1815 | The congress | Places Napoleon outside the protection of law |
| Diplomatic precedence regulation | 19 March 1815 | The congress | Three classes of agent; precedence by date of notification |
| German Federal Act | 8 June 1815 | German states | Confederation of 39; Diet at Frankfurt; Austrian presidency |
| Final Act of the Congress | 9 June 1815 | The congress | Consolidates all agreements into one general treaty |
| Holy Alliance | 26 September 1815 | Russia, Austria, Prussia | Declaration of Christian moral principles; no enforceable obligation |
| Second Treaty of Paris | 20 November 1815 | France and the allies | 1790 frontiers; 700 million francs; occupation up to five years |
| Quadruple Alliance | 20 November 1815 | Britain, Austria, Prussia, Russia | Maintains the settlement; **Article VI** provides periodic meetings |
| Swiss neutrality declaration | 20 November 1815 | The powers | Recognises and guarantees perpetual neutrality |
| Carlsbad Decrees | 20 September 1819 | German Confederation | Censorship; university curators; Burschenschaften dissolved; Mainz commission |
| British State Paper | 5 May 1820 | Britain | Refuses a standing obligation to superintend other states' internal affairs |
| Vienna Final Act (German) | 15 May 1820 | German Confederation | Completes the federal constitution; permits federal intervention |
| Troppau Protocol | 19 November 1820 | Austria, Prussia, Russia | A state altered by revolution leaves the alliance and may be restored by force |
| Monroe Doctrine | December 1823 | United States | Warns Europe against intervention in the Americas |
| London Protocol | 3 February 1830 | Britain, France, Russia | Recognises Greece as fully independent and sovereign |
| Treaty of Constantinople | 1832 | The powers and the Ottomans | Completes Greek recognition; fixes borders; Otto of Bavaria as king |
| Treaty of London | 19 April 1839 | The powers | Guarantees Belgian neutrality and independence |

## The four alliances and the Concert, in one table

| | Holy Alliance | Quadruple Alliance | Quintuple Alliance | Concert of Europe |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Date | 26 Sept 1815 | 20 Nov 1815 | From 1818 | No date - a practice |
| Members | Russia, Austria, Prussia | Britain, Austria, Prussia, Russia | The four plus France | The recognised great powers |
| Nature | Moral declaration | Treaty of alliance | The same, enlarged | Habit of consultation |
| Obligation | None enforceable | Armed support; Article VI meetings | Same | None |
| Britain | Declined to sign | Full member | Full member | Full participant |
| Practical weight | Rhetorical | Operative | Operative, purpose diluted | The system as experienced |

## The four congresses, in one table

| Congress | ✅ Year | ✅ Question | ✅ Outcome | ⚠️ Effect |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Aix-la-Chapelle | 1818 | The end of the occupation | Occupation ended; France admitted | Peak of consensus; adversary removed |
| Troppau | 1820 | Revolution in Naples and Spain | Troppau Protocol; three sign, two refuse | Doctrinal split opens |
| Laibach | 1821 | Executing the Troppau principle | Austrian intervention in Naples and Piedmont | Split acted on |
| Verona | 1822 | Revolution in Spain | French intervention authorised; Wellington withdraws | Rupture; last effective congress |

## Argument map: peace or repression?

```text
  CLAIM: the Vienna order was an instrument of peace
  --------------------------------------------------
  EVIDENCE  no general war among great powers 1815 to 1853-56
            France readmitted within three years of Waterloo
            Belgium 1830 settled by conference, guaranteed 1839
            Greece 1827-32 settled collectively, not unilaterally
            technical law: diplomatic ranks, rivers, slave trade

  CLAIM: the Vienna order was an instrument of repression
  -------------------------------------------------------
  EVIDENCE  Carlsbad Decrees 1819 - press, universities, students
            Troppau Protocol 1820 - intervention doctrine
            Naples and Piedmont 1821; Spain 1823
            Congress Poland; Polish rising crushed 1831
            Vienna Final Act 1820 - federal intervention in Germany

  THE MOVE THAT RESOLVES IT
  -------------------------
  These are not two systems. They are ONE MECHANISM with TWO
  OUTPUTS: great-power discipline, exercised outward against war
  and inward against constitutional change.
  -> VERDICT: "the same great-power discipline that prevented war
     among states was used to prevent change within them"

  WHAT THE VERDICT MUST STILL CONCEDE
  -----------------------------------
  the alternative to a great-power peace was not a liberal Europe
  but a great-power war - and the wars of 1792-1815 had killed on
  a scale no censorship regime approaches
```
