## Argument map: the Concert and collective security

```text
  INHERITED (method)                 NEVER PRESENT (institution)
  ------------------                 ---------------------------
  conference diplomacy               charter or constituent instrument
  the joint guarantee                defined membership with rules
    Switzerland 1815                 permanent organ or secretariat
    Greece 1830                      obligation to act
    Belgium 1839                     sanction for inaction
  consolidated general settlement    decision procedure
  technical international law        prohibition on the use of force
    ranks 1815, rivers, slave trade  non-European membership
                                     sovereign equality
                                     a purpose compatible with
                                       self-determination
  ---------------------------------------------------------------
  THE TWO THAT MAKE THE DIFFERENCE CATEGORICAL
  no non-European member + a declared purpose that included
  preventing constitutional government
  -> the Concert is the arrangement later organisations were
     designed to REPLACE, not an early version of them
  ---------------------------------------------------------------
  THE PORTABLE PROPOSITION
  an arrangement the strongest powers do not accept will not hold
  -> great-power management is a permanent problem, and the
     Concert's restraint rested on a shared memory of catastrophic
     war and a shared fear of revolution
```

## Vienna against later settlements

| Dimension | ✅ Vienna, 1814-18 | ✅ The post-1919 settlement | ⚠️ Discrimination to make |
|---|---|---|---|
| Criterion of legitimacy | Dynastic right | National self-determination | Each criterion decides some cases and cannot decide others |
| The defeated power | Present, represented, readmitted 1818 | Not a negotiating party | A real difference of method, and Vienna's strongest point |
| Financial terms | 700 million francs, met early | Fixed later, contested, renegotiated | Finite and payable matters more than the size |
| Machinery | None - a practice only | An organisation with organs and obligations | Vienna declined institutionalisation on principle |
| Durability among great powers | No general war 1815 to 1853-56 | A second general war within 21 years | Partly a comparison of conditions, not only of skill |
| ⚠️ Verdict | Better on the defeated power, worse on subject peoples - and both follow from the same criterion | | ❌ Never "Vienna succeeded where Versailles failed" as a bare claim |

## Answer-writing spine

```text
  1. READ THE DIRECTIVE. Explain? Examine? How far? Evaluate?
     Each has a different demand and a different failure mode.
  2. OPEN WITH A THESIS CONTAINING A NEGATION.
     "Vienna succeeded at the objective it set itself and failed
      at the one it refused to acknowledge."
  3. ATTACH A CONSEQUENCE TO EVERY TERRITORIAL FACT.
     Never "Austria got Lombardy and Venetia" alone; always
     "...which made Austria the visible foreign ruler of northern
     Italy and fixed the anti-Austrian character of Italian
     nationalism."
  4. NAME THE INSTRUMENT, NOT "THE POWERS".
     Troppau Protocol, not "the Holy Alliance"; second Treaty of
     Paris, not "the Congress".
  5. USE ONE PAIRED CONTRAST AS THE ANALYTICAL CENTRE.
     Belgium against Poland in 1830 is the most efficient.
  6. SCALE THE CONTENT, NOT THE LENGTH.
     10 = principles and consequence. 15 = adds settlement,
     Concert, both records. 20 = adds the chain to 1848 and
     either historiography or a comparison.
  7. CLOSE WITH A VERDICT THAT CONCEDES SOMETHING SPECIFIC.
     Not "partly successful" but "substantial success by its own
     criteria; by the century's criteria, a settlement that
     mistook the absence of war for the presence of stability."
```

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## 1. The instrument chain, 1814-15

- **Chaumont, 1 March 1814** - Britain, Austria, Prussia, Russia; 20-year alliance; 150,000 men each; no separate peace. Converts a war coalition into a standing guarantee.
- **First Paris, 30 May 1814** - 1792 frontiers; Louis XVIII; no indemnity, no occupation; **Article 32** summons the congress.
- **Congress** - delegates from **late September 1814**; a formal opening in November is given in some accounts; runs to June 1815. Write "1814-15" and reserve exact days for instruments.
- **German Federal Act, 8 June 1815**; **Final Act (General Treaty), 9 June 1815** - a consolidating instrument, signed nine days before Waterloo.
- **Hundred Days** - Napoleon lands **1 March 1815**; outlawed by the Congress **13 March**; **Waterloo 18 June**.
- **Second Paris, 20 November 1815** - 1790 frontiers; **700 million francs**; occupation up to **five years**, ended **1818**.
- ⚠️ The chain, not any single document, is the settlement. Moderation in 1814 was a balance measure; severity in 1815 was a repricing; both served integration.

## 2. Negotiators, method and the congress's technical output

- **Metternich** (Austria; foreign minister from 1809, chancellor from 1821); **Castlereagh** (Britain, Foreign Secretary 1812-22); **Alexander I** (Russia, in person); **Hardenberg** (Prussia); **Talleyrand** (France); **Wellington** for Britain in Castlereagh's absence.
- Objectives differed: Austrian primacy and monarchical authority; British equilibrium without commitment; a Russian Poland; Prussian compensation; French re-entry. The settlement is a bargain among incompatible aims, not a shared ideology.
- Method: no plenary decision-making; concentric circles of powers; specialist committees; continuous informal negotiation. **The great powers decide and the rest of Europe is informed** - the habit that becomes the Concert.
- Talleyrand's lever: **legitimacy**, used to defend the King of Saxony; it made France a party to the secret alliance of 3 January 1815.
- Technical output that outlived the political settlement: **slave-trade declaration, 8 February 1815** (no enforcement machinery); **diplomatic precedence, 19 March 1815** (three classes; precedence by notification date); **free navigation of international rivers**; **Swiss perpetual neutrality, 20 November 1815**.

## 3. The four operating considerations

- **Legitimacy** - lawful dynastic right as the test of a valid claim. Restored dynasties, not the old constitutional order: no Holy Roman Empire, no Venetian or Genoese republic, no ecclesiastical states. Bound the powers where they could afford it (Saxony) and was silent where a people had no dynasty (Poland).
- **Balance of power** - a distribution among **five**, deliberately including the defeated power, which is what makes the equilibrium self-correcting. Silent about everything inside states.
- **Compensation** - territory as transferable power. Structurally incompatible with consent; the source of the settlement's grievances.
- **Containment** - never printed; the rule that selects among options the other three leave open. Explains the Netherlands, the Prussian Rhineland, guaranteed Swiss neutrality, enlarged Piedmont, Austria behind the Italian barrier.
- Collisions: **Saxony** - legitimacy plus balance beat compensation; **Poland** - compensation partly beats balance; **the nations** - no principle speaks for them at all.

## 4. The territorial settlement and its consequences

| Region | Decision | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| France | Bourbons restored; contained not destroyed | A great power readmitted in 1818, not a permanent revisionist |
| Netherlands | Belgium joined to Holland | A union across religion, language and economy; broken 1830 |
| Prussia west | Rhineland and Westphalia | The industrial base and the French frontier - Prussian primacy |
| Prussia east | Part of Saxony | The Saxon king survives; Prussia strengthened, not dominant |
| Germany | Confederation of **39**; Diet at Frankfurt; Austrian presidency | Unity postponed; the structure 1848 attacked and Bismarck replaced |
| Italy | Austria holds Lombardy and Venetia; influence elsewhere | Austrian rule contestable only by war; anti-Austrian nationalism |
| Piedmont | Enlarged by Genoa | A barrier state with a great port - the nucleus of unification |
| Poland | Congress Poland under the Tsar | A nation subordinated to bargaining; rising crushed 1831 |
| Switzerland | Perpetual neutrality guaranteed | The one 1815 arrangement still in force |

- ⚠️ Austria's German predominance was **constitutional** and contestable politically; her Italian predominance was **territorial** and contestable only by war. That difference produced two structurally different national movements.
- ⚠️ The settlement's compensations built **Prussia** and **Sardinia-Piedmont** - the two states that dismantled its central European arrangements.

## 5. The Polish-Saxon crisis

- One transaction, not two: a Russian Poland required Prussia's surrender of her Polish lands, and Saxony was the payment.
- Austria objected geographically (Russia on the Vistula; Galicia exposed); Britain objected systemically (an unbalanceable central-eastern bloc).
- **Secret defensive alliance, 3 January 1815** - Britain, Austria, France - designed to become known rather than used.
- Compromise: a **smaller Congress Poland** under the Tsar, with its own constitution and administration; **Austria keeps Galicia**; **Prussia takes part of Saxony**, Frederick Augustus I retained; Prussia compensated **westward**.
- ⚠️ Prussia lost the argument and, through the redirection, gained the territory that made her the leading German power.
- ⚠️ What the crisis proves: principles real but not sovereign; the balance enforced against the victors; combination rather than authority; the defeated power re-enters through the victors' quarrel; the cost falls on the weakest party present.

## 6. The three alliances and the Concert

- **Holy Alliance, 26 September 1815** - Russia, Austria, Prussia; Christian moral principles; **Britain declined**; Pope and Sultan excluded; limited practical importance; supplies a vocabulary of legitimation.
- **Quadruple Alliance, 20 November 1815** - Britain, Austria, Prussia, Russia; extends Chaumont; **Article VI** provides periodic meetings, without defining their competence.
- **Quintuple Alliance, 1818** - France added at Aix-la-Chapelle; the object widens from containing France to maintaining the European order - and the alliance loses its adversary.
- **Concert of Europe** - not an instrument: the practice of great-power consultation to preserve the status quo; no membership rule, no obligation; can act where the treaties are silent; outlives the congress rhythm.
- Diagnostic: **what does it oblige anyone to do?** Holy Alliance - nothing enforceable. Quadruple Alliance - armed support and meetings. Concert - nothing, because it is a practice.

## 7. The congress system, 1818-1822

- **Aix-la-Chapelle 1818** - occupation ended; indemnity settled; **France admitted**. The last congress whose subject lay inside the alliance's agreed competence.
- **Troppau 1820** - **Protocol of 19 November 1820**; Austria, Prussia, Russia; a state altered by revolution leaves the alliance and may be restored by force. **Britain and France refuse**; both attend as observers.
- **Laibach 1821** - **Austrian intervention in Naples and Piedmont** authorised and executed; Britain observes only.
- **Verona, October-December 1822** - **French intervention in Spain** authorised; **Wellington withdraws on Canning's instructions**; the last major effective congress.
- ⚠️ The system exhausted interstate questions and moved to intrastate ones on which its members held incompatible convictions. Characteristic form: **collectively authorised, nationally executed**.

## 8. The intervention quarrel

- **Troppau doctrine** - three claims: membership (revolution removes a state from the alliance), restoration (force may bring it back), collectivity (the action is European).
- **British State Paper, 5 May 1820** - the alliance was made against the military domination of Europe by France; it is not a union for the government of the world or the superintendence of other states' internal affairs. **It precedes Troppau by six months.**
- Britain refused a **standing obligation**, not intervention as such - which is why she could act for Greece in 1827 without inconsistency.
- ⚠️ **The structural point:** each power's position on intervention abroad followed from the kind of state it was at home - multinational dynastic empire, autocracy, parliamentary monarchy. No diplomacy could have resolved it.

## 9. Intervention in practice

- **Spain, January 1820** (Riego; the Constitution of 1812 restored); **Naples, July 1820** (Ferdinand I of the Two Sicilies); **Portugal, August 1820**; **Piedmont, March 1821** - constitutionalist movements demanding written constitutions, commonly on the Spanish 1812 model, led by officers, professionals and sections of the nobility.
- **Austria** suppressed Naples and Piedmont in 1821 under Laibach; **France** intervened in Spain in 1823 under Verona - the **Hundred Thousand Sons of Saint Louis** - restoring Ferdinand VII's absolute authority.
- ⚠️ Moderate constitutionalism was more dangerous to the eastern powers than republicanism, because it could not be dismissed as criminal fanaticism.
- ⚠️ The verdict: the interventions succeeded completely and destroyed the system that carried them out.
- ⚠️ In Italy they converted Austrian territorial predominance into an acknowledged right of policing - the origin of anti-Austrian Italian nationalism. In Spain they made the defeated power of 1815 the settlement's enforcement agent by 1823.

## 10. The two cases where the principle broke

- **Spanish America** - Britain **blocked the extension of intervention at Verona**; the **Monroe Doctrine of December 1823** added an independent American warning. Two independent acts, one effect. ❌ Britain did not enforce the doctrine, and the doctrine did not cause the British position.
- **Greece, 1821-1832** - **1827**: Britain, France and Russia agree on intervention; **Navarino, 20 October 1827**; **Treaty of Adrianople, 1829**; **London Protocol, 3 February 1830** recognises full independence; **Treaty of Constantinople, 1832** fixes borders; **Otto of Bavaria** as king. **Canning** moved Britain away from the strict non-intervention urged by Metternich's Austria.
- ⚠️ Four reasons for the reversal: the Sultan stood outside the Christian monarchical club; the Eastern question overrode the doctrine; joint action bounded Russian gains; European public opinion was engaged - its first appearance as a force in this topic.
- ⚠️ **Legitimacy bound the powers inside the European circle and lapsed at its edge.** Greece ends the Concert as an ideology and demonstrates it working as a method.

## 11. Britain's position

- Four structural sources: **constitution** (no ministry can bind Parliament, and the doctrine condemned Britain's own constitutional history); **geography** (an island needs equilibrium, not a directorate); **commerce** (markets are indifferent to a customer's government); **empire** (the field of expansion lies outside Europe).
- **Castlereagh 1812-22** dissented from within; **Canning from 1822** acknowledged the separation. The substance was identical; only its management changed.
- Positive contributions: the moderate peace with France; Article VI and the consultative machinery; the **Belgian settlement of 1830**; the fleet at Navarino.
- Effect on the system: the Concert could not become a monarchical directorate - and could not become anything else either. Every challenger acquired a potential patron.

## 12. The order inside the states

- **Carlsbad Decrees, 20 September 1819**, after the assassination of **August von Kotzebue** on **23 March 1819** by the student **Karl Sand**: **press censorship**; **university curators**; **Burschenschaften dissolved**; **Central Investigation Commission at Mainz**.
- **Vienna Final Act of the German Confederation, 15 May 1820** - completes the federal constitution; permits **federal intervention against revolutionary activity**.
- > 🔑 Two Final Acts: the **Congress's**, 9 June 1815, and the **Confederation's**, 15 May 1820.
- ⚠️ The target was institutional - press, university, student association - not a conspiracy or a class. A state that arrests conspirators faces a plot; a state that places a curator in every university faces a milieu.
- ⚠️ Carlsbad (1819) precedes Troppau (1820): the German practice was generalised into a European doctrine.
- ⚠️ **The Age of Metternich** names a system with Austria at its centre, not a personal dominion; Britain and Russia pursued their own priorities.
- ⚠️ **The central paradox:** by denying nationalism a political outlet, the system pushed it into literature, scholarship and history, where it could not be policed and where it grew.

## 13. The three tests

- **1820-21** - constitutional; narrow; suppressed easily; the system split doing it.
- **1830-31** - constitutional **and national**; the system **chose**: the July Revolution in France accepted (intervention meant war with a great power); **Belgian independence accepted**, and neutrality **guaranteed by the Treaty of London, 19 April 1839**; the **Polish rising crushed by Russia** with no intervention; Italy and Germany suppressed.
- **1848** - constitutional, national **and social**; continental and simultaneous; **Metternich fell in March 1848** and fled; the revolutions nevertheless failed.
- Four reasons 1848 failed: incompatible demands inside the coalition; national claims mutually destructive in multinational regions; dynastic armies intact and obedient; liberal nationalism had no instrument of force.
- ⚠️ **Belgium against Poland** is the decisive pair: identical in kind, opposite in outcome, explained by capability rather than principle.
- ⚠️ The trend: two expeditions, then two concessions plus an army, then survival only through the challengers' own collapse.

## 14. The peace claim

- **Defensible form:** no general war among the European great powers between **1815 and the Crimean War of 1853-56**; and no continent-wide coalition war comparable to the Napoleonic wars before 1914.
- **Standard caution:** the Concert prevented a **general war**, not intervention or regional war.
- Four contributions: the specific cause of the previous wars removed; an equilibrium with no obvious revisionist; a consultative practice that resolved Belgium 1830 and Greece 1827-32; a shared dynastic fear that war produces revolution.
- ❌ The over-claim to refuse: the settlement created **no mechanism** - no obligation, procedure, sanction or organ.
- Three competing explanations to concede: exhaustion; the absence of a hegemonic challenger; preoccupation with industrialisation and extra-European expansion.
- ⚠️ **The Concert did not end because a war broke out; a war broke out because the Concert had ended.**

## 15. Historiography and the counterfactual

- **Reactionary reading** - dominant for much of the early twentieth century: restoration of monarchical legitimacy, suppression of national and liberal forces, dynasties and geopolitics over peoples. **Nicolson, *The Congress of Vienna* (1946)** - diplomatic narrative; pragmatic but conservative negotiators.
- **Equilibrium reading** - **Webster** rehabilitates Castlereagh and British diplomacy, stressing pragmatism and consensus-building; **Kissinger, *A World Restored* (1957)** presents a sophisticated equilibrium in which legitimacy is balanced against political reality and no single grievance can destabilise the whole.
- **Transformation thesis** - **Schroeder, *The Transformation of European Politics 1763-1848* (1994)**: the first modern attempt at a consensual, collectively managed order resting on mutual restraint and consultation.
- **The standing criticism**: the settlement contained rather than resolved national demands in Italy, Germany and Poland.
- Four separable questions: what kind of thing it was; balance as principle or pretext; what Concert diplomacy was; the legacy. Each has its own synthesis, produced by a different move.
- ⚠️ **Position recommended:** Vienna transformed the **practice** of diplomacy, not its **structure**.
- Counterfactuals: a punitive France - worse; national states - unavailable; **constitutional guarantees - the real missed alternative, blocked by Austria's own composition**; institutionalisation - incompatible with indivisible sovereignty.
- ⚠️ The counterfactual analysis **exonerates the design and indicts the administration**.

## 16. Legacy and the non-equivalence

- Inherited: **conference diplomacy**; the **joint guarantee** (Switzerland 1815, Greece 1830, Belgium 1839); the **consolidated general settlement**; **technical international law**.
- Never present: charter, defined membership, permanent organ, obligation, sanction, decision procedure, prohibition on force, non-European member, sovereign equality, and a purpose compatible with self-determination.
- ⚠️ The last two make the difference **categorical**: the Concert is the arrangement later organisations were designed to replace.
- ⚠️ **The formula:** a method, not an institution - and collective security is an institution.
- 📰 One current linkage: the 2026 debate on a new concert of major powers; advocates argue from the 1815 analogy, critics that its foundations cannot be recreated. It supplies no historical evidence.

## 17. Rapid recall - the numbers and names this session asserts

| Item | Value |
|---|---|
| Chaumont, force pledged by each power | 150,000 men |
| Chaumont, duration of the alliance | 20 years |
| First Paris frontiers | Those of 1792 |
| Second Paris frontiers | Those of 1790 |
| Second Paris indemnity | 700 million francs |
| Second Paris occupation | Up to five years; ended 1818 |
| German Confederation | 39 states |
| Diplomatic classes, 1815 regulation | Three |
| Congresses of the system | Four - 1818, 1820, 1821, 1822 |
| Great powers in the Concert | Four from 1815; five from 1818 |
| The long interval without general war | 1815 to 1853-56 |
| Named negotiators | Metternich, Castlereagh, Alexander I, Hardenberg, Talleyrand, Wellington |
| Later named statesmen | Canning; Frederick Augustus I of Saxony; Ferdinand I of the Two Sicilies; Ferdinand VII of Spain; Louis XVIII; Otto of Bavaria; Rafael del Riego; August von Kotzebue; Karl Sand |
| Named historians | Nicolson (1946); Webster; Kissinger (1957); Schroeder (1994) |

## 18. Prohibited formulations

- ❌ The Holy Alliance equated with the Concert of Europe.
- ❌ The indemnity, occupation or periodic meetings attributed to the Congress of Vienna.
- ❌ The two Final Acts - 9 June 1815 and 15 May 1820 - confused.
- ❌ "Vienna created nation-states by popular will."
- ❌ Any number of German Confederation states other than 39.
- ❌ "The settlement drew all borders permanently."
- ❌ "The Concert prevented all conflict" or "collapsed immediately".
- ❌ "Vienna gave Europe a hundred years of peace."
- ❌ "Vienna succeeded where Versailles failed" as a bare claim.
- ❌ "Canning caused the split."
- ❌ "Britain was the liberal power."
- ❌ Any verbatim quotation attributed to Metternich, Castlereagh, Canning, Talleyrand or Alexander I.
- ❌ Any population, area, revenue, troop or casualty figure beyond the four numbers listed in section 17.
- ❌ Any claim that war was imminent or that any power mobilised during the Polish-Saxon crisis.
- ❌ Narration of Latin American independence, Italian or German unification, the Frankfurt assembly, or the causes and course of the Crimean War.

Supplied the six-row analytical timeline; the four-row central debate matrix on the nature of Vienna, the balance of power, Concert diplomacy and legacy, with the recommended synthesis for each; the statement that the settlement did not respect national aspirations in Italy, Germany, Poland and elsewhere and is best read as a successful short-term peace system with a long-term legitimacy deficit; the five-row table on what held the system together, including shared fear of renewed French expansion, great-power consultation, Austrian leadership in central Europe, the repression tools of censorship, university control and investigation, and the British limit on wider intervention in Spanish America at Verona; the account of why the system weakened, including the obsolescence of the Concert's original form after 1830, 1848 and the later unifications, the exposure of disagreement over intervention at Verona, and the structural point that a dynastic settlement could not contain forces released by the French Revolution; the peace-against-legitimacy block; the world-history significance section with the longer arc and the note that Vienna is an early example of multilateral diplomacy though not of modern democracy; the must-know facts, the trap against writing that the Concert collapsed immediately, and the three advanced misconception corrections including the Holy and Quadruple Alliance distinction; and the Mains-answer framework with its probable questions.

|---|---|---|
| Instrument chain and negotiators | Treaty of Chaumont, 1 March 1814, with its four parties, twenty-year term and 150,000-man pledge; first Treaty of Paris, 30 May 1814, with the 1792 frontiers, the Bourbon restoration and Article 32 summoning the congress; the assembly of delegates from late September 1814 and the competing November 1814 opening convention; the Final Act of 9 June 1815 as a consolidating general treaty; the second Treaty of Paris of 20 November 1815 with the 1790 frontiers, 700 million francs and the five-year occupation maximum ended early in 1818; the five principal negotiators and Wellington's substitution | Lessons 1, 2 and 6; the master chronology; the instruments table; register notes 1 and 2 |
| The Poland-Saxony crisis | The linkage of the two demands; the Austrian and British objections; Talleyrand's use of legitimacy to defend the King of Saxony; the secret defensive treaty of 3 January 1815 among Britain, Austria and France and its purpose of becoming known; the compromise creating a smaller Congress Poland under the Tsar with its own constitution and administration, Austria retaining Galicia, Prussia receiving part of Saxony with Frederick Augustus I retained, and Prussian compensation redirected into Germany | Lesson 5; cumulative check B2; register notes 5 |
| Alliances and the congress system | The Holy Alliance of 26 September 1815 with its three members, its Christian-principles content, Britain's non-adherence through the Prince Regent's refusal and the exclusion of the Pope and the Ottoman Sultan; the Quadruple Alliance of 20 November 1815 with its four members and Article VI providing periodic meetings; the Quintuple Alliance formed by the admission of France at Aix-la-Chapelle in 1818; the four congresses of 1818, 1820, 1821 and 1822; the Troppau Protocol of 19 November 1820 signed by three powers and refused by Britain and France; Wellington's withdrawal from Verona on Canning's instructions; Castlereagh's tenure of 1812-22 and his death in August 1822; Metternich's foreign ministry from 1809 and chancellorship from 1821 | Lessons 7, 8, 9, 12 and 13; the alliance and congress tables; register notes 6, 7, 8 and 11 |
| The British position | The State Paper of 5 May 1820 and its formulation that the alliance was made against the military domination of Europe by France and was not a union for the government of the world or for the superintendence of other states' internal affairs | Lessons 9 and 12; register notes 8 and 11; Mains Q4 |
| Cases at the system's edge | French intervention in Spain in 1823 and the identity of the expedition; Austrian intervention in Naples and Piedmont in 1821; the Monroe Doctrine of December 1823; the Greek sequence - tripartite agreement in 1827, Navarino on 20 October 1827, the Treaty of Adrianople of 1829, the London Protocol of 3 February 1830 recognising full independence under great-power guarantee, and the Treaty of Constantinople of 1832 with Otto of Bavaria as king; Canning's movement of Britain away from strict non-intervention | Lessons 10 and 11; register notes 9 and 10 |
| The domestic order | The murder of Kotzebue in March 1819; the Carlsbad Decrees of 20 September 1819 with press censorship, university curators, the dissolution of the Burschenschaften and the Central Investigation Commission at Mainz; the German Confederation's Vienna Final Act of 15 May 1820 completing the federal constitution and permitting federal intervention; Metternich's flight in March 1848 | Lesson 13; Lesson 14; register notes 12 and 13 |
| Secondary instruments and later guarantees | The German Federal Act of 8 June 1815 with the 39 states, the Frankfurt Diet and the Austrian presidency; the slave-trade declaration of 8 February 1815 and its lack of enforcement machinery; the diplomatic-precedence regulation of 19 March 1815 with its three classes; free navigation of international rivers; Swiss perpetual neutrality of 20 November 1815; the Treaty of London of 19 April 1839 guaranteeing Belgian neutrality | Lessons 2, 4, 14 and 17; the instruments table; register notes 2, 4, 13 and 16 |
| The peace claim and its terminus | The absence of a general European war between 1815 and the Crimean War of 1853-56, and the looser claim extending to 1914 with its qualifications | Lesson 15; register notes 14 |
| Historiographical positions | Nicolson's *The Congress of Vienna* (1946) as diplomatic narrative treating the negotiators as pragmatic but conservative; Webster's rehabilitation of Castlereagh and of the order's pragmatism and flexibility; Kissinger's *A World Restored* (1957) and the equilibrium reading; Schroeder's *The Transformation of European Politics 1763-1848* (1994) and the transformation thesis; and the standing criticism that the settlement contained rather than resolved national demands | Lesson 16; register notes 15 |
| The single current linkage | A policy debate reported through 2026 on whether an informal concert of today's major powers could stabilise the international order, with advocates arguing from the post-1815 analogy and critics arguing that the contemporary system has more actors, deeper ideological divisions, greater interdependence and transnational problems a small directorate cannot address, and that the original Concert's restricted membership, shared cultural background and executive discretion cannot be recreated | Lesson 17 section 6 only - the single current linkage in this session, carrying no historical fact |
| Closing confirmation of remaining standard dates | Napoleon's first abdication on 6 April 1814 and his exile to Elba; his escape on 26 February 1815 and landing in France on 1 March 1815, opening the Hundred Days; Waterloo on 18 June 1815; the Spanish revolution of January 1820 led by Rafael del Riego, restoring the Constitution of 1812; the Neapolitan revolution of July 1820 against Ferdinand I of the Two Sicilies; the Portuguese revolution of August 1820; the Piedmontese revolution of March 1821; and the assassination of August von Kotzebue on 23 March 1819 by the student Karl Sand | Lessons 1, 8, 10 and 13; the master chronology; register notes 1, 9, 12 and 17. Run as the closing stage of the same bounded material-fact pass, so that no standard examinable date in this topic is either omitted or asserted without verification |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the material-fact pass was authorised expressly so that the standard examinable names, dates and instruments of this topic would be taught rather than omitted, and its scope was confined to fixing those items. It supplies no interpretation. Every analytical claim in this session is either drawn from the two canonical owners or is marked as an inference and argued from evidence stated in the lesson.

## Endpoints identified but not retrieved in this pass

Nothing in the teaching depends on text that was not read. The following were identified and **not** fetched, in accordance with the bounded research rule: the full text of the Final Act of the Congress of Vienna and of its annexed acts; the texts of the Treaty of Chaumont, the two Treaties of Paris, the German Federal Act, the Holy Alliance, the Quadruple Alliance, the Troppau Protocol, the Carlsbad Decrees, the Vienna Final Act of 1820, the London Protocol of 1830, the Treaty of Constantinople of 1832 and the Treaty of London of 1839; the full text of Castlereagh's State Paper; the published works of Nicolson, Webster, Kissinger and Schroeder; any monograph or article on the congress, the Concert, the Eastern question or the revolutions of 1820, 1830 and 1848; any statistical series for population, revenue, army size or casualties in this period; and the policy commentary underlying the single current linkage beyond its search return. In every case the two canonical owners, the subject's chronology and revision chart, or the bounded material-fact pass supplied the teaching content, and the current item is reported no further than its search return supported.

## Open uncertainties

The following remain genuinely open and are **not** asserted anywhere.

1. The exact date on which the congress may be said to have opened, the two conventions being stated openly in Lesson 1 rather than silently resolved.
2. The internal deliberations of the committees, the number of delegations present, and the extent to which the smaller states influenced rather than merely registered outcomes.
3. The private convictions of the eastern monarchs regarding the Holy Alliance's religious formulation, beyond the general acceptance that Alexander I's was sincere.
4. Whether Russia and Prussia would in fact have gone to war had the Polish-Saxon compromise failed.
5. The share of the Saxon territory transferred to Prussia and any population or area measure of any transfer.
6. The practical reach and effectiveness of the Carlsbad machinery, the volume of its investigations and the number of persons affected.
7. The relative weight of the four explanations for the powers' reversal in the Greek case.
8. The relative weight of the settlement, post-war exhaustion, the absence of a hegemonic challenger and extra-European preoccupation in producing the long interval without a general war.
9. Whether Castlereagh's method, had he lived, could have kept Britain formally inside the congress framework without accepting the interventionist doctrine.
10. Whether a generalised constitutional settlement in 1815 would have stabilised the order or accelerated the national challenge.
11. Whether the congress system could have survived had the revolutions of 1820 not occurred.
12. The degree to which the restraint described by the transformation thesis was a shared understanding rather than a convergence of separate calculations.

## Evidence and limitation controls

- No content from the excluded derived or generated package tree was read, cited or used, in accordance with the permanent live-session source exclusion; no learner-v2 artifact was used either, including this subject's own v2 learning sessions, solved workbooks and the complete-topic-package file that duplicates this topic. All were identified during source discovery and deliberately not opened.
- No workbook operation of any kind was performed: no MCQ corpus was constructed, copied, relocated or migrated, and no solved-PYQ workbook was created.
- No quotation is attributed to Metternich, Castlereagh, Canning, Talleyrand, Alexander I, Hardenberg, Wellington or any other statesman of the period, because no such quotation is held in this evidence base. The only quoted matter is the verbatim syllabus clause and the five adjacent verified questions.
- No population, territorial-area, revenue, army-size, casualty or expenditure figure appears for any transfer, intervention, rising or war in this period. The only numerical quantities asserted anywhere are the twenty-year term and 150,000-man pledge of Chaumont, the 700 million francs and five-year occupation maximum of the second Treaty of Paris, the 39 states of the German Confederation and the three classes of the 1815 diplomatic regulation.
- No claim is made that any power mobilised or that war was imminent during the Polish-Saxon crisis; what is recorded is the secret alliance of 3 January 1815, its intended publicity and the compromise that followed.
- The Latin American independence movements, the political narrative of Italian and German unification including the Frankfurt assembly, the drivers and partition of New Imperialism, the causes and course of the Crimean War, the later Eastern question and the doctrines of liberalism, conservatism and nationalism as doctrines are all routed to their owners in the opening scope note, in the relevant lessons and in the boundary table of Lesson 18, and none of their material is reproduced here.
- Named historiographical positions are asserted only to the extent the single bounded material-fact pass supports them - author, work, date where held, and the position taken. No historian is quoted, no position is elaborated beyond that return, and the limitation is stated in Lesson 16.
- Objective-question neutrality does not arise, because this topic owns no objective question in any audited span; the five adjacent Mains questions are displayed with their exact wording, route and boundary note, and no solved model answer for any of them appears, as the governing boundary requires.
- The single current linkage is confined to Lesson 17 section 6 and to the table above. It carries no historical fact, and no teaching claim anywhere in this session depends on a live source. The other seventeen pre-teach checklists record truthfully that no current affairs were sought.
